# Biomarker and pathogenic study of immune-mediated neuropathies
**Authors:** Ivan Kmezic
**DOI:** [10.69622/28457924.v1](https://doi.org/10.69622/28457924.v1)

## Content

<p dir="ltr">There is a lack of reliable biomarkers for diagnosis, prognosis assessment, and monitoring of treatment response in immune-mediated neuropathies. The diagnosis is predominantly based on clinical assessment and electrophysiological findings. Although immune-mediated neuropathies are treatable, existing therapies are nonspecific and fail to provide adequate benefit for a significant number of patients.</p><p dir="ltr">In Study I, we investigated the diagnostic and prognostic value of axonal injury biomarkers, neurofilament light (NfL) chain and total tau (T-tau), in plasma and CSF of patients with Guillain-Barre syndrome (GBS), chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyradiculoneuropathy, multifocal motor neuropathy (MMN), and paraproteinemic demyelinating neuropathy (PDN). In GBS, CIDP, and PDN, levels of NfL were elevated in both plasma and CSF, whereas in patients with MMN the levels of NfL were elevated only in plasma. NfL in plasma and CSF was associated with prognosis in GBS and may also help distinguish MMN from amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS). Additionally, T-tau in plasma emerged as a novel diagnostic biomarker for both acute and chronic immune-mediated neuropathies.</p><p dir="ltr">In Study II, we analysed over 1100 biomarkers in plasma and CSF using proximity extension assay (PEA) technology in two independent cohorts (discovery and replication) of patients with GBS and CIDP. We identified several candidate biomarkers with potential diagnostic value for GBS and CIDP. However, the most consistent finding was upregulation of interleukin-8 (IL8) in CSF. To validate this finding, we further analysed levels of IL8 using a chemiluminescence assay in a third (validation) cohort, which included patients with GBS, CIDP, PDN, MMN, healthy individuals, and other disease controls. IL8 in CSF is validated as a diagnostic biomarker for GBS and CIDP, and shows potential as a prognostic biomarker for both short- and long-term outcomes in GBS.</p><p dir="ltr">Beta-trace protein (BTP) is the second most abundant protein in CSF after albumin, yet it has not previously been studied in patients with immune-mediated neuropathies. In Study III, we investigated the levels of BTP in CSF and plasma of patients with GBS, CIDP, disease controls, and healthy controls. In CIDP, levels of BTP were elevated in both CSF and plasma, while in GBS only in CSF. Notably, in patients with GBS and CIDP, levels of BTP in CSF were elevated regardless of the Q.Alb, suggesting that BTP may serve as a more informative diagnostic biomarker than albumin. Furthermore, the levels of BTP in CSF may have predictive value for treatment response in CIDP.</p><p dir="ltr">Numerous attempts to develop new therapies for GBS other than intravenous immunoglobulin and plasma exchange have been unsuccessful. Previous studies have shown that blocking the tyrosine kinase receptor platelet-derived growth factor receptor-alpha (PDGFRa) with imatinib, a small tyrosine kinase inhibitor, regulate the function of the brain-blood barrier. In Study IV, we found that imatinib reduced the severity of experimental autoimmune neuritis (EAN), an animal model of GBS, by improving blood-nerve barrier (BNB) integrity. This conclusion was supported by clinical scoring, functional electrophysiological testing, and histological assessment of the BNB. These findings demonstrate the efficacy of imatinib in treating EAN and provide evidence for its potential clinical application in patients with GBS.</p><p dir="ltr">In summary, this thesis identifies candidate biomarkers for immune-mediated neuropathies and proposes a novel therapeutic strategy for GBS.</p><h3>List of scientific papers</h3><p dir="ltr">I. <b>Ivan Kmezic</b>, Kristin Samuelsson, Anja Finn, Zane Upate, Kaj Blennow, Henrik Zetterberg, Rayomand Press. Neurofilament light chain and total tau in the differential diagnosis and prognostic evaluation of acute and chronic inflammatory polyneuropathies. Eur J Neurol. 2022 Sep;29(9):2810-2822. <a href="https://doi.org/10.1111/ene.15428">https://doi.org/10.1111/ene.15428</a></p><p dir="ltr">II. <b>Ivan Kmezic</b>, Rasmus Gustafsson, Katharina Fink, Anders Svenningsson, Kristin Samuelsson, Caroline Ingre, Tomas Olsson, Magnus Hansson, Ingrid Kockum, Milena Zeitelhofer Adzemovic, Rayomand Press. Validation of elevated levels of interleukin-8 in the cerebrospinal fluid, and discovery of new biomarkers in patients with GBS and CIDP using a proximity extension assay. Front Immunol. 2023 Nov 23:14:1241199. <a href="https://doi.org/10.3389/fimmu.2023.1241199" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank">https://doi.org/10.3389/fimmu.2023.1241199</a></p><p dir="ltr">III. <b>Ivan Kmezic</b>, Rasmus Gustafsson, Magnus Hansson, Rayomand Press. Beta-trace protein in chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyradiculoneuropathy and Guillain-Barré syndrome clinical utilization and a new insight into pathophysiological mechanisms. Online ahead of print. J Neurol Sci. 2025 Feb 28:472:123439. <a href="https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jns.2025.123439" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank">https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jns.2025.123439</a></p><p dir="ltr">IV. <b>Ivan Kmezic</b>, Charles Dorris, Humberto Skott, Manuel Arauji, Rayomand Press, Manuel Zeitelhofer, Milena Zeitelhofer Adzemovic. Imatinib ameliorates experimental autoimmune neuritis, an animal model of Guillain-Barre syndrome. [Manuscript]</p>

Popular science summary of the thesis  
Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS), chronic inflammatory demyelinating 
polyradiculoneuropathy (CIDP), 
and other 
related immune -mediated 
neuropathies, 
including multifocal motor neuropathy 
(MMN), autoimmune 
nodopathy and paraproteinemic demyelinating neuropathy (PDN) , are rare 
neurological autoimmune disorders. While 
they are not common, they can be 
potentially debilitating, leading to severe muscle weakness, loss of mobility, and, 
in some cases, difficulty breathing due to respiratory failure. Early diagnosis is 
crucial because immune-mediated conditions 
are treatable, and also since GBS 
and certain types of autoimmune 
nodopathy can be life-threatening if not treated 
promptly. The 
diagnosis of immune- mediated neuropathies relies primarily on 
patient history 
and physical examination, with support from tests such as blood 
work, cerebrospinal fluid analysis, and electrophysiological assessments of nerve 
function. However, no test is entirely accurate, and it’s not uncommon to either 
miss the diagnosis, thereby delaying treatment, or to make a misdiagnos is, 
potentially 
leading to unnecessary and risky immunosuppressive treatments in 
patients who 
do not actually have
 an immune-mediated neuropathy. Even when 
the diagnosis 
is correct, it can be difficult to assess how effective the treatment 
is and whether the disease is still active. In some cases, patients receive excessive 
treatment, continuing immunotherapy even 
when it should be discontinued. With 
the exception of autoimmune nodopathy and promising results from B -cell-
depleting therapies, little progress has 
been made in treating these conditions 
since the early 1990s. 
Driven by the challenges faced in 
everyday clinical practice caring for patients 
with immune-mediated neuropathies, this thesis 
explored a range of potential 
biomarkers 
with the aim 
of improving the diagnosis, predict ing outcomes, 
assessing treatment responses, and gaining 
insight into the underlying disease 
mechanisms of these conditions. Additionally,  our research examined the 
therapeutic potential of imatinib, 
a drug 
that targets a specific protein in the 
blood-nerve barrier, 
using an animal model of GBS. 
When nerve cells are damaged 
by disease, they break down and release various 
components into body fluids, which 
can then be measured.  Key structural 
components of nerve cells, 
such as neurofilaments and tau, were the focus of our 
initial study. We found that levels of neurofilament light (NfL) protein were 
elevated in both the 
blood and spinal fluid of patients with GBS, CIDP, and PDN. 


Interestingly, we found that levels of NfL could help predict recovery in GBS 
patients and differentiate between the treatable disease MMN and the fatal  
amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS). We 
also observed elevated levels of tau in the 
blood of all our patients with different immune-mediated neuropathies compared 
to both healthy individuals 
and disease controls, highlighting tau as a promising 
biomarker for diagnosing these conditions. 
Technologies for identifying new biomarkers 
— measurable indicators of a disease 
or 
condition — are advancing rapidly. Today, it is possible to analyse hundreds of 
different biomarkers from just 
a few microliters of fluid, such as blood. In our 
second study, we employed Olink -based proteomics, which enabled us to 
examine over 1,100 biomarkers  per 
sample. Our analysis revealed several 
promising biomarkers in both blood 
and spinal fluid of patients with GBS and CIDP 
which warrant further investigation. 
The most consistent finding, however, was 
significantly elevated levels of 
interleukin-8 (IL8), a protein involved in specific 
immune responses, in 
the spinal 
fluid of patients with GBS compared to those with 
CIDP. This finding 
may help differentiate between these two immune-mediated 
neuropathies in the 
acute phase 
of disease . This distinction is crucial as it 
influences treatment decisions. Additionally, 
we found that levels of IL8 might also 
help predict both short - and long-term outcomes for GBS patients. During the 
acute phase of the 
disease, the levels of IL8 in spinal fluid may help identify 
patients with GBS at risk of further deterioration in the coming days and provide 
insight into their potential for 
recovery after six months. Beyond its diagnostic and 
prognostic value, this discovery offers a deeper understanding of the underlying 
mechanisms of GBS and CIDP and opens new avenues for treatment trials. IL8 
inhibitors — drugs that 
specifically target IL8 — are already being tested in cancer 
patients. If 
our results are corroborated by other research groups, exploring the 
use of IL8 inhibitors in patients with GBS and CIDP may be worth considering. 
Ever since Guillain-Barré syndrome 
was described for the first time over 100 years 
ago, elevated levels of 
albumin, a protein, in the spinal fluid have been considered 
a hallmark of GBS and several other immune -mediated neuropathies. When 
present, it supports the diagnosis. 
However, elevated levels of albumin in spinal 
fluid are not always observed in all patients with immune-mediated neuropathies, 
nor are they
 specific to 
these conditions. To find a more reliable marker, we turned 
to another protein in 
the spinal fluid, beta-trace protein (BTP), the second most 
abundant protein after albumin. 
In our third study, we discovered that patients 
with GBS and CIDP who had normal levels of albumin in spinal fluid  exhibited 


 
 
elevated levels of BTP instead. This suggests that BTP may be a more informative 
diagnostic biomarker than albumin. Moreover, we found that the levels of BTP in 
spinal fluid could also predict how well patients with CIDP would respond to 
treatment, offering a more precise and personalized approach to diagnosing and 
managing CIDP. 
In the search for new 
treatments which are both effective and safe, potential drugs 
are often first tested on 
laboratory animals. In our fourth study, we investigated 
the therapeutic potential of imatinib in an animal model of GBS. Imatinib is a drug 
that specifically targets and restores the integrity of the blood-nerve barrier, a 
protective shield between the 
blood and the nervous system, which is 
compromised in GBS. Our results were promising. Not only did imatinib improve 
the neurological function of 
laboratory animals, but it also preserved the integrity 
and function of the blood-nerve 
barrier, as demonstrated by objective methods 
including molecular biology 
techniques, imaging 
studies, and electrophysiological 
nerve function tests. This study could set the stage for future clinical trial of 
imatinib in patients with GBS, offering hope for a new treatment option for this 
challenging condition. 
 
  


Populärvetenskaplig sammanfattning [in Swedish] 
Guillain-Barré syndrom (GBS), kronisk inflammatorisk demyeliniserande 
polyradikuloneuropati ( eng. chronic 
inflammatory demyelinating 
polyradiculoneuropathy, 
CIDP) och andra immunmedierade neuropatier, inklusive 
multifokal motorisk neuropati (MMN), paraprotein -relaterad demyeliniserande 
neuropati (PDN) och autoimmun nodopati, 
är sällsynta neurologiska autoimmuna 
sjukdomar. Dessa sjukdomar kan orsaka en omfattande funktionsnedsättning och 
påverka livskvalité negativt till följd av muskelsvaghet i armar och ben, gång- och 
balanssvårigheter, smärta och i vissa fall andningssvårigheter till följd av påverkan 
på andningsmuskulaturen. Tidig diagnos 
är avgörande , eftersom tillstånden är 
behandlingsbara, och kan i 
vissa akuta fall vara livshotande om de inte behandlas 
i tid. Trots alla framsteg inom medicin, baseras diagnosen fortfarande främst på 
patientens symtomberättelse och klinisk undersökning, med visst stöd av 
blodprover, analys av ryggvätska och nervfunktionsundersökning. Inget test är 
dock helt tillförlitligt. Det är inte ovanligt att diagnosen missas, vilket kan fördröja 
behandling, eller att fel 
diagnos ställs, vilket i sin tur kan leda till onödig och 
potentiellt riskfylld immunsuppressiv behandling.  Därtill kan det vara svårt att 
bedöma 
hur väl behandlingen fungerar, även om diagnosen är korrekt. Med 
undantag för behandling av autoimmun nodopati och lovande resultat från B-
cellshämmande terapi har det gjorts få framsteg i behandlingen av dessa tillstånd 
sedan början av 1990-talet. 
Motiverat av de 
utmaningar som vi 
möter i den kliniska vardagen syftar denna 
avhandling till att förbättra 
diagnostiken av autoimmuna neuropatier, förutsäga 
sjukdomsförlopp, utvärdera behandlingssvar och undersöka de underliggande 
sjukdomsmekanismerna. Dessutom undersöker vår forskning den terapeutiska 
potentialen av
 imatinib, ett läkemedel som riktar sig mot ett specifikt protein i 
blod
-nervbarriären, i en djurmodell av GBS. 
När nervceller skadas 
av en 
sjuklig process, bryts de ne d och cellernas delar  
frisätts i olika kroppsvätskor, till 
exempel blod eller ryggvätska. Viktiga strukturella 
komponenter i nervceller, såsom neurofilament och tau, var i fokus för vårt första 
forskningsprojekt. Vi fann att 
nivåerna av neurofilament är förhöjda i både blod och 
ryggvätska hos patienter med GBS, CIDP och PDN. Särskilt intressant är att 
nivåerna av neurofilament kan hjälpa till att förutsäga återhämtningsförmågan hos 
patienter med GBS, samt 
skilja mellan behandlingsbar MMN och den dödliga 
amyotrofiska lateralsklerosen (ALS). Vi upptäckte även förhöjda nivåer av tau i 


 
 
blodet hos samtliga patienter med autoimmuna neuropatier, jämfört med både  
friska individer och sjukdomskontroller, vilket 
gör tau till en lovande diagnostisk 
biomarkör för autoimmuna neuropatier.  
Teknologier för 
att identifiera nya biomarkörer – mätbara indikatorer på en 
sjukdom eller 
ett tillstånd – utvecklas snabbt. Idag är det möjligt att analysera 
hundratals olika biomarkörer från bara några mikroliter vätska, såsom blod. I vår 
andra studie använde vi Olink -baserad proteomik, vilket gjorde det möjligt att 
undersöka över 1 100 
biomarkörer i både blod och ryggvätska. Vår analys avslöjade 
flera lovande potentiella biomarkörer för GBS och CIDP som motiverar vidare 
undersökning. Den mest konsekventa fynden var dock signifikant förhöjda nivåer 
av interleukin -8 (IL8), ett protein som är involverat i immunresponsen. Vi 
upptäckte att nivåer av IL8
 i ryggvätskan är avsevärt högre hos patienter med GBS 
jämfört hos patienter med
 CIDP, vilket kan hjälpa till att särskilja dessa två tillstånd. 
Dett
a är viktigt,
 eftersom val av behandling kan skilja sig åt mellan GBS och akut 
CIDP. Dessutom fann vi att nivåer av IL8 även kan hjälpa till att förutsäga både 
kort- och långsiktiga prognos för patienter med GBS. Under den akuta fasen av 
sjukdomen kan nivåerna av 
IL8 i ryggvätskan hjälpa till att identifiera patienter som 
är i riskzonen för ytterligare försämring de kommande dagarna och till och med ge 
en uppfattning om deras potential för återhämtning efter sex månader. Utöver sitt 
diagnostiska och prognostiska värde 
ger denna upptäckt en djupare förståelse för 
de underliggande mekanismerna bakom dessa sjukdomar och skapar 
förutsättningar för att testa nya behandlingar. IL8
 -hämmare – läkemedel som 
specifikt riktar sig mot 
IL8 – testas redan på patienter med cancer. Med tanke på 
våra resultat skulle användningen 
av dessa läkemedel hos patienter med 
autoimmuna neuropatier också kunna övervägas. 
Sedan Guillain-Barré syndrom först 
beskrevs för över 100 år sedan, har förhöjda 
nivåer av albumin, ett 
äggviteämne, i ryggvätskan betraktats som ett kännetecken 
för GBS och andra autoimmuna neuropatier. När nivåerna är förhöjda, stöder detta 
diagnosen. 
Detta fynd observeras dock 
inte alltid hos varje patient och är inte 
specifikt för autoimmuna neuropatier. För att hitta en mer pålitlig markör tittade vi 
närmare på ett annat äggviteämne i ryggvätskan, beta -trace protein (BTP), det 
näst mest förekommande äggviteämnet 
efter albumin. I vår tredje studie 
upptäckte vi att patienter med GBS och CIDP som hade normala nivåer av albumin 
i stället hade förhöjda nivåer av BTP. Detta tyder på att BTP kan vara en mer känslig 
diagnostisk biomarkör än albumin. Dessutom 
fann vi att nivåer av BTP i 
ryggvätskan också kunde förutsäga hur väl patienter med CIDP svarar på 


behandling, vilket erbjuder ett mer precist och individualiserat tillvägagångssätt 
för att diagnostisera och hantera dessa sjukdomar.  
I jakten på nya behandlingar 
som både är effektiva och säkra testas potentiella 
läkemedel ofta på laboratoriedjur. Med hänsyn till etiska riktlinjer för djurförsök 
följde vi noggrant 3R-principen — ersätt, reducera och förfina (eng. 3R - replace, 
reduce, refine) — som syftar till att minimera användningen av djur samtidigt som 
deras 
välbefinnande främjas. I vår 
fjärde studie undersökte vi den terapeutiska 
potentialen hos imatinib i en djurmodell av GBS. Imatinib är ett läkemedel som 
specifikt riktar sig mot och återställer blod -nervbarriären, en skyddande barriär 
mellan blodet 
och nervsystemet, som är påv erkad vid GBS. Våra resultat var 
lovande. Imatinib förbättrade inte bara de neurologiska funktionerna hos djuren, 
utan visade också positiva effekter på själva blod
 -nervbarriären, vilket påvisades 
med objektiva metoder såsom 
molekylärbiologiska tekniker, avbildning av nerver 
och nervfunktionsundersökningar. Denna studie banar vägen för en framtida 
klinisk prövning av imatinib hos 
patienter med GBS, och inger hopp om ett nytt 
behandlingsalternativ.  
 
  


 
 
Popularno-znanstveni sažetak doktorskog rada  [in 
Croatian] 
Guillain-Barréov sindrom (GBS), kronič na upalna demijelinizirajuća 
poliradikuloneuropatija ( eng. chronic inflammatory demyelinating 
polyradiculoneuropathy, CIDP) i druge imunološki posredovane neuropatije, poput 
multifokalne motorne neuropatije (MMN), autoimune nodopatije i 
paraproteinemijske demijelinizirajuće neuropatije (PDN) pripadaju skupini 
autoimunih neuroloških bolesti. Iako rijetke, važno ih je prepoznati na vrijeme jer 
potencijalno mogu dovesti do 
značajnih poteškoća poput mišićne slabosti ruku i 
nogu, gubitka hoda, pa čak i do zatajenja  disanja.  Ova stanja su lječiva i rana 
dijagnoza  je od presudne 
je važnosti. U protivnom, stanja poput GBS-a i određenih 
vrsta autoimune nodopatije 
mogu biti životno ugrožavajuća. Dijagnosticiranje ovih 
bolesti je u ve
ćini slučajeva teško. Liječnici se uglavnom oslanjaju na anamnezu 
(pacijentovo opisivanje 
simptoma) i fizički pregled, a objektivni testovi poput 
pretraga cerebrospinalne tekućine, krvi i elektrofizioloških testova živčanog 
sustava su nezadovoljavajući i nespecifični. Kao rezultat toga postoji opasnost da 
se dijagnoza propusti, što dovodi do kašnjenja u liječenju, ili da se dijagnoza 
pogrešno postavi, što dovodi to nepotrebnog liječenja i izlaganja pacijenta 
potencijalno opasnim imunosupresivnim lijekovima. Čak i kada je dijagnoza 
ispravna, teško je procijeniti  učinkovitost liječenja. U nekim slučajevima pacijenti 
primaju prekomjerno liječenje i nastavljaju s imunoterapijom iako bi ju zapravo 
trebalo prekinuti jer bolest više nije aktivna. Uz iznimku autoimune nodopatije i vrlo 
uspješne upotrebe 
terapije monoklonalnim antitijelima, u zadnjih trideset godina 
potraga za novim terapijama je bila bez većeg uspjeha. 
Motivirani izazovima s kojima se 
susrećemo u svakodnevnoj kliničkoj praksi, ovaj 
doktorski rad ima za cilj poboljšati dijagnostički proces, predvidjeti ishode 
(prognoze), dati procjenu terapijskog odgovora i istražiti osnovne patološke 
mehanizme ovih bolesti. Također, u istraživanju je  uključeno i ispitivanje 
potencijalno pozitivnog učinka lijeka imatinib u životinjskom modelu GBS-a. 
Kada bolest ošteti ž
ivčane stanice dolazi do njihove razgradnje i oslobađanja 
dijelova stanica u različite tjelesne tekućine , poput krvi . Ključne strukturne 
komponente živčanih stanica, poput neurofilament i tau proteina, bile su u središtu 
našeg istraživanja. U prvom istraživanju smo utvrdili da su razine neurofilamenata 
u krvi i cerebrospinalnoj tekućini kod pacijenata s GBS -om, CIDP-om i PDN-om 
povišene u usporedbi 
sa normalnim razinama što bi potencijalno moglo olakšati 


dijagnostički proces. Što je još zanimljivije, razine neurofilamenata mogu pomoći u 
predviđanju (prognosticiranju) oporavka  pacijenata s GBS-om, te u razlikovanju 
između lje
čivog MMN- a od smrtonosne i neizlječive amiotrofične lateralne 
skleroze (ALS). Takođ
er smo uočili da su razine tau proteina značajno povišene u 
krvi pacijenata sa GBS-om, CIDP-om, MMN-om i PDN-om u usporedbi sa razinama 
u krvi zdravih osoba 
i bolesnicima s drugim bolestima. Navedeno izdvaja tau 
protein kao vrlo obećavajući biomarker za dijagnosticiranje ovih stanja. 
Tehnologije za otkrivanje novih biomarkera —
 mjerljivih znakova zdravlja ili bolesti 
— brzo napreduju. Danas 
je moguće analizirati stotine različitih biomarkera iz samo 
nekoliko mikrolitara tekuć
ine, npr. krvi. U našem drugom istraživanju smo koristili 
Olink proteomiku, koja je omogućila analizu više od 1100 različitih biomarkera u krvi 
i cerebrospinalnoj tekućini. Analizom smo otkrili nekoliko novih potencijalnih 
dijagnostičkih biomarkera za GBS i CIDP koji su vrijedni daljnjeg istraživanja. 
Najkonstantniji nalaz bila je značajno povišena razina interleukina-8 (IL8), 
 proteina 
uključenog u 
imunološki odgovor tijela. Otkrili smo da su razine IL8 u 
cerebrospinalnoj tekućini 
pacijenata s GBS -om znatno povišene u usporedbi s 
pacijentima s CIDP -om, što mo
že pomo ći u razlikovanju te dvije bolesti  i time 
utjecati na odluku o 
terapiji. Također, otkrili smo da IL8 može pomoći u predviđanju 
kratkoročnog i 
dugoročnog ishoda za pacijente s GBS -om. Tijekom akutne faze 
bolesti, IL8 mož
e ukazivati koji su pacijenti u riziku od daljnjeg pogoršanja u 
nasljednim danima i pružiti uvid u mogućnost oporavka nakon šest mjeseci. Osim 
ovog 
dijagnostičkog i prognostičkog potencijala IL8 proteina, ovo otkriće daje uvid 
u osnovne mehanizm
e bolesti i otvara mogućnost za razvoj novih terapija. Tzv. IL8-
inhibitori — lijekovi koji cilja
no djeluju na  IL8 — već se testiraju kod onkolo ških 
pacijenata. S obzirom na naše rezultate bilo bi vrijedno istražiti učinkovitost IL8-
inhibitora kod pacijenata sa autoimunim neuropatijama.  
Od prvog 
opisa Guillain-Barré 
sindroma prije više od 100 godina, povišene razine 
proteina albumina u cerebrospinalnoj 
tekućini se smatraju obilježjem GBS -a i 
srodnih autoimunih neuropatija. Iako 
prisutnost povišenih razina albumina 
podržava dijagnozu autoimunih neuropatija, ona nije uvijek prisutna niti je 
specifična za ove bolesti. Kako bismo pronašli pouzdaniji biomarker, usmjerili smo 
se na beta -trace protein (BTP), drugi najzastupljeniji protein u cerebrospinalnoj 
tekućini nakon 
albumina. U trećem istraživanju smo otkrili da su kod pacijenata s 
GBS-om i CIDP-om, koji su imali normalne razine albumina, razine BTP-a poviš ene, 
što 
ukazuje da  BTP može biti pouzdaniji dijagnostički biomarker od albumina. 
Nadalje,  otkrili smo da razine 
BTP-a u cerebrospinalnoj tekućini mogu poslužiti u 


 
 
predviđanju terapijskog odgovora kod  pacijenat a s CIDP- om, omogućujući 
preciznije dijagnosticiranje i individualniji pristup liječenju. 
U 
potrazi za novim terapijama 
uobičajeno je testirati nove, potencijalne lijekove na 
laboratorijskim životinjama u svrhu utvrđivanja njihove učinkovitosti i sigurnosti. U 
svrhu pridržavanja etičkih smjernice i standarda, pažljivo smo slijedili koncept 3R 
načela: Replacement (zamjena), Reduction (smanjenje) 
i Refinement (poboljšanje) 
koji poma
že u određivanju optimalne upotrebe laboratorijskih životinja u 
znanstvenim istraživanjima. U našem četvrtom radu smo istraživali terapijski 
učinak lijeka imatinib u životinjskom modelu GBS-a. Imatinib je lijek koji specifično 
djeluje na obnavljanje krvno-mo
ždane  barijere, za štitnog sloja  između krvi i 
živ
čanog sustava, koja je oštećena u GBS-u. Rezultati su obećavajući. Imatinib je 
poboljšao neurolo
šku funkciju laboratorijskih životinja i pokazao pozitivne učinke 
na krvno -moždanu 
barijeru, što smo dokazali objektivnim metoda poput 
imunohistokemije, slikovnih pretraga i elektrofizi
oloških testova funkcije živčanog 
sustava. 
Ovo istraživanje predstavlja temelj za buduća klinička ispitivanja  učinka 
imatiniba kod
 pacijenata s GBS -om, nudeć i nadu za novu  mogućnost liječenja  
ovog izazovnog stanja.  
  


  


 
 
Abstract 
There is a lack of reliable biomarkers for diagnosis, prognosis assessment, and 
monitoring of treatment response in immune -mediated neuropathies. The 
diagnosis is predominantly 
based on clinical assessment and electrophysiological 
findings. Although immune -mediated neuropathies are treatable, existing 
therapies 
are nonspecific and fail to provide adequate benefit for a significant 
number of patients. 
In Study I, we investigated 
the diagnostic and prognostic value of axonal injury 
biomarkers, neurofilament light (NfL) chain and total tau (T -tau), in plasma and 
CSF of patients with 
Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS), chronic inflammatory 
demyelinating polyradiculoneuropathy, multifocal motor 
neuropathy (MMN), and 
paraproteinemic demyelinating neuropathy (PDN). In GBS, CIDP, and PDN, levels of 
NfL were elevated in both plasma and CSF, whereas in patients with MMN the 
levels 
of NfL were elevated only in plasma. NfL in plasma and CSF was associated 
with prognosis in GBS and may also help distinguish MMN from amyotrophic 
lateral sclerosis (ALS). Additionally, T-tau in plasma emerged as a novel diagnostic 
biomarker for both acute and chronic immune-mediated neuropathies. 
In Study II, 
we analysed 
over 1100 biomarkers in plasma and CSF using proximity 
extension assay (PEA) technology in two independent cohorts (discovery and 
replication) of patients with GBS and CIDP . We identified several candidate 
biomarkers with potential diagnostic value for GBS and CIDP. However, the most 
consistent finding was upregulation of interleukin-8 (IL8) in CSF. To validate this 
finding, we further analysed 
levels of IL8 using a chemiluminescence assay in a 
third (validation) cohort, which included patients with GBS, CIDP, PDN, MMN,  
healthy individuals, and other disease 
controls. IL8 in CSF is validated as a 
diagnostic biomarker for GBS 
and CIDP, and shows potential as a prognostic 
biomarker for both short- and long-term outcomes in GBS.  
Beta-trace prote in (BTP) is the  second most abundant protein in CSF after 
albumin, yet it has not 
previously been studied in patients with immune-mediated 
neuropathies. In Study III, we 
investigated the levels of BTP in CSF and plasma of 
patients with GBS, CIDP, disease controls, and healthy controls. In CIDP, levels of 
BTP were elevated in both CSF and plasma, while in GBS only in CSF. Notably, in 
patients with GBS and CIDP, levels of BTP in CSF were elevated regardless of the 
Q.Alb, suggesting that BTP ma
y serve as a more informative diagnostic biomarker 


than albumin. Furthermore, the levels of BTP in CSF may have predictive value for 
treatment response in CIDP.  
Numerous attempts to develop new 
therapies for GBS other than intravenous  
immunoglobulin and plasma exchange have been 
unsuccessful. Previous studies 
have shown that blocking the tyrosine kinase receptor platelet- derived growth 
factor receptor-alpha 
(PDGFRa) with imatinib, a small tyrosine kinase inhibitor, 
regulate the function of the brain-blood barrier. In Study IV, we found that imatinib 
reduced the severity of 
experimental autoimmune neuritis (EAN), an animal model 
of GBS, by improving blood -nerve barrier (BNB) integrity. This conclusion was 
supported by clinical scoring, 
functional electrophysiological testing, and 
histological assessment of the BNB. These findings demonstrate the efficacy of 
imatinib in treating EAN and provide evidence for its potential clinical application 
in patients with GBS.  
In summary, this thesis identifies candidate 
biomarkers for immune -mediated 
neuropathies and proposes a novel therapeutic strategy for GBS.

Thesis at a glance 
The overall aim of this thesis was to study biomarkers in immune -mediated 
neuropathies, and to explore the 
potential therapeutic benefits of imatinib in an 
animal model of Guillain-Barré syndrome.  
Paper I 
Neurofilament light chain and total tau in the differential diagnosis and 
prognostic evaluation of acute and chronic inflammatory polyneuropathies 
Aim:
 To investigate the diagnostic and prognostic value of axonal injury 
biomarkers in patients with GBS, CIDP, PDN, and MMN. 
Method: NfL and 
T-tau were measured in the CSF and plasma of patients with GBS, 
CIDP, 
PDN, and MMN, disease-free 
controls, and other disease controls. Outcome 
was measured with the GBS disability scale or INCAT.  
Results:
 In patients with 
GBS, CIDP, and PDN, levels of NfL in CSF and plasma were 
elevated compared to disease
-free controls.  In MMN, levels of NfL in plasma were 
elevated compared to disease
-free controls, but lower in both plasma and CSF 
compared to ALS. Levels of T-tau in plasma were elevated in GBS, CIDP, PDN, and 
MMN compared to all 
control groups. Elevated levels of NfL in CSF and plasma 
were associated with long-term outcome in GBS, but not in CIDP.  
Conclusions: Levels of NfL 
in CSF and plasma are elevated in GBS, CIDP, and PDN. 
In MMN , levels 
are elevated only in plasma. NfL is validated as a prognostic 
biomarker only in GBS. NfL could be used in differentiating patients with MMN from 
ALS. T- tau 
in plasma is a novel biomarker that could be used in a diagnostic 
assessment of patients with  both acute 
and chronic inflammatory 
polyneuropathies. 
 
Paper II 
Validation of elevated levels of interleukin-8 in the cerebrospinal fluid, and 
discovery of new biomarkers in patients with GBS and CIDP using a proximity 
extension assay 
Aim:
 To utilize high-throughput proteomics technology for the discovery of novel 
biomarkers and to validate elevated levels of IL8 in CSF in GBS and CIDP.  


 
 
Method: CSF and plasma from patients with GBS, CIDP, healthy controls, and 
disease controls were analysed 
with Olink multiplex PEA technology from two 
independent cohorts (discovery and replication).
 Levels of IL8 in CSF were further 
analysed using  chemiluminescence 
assay in patients with GBS, CIDP, PDN, MMN, 
healthy controls, and disease 
controls in a third cohort  (validation), and the 
relationship with clinical outcomes was investigated.  
Results: In the CSF of 
patients with 
GBS, nine candidate proteins (IL2Ra, IL6, IL8, 
SELE, CR2, CD1C, 
THBD, ITGAM, and NRP1) were upregulated compared to healthy 
individuals. In the plasma of patients with GBS, CD28 was upregulated, while  five 
additional proteins (MMP3, MMP7, NCAM1, 
THBD, and NRP1) were downregulated. 
In the CSF of patients with CIDP, only IL8 passed  the statistical correction 
analyses. In the plasma of 
patients with CIDP, MMP3 and THBD were upregulated, 
while ITGAM was downregulated. Comparing GBS to CIDP, five  proteins (IL2Ra, IL6, 
IL8, ITGAM, and NRP1) were 
upregulated in the CSF. In the plasma of the same 
comparison, MMP3 and KIT were downregulated.  
In third cohort, 
elevated levels of IL8 
in CSF in GBS and CIDP were validated. In 
PDN, the 
levels of IL8 in CSF were elevated compared NIP and HC. Levels of IL8 in 
CSF 
correlated with impairment 
in the acute phase of GBS, as well as outcome at 
the 6-month follow up. 
Conclusions: IL8 in 
CSF is validated as a diagnostic biomarker for GBS and CIDP, 
and 
may also serve 
as a prognostic 
biomarker in GBS. Multiplex PEA identified 
several potential biomarkers in GBS and CIDP that warrant further investigation.  
 
Paper III 
Beta-trace protein in chronic inflammatory demyelinating 
polyradiculoneuropathy and Guillain-Barré syndrome – clinical utilization and a 
new insight into pathophysiological mechanisms 
Aim: To investigate the role of BTP, the second most abundant protein after 
albumin in CSF, in patients with GBS and CIDP.  
Method: BTP was measured 
in the 
CSF and plasma of patients with GBS, CIDP, 
disease-free controls, and disease controls.  
Results: In 
CIDP, pre-treatment levels of BTP were elevated in both CSF and plasma 
compared to all controls. 
In GBS, pre-treatment levels of BTP were elevated in CSF 


compared to disease-free controls. In GBS and CIDP, levels of BTP in CSF were 
elevated compared to disease-free controls irrespective of Q.Alb. Elevated pre-
treatment levels of BTP in 
CSF predicted therapy failure 3 months after initiation 
of immunotherapy in CIDP.  
Conclusions: BTP in CSF 
and plasma may be a potential biomarker to aid in the 
diagnosis of CIDP. BTP 
in CSF could be a potentially more informative biomarker 
than albumin in a 
subgroup of patients with GBS and CIDP who have a normal or 
mildly elevated Q.alb. BTP 
in CSF may be predictive for therapy response in 
patients with CIDP.  
 
Paper IV 
Imatinib ameliorates experimental autoimmune neuritis, an animal model of 
Guillain-Barré syndrome 
Aim: 
To investigated if 
blocking platelet-derived growth factor receptor -alpha 
(PDGFRα) using imatinib 
could have an effect on  experimental autoimmune 
neuritis (EAN), by enhancing the blood-nerve barrier (BNB) integrity. 
Method: EAN was induced in 
male and female Lewis rats by inoculation with P253-
78 peptide. The experimental group was treated with imatinib, whereas controls 
were treated with phosphate -buffered saline (PBS). Disease severity and 
neurological impairment were assessed using a 10-point clinical symptoms scale, 
as well as functional electrophysiological tests. BNB integrity was assessed with a 
fluorescently labelled dextran tracer.  Vascular fragments 
were isolated from 
spinal
- and peripheral nerves, and analys ed by quantitative real -time PCR 
analyses. 
Results: Imatinib 
reduced severity 
and delayed onset of the disease in EAN.  
Decreased extravasation of the fluorescently labelled  tracer into the parenchyma 
of peripheral nerves, along with
 downregulation of transcripts expressed on BNB 
implicated 
in endothelial cell activation, leukocyte
 recruitment, and angiogenesis, 
was observed in rats treated 
with imatinib compared to controls. Rats treated with 
imatinib exhibited preserved compound 
muscle action potential (CMAP) 
amplitudes, durations, and distal latencies (DLs) throughout the course of EAN. 
CMAP amplitudes were significantly 
lower at the peak of disease and in the  
recovery phase, respectively, in controls 
compared with rats treated with imatinib. 


 
 
Conclusions: Here, we introduce a novel therapeutic approach that primarily 
targets the site of entry of immune cells into the peripheral nervous system, i.e., 
the BNB. The 
clinical disease severity of EAN was ameliorated, while both BNB 
integrity and nerve function 
were largely preserved by imatinib treatment. This 
demonstrates the efficacy of 
imatinib in the treatment of EAN and  strong 
evidence for its potential clinical 
application in the treatment of patients with GBS.

Introduction 
The diagnosis and treatment of immune- mediated neuropathies are often 
challenging due 
to their highly variable clinical presentations and the lack of 
reliable diagnostic and monitoring tools. Common examples include Guillain-Barré 
syndrome (GBS) and chronic inflammatory 
demyelinating 
polyradiculoneuropathy (CIDP). Although relatively rare, immune -mediated 
neuropathies are important to identify 
promptly, as they can lead to considerable 
disability, negatively impact quality of life, impose substantial social and economic 
burdens, and are treatable (1-4). The immunopathogenesis of immune-mediated 
neuropathies is not fully understood, 
but evidence suggests involvement of both 
innate and adaptive immune responses, along with disturbances at the blood -
nerve barrier that permit circulating 
immune cells to access peripheral nerve 
tissue (1, 5-10). The diagnosis of immune-mediated neuropathies relies mainly on 
clinical 
examination and electrophysiological criteria (1, 2). The clinical 
presentation is heterogeneous, and in some cases, distinguishing immune -
mediated from non-inflammatory 
neuropathies can 
be challenging (1, 11, 12). As a 
result, both underdiagnosis and overdiagnosis are relatively common  (13-15). 
Currently, there are no 
reliable biomarkers that can accurately identify immune-
mediated neuropathies, distinguish different subtypes and mimics, 
predict 
disease course and treatment response, 
or guide therapeutic decisions (1, 16-23). 
While immune- mediated neuropathies 
are treatable, current therapies are 
nonspecific and inadequate for a considerable portion of patients, highlighting the 
need for more effective treatment options (2, 8, 24-27). 




 
 1 
1 Background and literature review 
Neuropathies are common neurological disorders that affect the peripheral 
nervous system (PNS) (27, 28). They can be immune -mediated, resulting from an 
immunological attack against 
myelin, axon, node and paranode, or a combination 
of these structures (1, 29). This leads to a broad spectrum of disorders commonly 
referred to as inflammatory, immune-mediated, or autoimmune neuropathies (1, 3, 
4, 29). Their accurate diagnosis and differentiation from other non-inflammatory 
neuropathies, as well as 
other disorders of the nervous system, are essential, as 
they are 
treatable and, in some cases, life-threatening (1, 2). Immune -mediated 
neuropathies 
are traditionally classified as
 primary demyelinating or axonal, and, 
based on clinical 
course and progression over time, into acute, subacute, and 
chronic forms (Figure 1) (1-4, 30). However, since 2012, research interest in the 
pathology of the node of Ranvier (NoR) has been steadily growing, leading to the

2 
identification of a new disease entity, i.e., autoimmune nodo-paranodopathy, and 
challenging the traditional 
classification (29, 31- 38). The proposed new 
classification 
is certainly intriguing and is based on the involved domains of 
myelinated nerve fibres and target antigens (Figure 2) (29, 39).

 3 
1.1 CLINICAL FEATURES AND EPIDEMIOLOGY OF IMMUNE-MEDI ATED 
NEUROPATHIES 
Guillain-Barré syndrome 
Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS) is an acute and monophasic form of i mmune-
mediated neuropathy (25, 
26, 40). While treatable, GBS can be life-threatening. 
GBS 
is a clinically heterogeneous 
condition that, in its classical and most common 
form, presents as rapidly 
progressive (no more than 4 weeks) ascending 
symmetrical weakness of the arms and legs, associated with absent or decreased 
deep tendon reflexes, and with or without sensory symptoms and signs (15, 25, 26, 
40, 41). In some patients, 
the reflexes might be normal or even brisk, which does 
not exclude the diagnosis (15, 41).  
Over the years, different GBS variants with different patterns of weakness and/or 
other symptoms and signs have been 
added to the GBS spectrum. These include 
the pure motor variant, regional variants (such as the pharyngeal-cervical-brachial 
variant, bilateral facial palsy with 
limb paraesthesia, and paraparetic variant), the 
pure sensory variant, 
and Miller-Fisher syndrome (MFS) (15, 40, 41). The pure motor 
variant is characterized 
by motor 
weakness without sensory signs. The 
pharyngeal-cervical-brachial variant affects the bulbar region and proximal upper 
limbs, while bilateral facial palsy affects 
facial muscles and is accompanied by 
sensory symptoms in the 
limbs, but without weakness. The paraparetic variant 
presents with isolated involvement of both lower 
limbs, and the pure sensory 
variant is characterized by 
sensory symptoms, no muscle weakness, and 
sometimes ataxia. MFS (sometimes called the GQ1b antibody syndrome) consists 
of a triad of symptoms: ophthalmoplegia, ataxia, and areflexia  (42). It can also 
occur as an incomplete 
form (lacking all three features simultaneously) or as an 
MFS/GBS overlap , with features of both sensory -motor GBS and MFS.  
Furthermore, Bickerstaff brainstem encephalitis is also considered 
part of the GBS 
spectrum and consists of 
the MFS triad, impaired consciousness, pyramidal tract 
signs, and often overlaps with sensory-motor GBS (15, 25, 41).  
Regardless of the phenotype, n
eurological symptoms typically do not progress 
beyond 4 weeks, and large studies have demonstrated that most patients reach 
the maximum level of disability within 14 days (15, 25, 41, 43, 44). However, almost 
10% of patients 
with GBS can worsen again after initial improvement or 
stabilization, which is known as GBS treatment-related fluctuation (GBS-TRF) (40, 
45, 
46). In some rare 
cases, progression can last up to 6–8 weeks, and this is 


 
4 
sometimes referred to as subacute GBS or subacute inflammatory demyelinating 
polyneuropathy (SIDP) (47, 48). Severity of impairment in GBS varies from mild to 
severe, with intensive care potentially required due to respiratory, cardiovascular 
and/or autonomic complications. Mortality 
is estimated to 2-5% in high-income 
countries, and up to 17% in resource-limited settings (40, 44).  
Population-based studies report a GBS incidence of between 0.81 and 1.91/100 
000 person-years in 
Europe and North America (40, 49, 50), 0.4 – 2.1/100 000 
person
-years in Central 
and South America (40, 51), 0.44 – 0.67/100 000 person-
years 
in east 
Asia (52, 
53), 1.35/100 000 person-years in Australia (40), 2.11/100 
000 person-years in 
Iran (40), and 0.83 – 1.73/100 000 person-years in Africa 
(40). GBS occurs 
in all age groups, although the incidence increases with age, and 
with a male-to-female 
ratio of 1.5:1, i.e., occurs  more often in males than females 
(52).  
 
Chronic Inflammatory Demyelinating Polyradiculoneuropathy  
Chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyradiculoneuropathy 
(CIDP) is a 
spectrum 
of chronic, immune- mediated demyelinating neuropathies  with 
constantly evolving boundaries, 
as its clinical presentations  can vary significantly, 
often leading to challenges 
in diagnosis and treatment (3, 54-56). According to 
recently updated guidelines, the 
diagnostic categories 
are typical CIDP and CIDP 
variants (57). Typical CIDP accounts for approximately 50% of patients and starts 
with weakness and paraesthesia  in distal limbs, which progress in a proximal 
direction and is associated with  absent or decreased deep tendon reflexes (55, 
57). The disease course is typically progressive for more than 8 weeks, though it 
can also present in a relapsing- remitting pattern. Compared to GBS, the 
involvement of cranial nerves is 
uncommon, and neuromuscular respiratory failure 
and autonomic impairment are exceptional (58-61).  However, up to 16%  of CIDP 
cases may present 
as an acute-subacute 
sensorimotor neuropathy, i.e., starting 
acutely and 
initially resembling classical GBS or GBS-TRF. This form is also known 
as acute-onset CIDP  (A
-CIDP), but in contrast to GBS, patient s with A- CIDP 
continues to deteriorate for more than 8 weeks (62
-64). 
The 
most common CIDP variants include multifocal, 
focal, pure motor, pure 
sensory, and distal CIDP . The multifocal 
variant is also known as Lewis-Sumner 
syndrome or multifocal 
acquired demyelinating 
sensory and motor neuropathy 
(MADSAM) (65-67). It presents as an asymmetric predominantly distal 


 
 5 
neuropathy, usually affecting the upper limbs first (66, 68). Lower limbs may also 
be affected at onset, and 
cranial nerves involvement is more frequent compared 
to typical CIDP and 
other variants (66, 69-72). Focal CIDP is characterized by a 
sensory-motor monomelic distribution, 
involving only the brachial or lumbosacral 
plexus, or one or more peripheral nerves in either an upper or lower limb (55, 73-
76). One study showed that 
impairment remained monomelic in most patients 
with plexus involvement, whereas patients with motor or sensory-motor 
involvement of more distal peripheral nerves 
gradually developed MADSAM or 
multifocal motor neuropathy (MMN), in which  the pattern of weakness was 
asymmetric and multifocal (75). Motor CIDP has 
the same distribution of muscle 
weakness as typical CIDP, 
but without sensory signs and symptoms (77, 78), 
whereas the sensory variant is characterized by pure sensory involvement, with 
or without 
a sensory ataxia, and the absence of muscle weakness (79 -81). Distal 
CIDP is characterized by symmetrical distally accentuated 
predominantly sensory 
symptoms, although 
muscle weakness is not uncommon (55, 82, 83) .  Symptoms 
are usually more prominent in the lower than in the upper limbs, leading to gait 
instability. Importantly, all patients with a distal phenotype should be tested for 
paraprotein and anti-myelin-associated glycoprotein (MAG) antibodies, as this 
can affect the correct diagnosis, prognosis and treatment (e.g., in the case of anti-
MAG neuropathy and POEMS syndrome, which 
will be addressed later) (4, 41, 55, 
84). Moreover, 
it has been reported that CIDP variants can evolve over time into 
typical CIDP or another CIDP variant (75, 
85, 86). Furthermore, some patients with 
demyelinating polyneuropathy meeting the 2021 European Academy of 
Neurology/Peripheral Nerve Society (EAN/PNS) 
electrophysiological criteria do 
not fulfil the clinical 
criteria for either typical CIDP or a variant , highlighting the 
complexity and 
heterogeneity of CIDP, as well as the need for better diagnostic 
tools (87).  
Systematic literature reviews and meta-analysis 
report CIDP incidence of 0.2 – 
1.6/100 000 person-year, 
and prevalence 
of 0.8 – 10.3/100 000 persons in Europe, 
USA, Australia, and Japan 
(88-94). As in GBS, prevalence is higher in males than 
females with ratio reported between 1.4:1 and 4
.4:1 (88, 91, 95, 96). 
 
Multifocal Motor Neuropathy 
Multifocal motor neuropathy 
(MMN) is characterized by a slowly progressive, 
predominantly distal , and asymmetrical limb weakness that follows the 


 
6 
distribution of two or more nerves, without objective sensory deficits and upper 
motor neuron signs (1, 3, 
97). MMN often begins with muscle weakness in one arm, 
with commonly 
reported initial symptoms such as wrist drop and reduced grip 
strength (2, 3, 98). In approximately one-third of patients, MMN starts in the lower 
leg with foot drop
, while onset in the proximal part of the arm  is very uncommon 
(98-100). At an early 
stage, weakness without muscle atrophy is typical, but over 
time, 
symptoms may spread 
to other limbs and muscle atrophy can become more 
pronounced (1, 2, 98)
. Cranial nerve involvement and neuromuscular respiratory 
insufficiency are uncommon but have been reported (97, 101-106).  
IgM antibodies against ganglioside GM1 
have been associated with MMN, and 
although initial studies reported a high proportion of seropositivity among patient 
with MMN (ranging from 63% to
 85%), more recent studies have shown that less 
than 50% of patients 
test positive for these antibodies (107-112). Furthermore, IgM 
anti-GM1 antibodies have 
been reported in other neurological diseases, such as 
CIDP, paraproteinemic demyelinating neuropathy with anti-MAG antibodies, and 
amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (108, 111, 
112). In addition, antibodies against other 
antigens have 
also been reported  in MMN, including GM2, GD1b, 
galactocerebroside (GalC), the complex
 of GM1 and galactocerebroside 
(GM1/GalC), and the disulfated heparin disaccharide NS6S (110, 111).  
The reported prevalence 
of MMN 
is 0.40/100 000 persons in Japan, 0.65/100 
000 persons in Austria, and 2/100 000 persons in Italy (113-116).  Like in GBS and 
CIDP, prevalence is higher in males compared with females with a ratio 3:1 (117).  
 
IgM Paraproteinemic Demyelinating Neuropathy 
IgM paraproteinemic 
demyelinating neuropathy (PDN) in 
its typical form presents 
as a chronic, slowly progressive, 
predominantly distal, and symmetric sensory 
impairment in the lower limbs,
 leading to gait imbalance due to sensory ataxia (118-
121). Postural and action tremor 
in the hands is common (119-121). In the early 
stages, relatively 
mild or 
no weakness in distal muscle groups can be observed. 
This typical presentation is often described as the distal acquired demyelinating 
symmetric 
(DADS) phenotype, as 
it is associated with a demyelinating 
neuropathy, IgM monoclonal 
gammopathy (i.e., paraprotein or M-protein), and 
anti-myelin-associated glycoprotein 
(MAG) antibodies  (120-125). Anti-MAG 
antibodies are reported in 56% 
to 83% of patients with the DADS phenotype and 
M
-protein (126, 127). Therefore, the term "anti-MAG neuropathy" is used by some 


 
 7 
authors (84, 120, 128- 130). Although the majority of patients experience slow 
progression, PDN can, over 
time, lead to substantial disability (120, 127, 131). T he 
clinical spectrum may also vary, with
 some patients experiencing only distal 
paraesthesia without any difficulty in daily activities, while others may have a 
subacute or even 
GBS-like 
onset, a faster disease course, or an  asymmetrical and 
multifocal pattern (119, 120, 127). In 
these cases, it is important to rule out 
haematological proliferative disorders, since neuropathy with paraprotein can be 
associated with 
Waldenström macroglobulinemia  or 
other haematological  
malignancies, and IgM 
paraproteinemia is 
more likely to progress to a 
haematological malignancy compared with non -IgM paraproteinemia (e.g., IgG 
and IgA) (84, 118).  
The 
reported prevalence of PDN varies 
widely, ranging from 0.28/100 000 persons 
in Japan and 1.04/100 000 
persons in Southeast England to 5.1/100 000 persons 
in the southwest region 
of Norway (90, 130, 132). Similar to GBS, CIDP, and MMN, 
PDN predominantly affects males, with a male-to-female ratio of 1.9:1 (120, 127). 
Of 
note, patients with demyelinating 
neuropathy and IgG or IgA paraproteinemia 
commonly present with a phenotype resembling typical CIDP, i.e., symmetric 
sensory-motor impairment in both distal and proximal limbs and are negative for 
anti-MAG antibodies (118
-121). In the case of a rapid progression and therapy  
failure, it should prompt re-evaluation of the diagnosis and further investigation 
for other possible explanations, such as POEMS (polyneuropathy, organomegaly, 
endocrinopathy, M-protein and skin changes) syndrome (118, 121, 124). Other less 
common conditions associated with 
paraproteinemia and neuropathies (whether 
demyelinating, axonal, or mixed) include light chain (AL) amyloidosis, CANOMAD 
(chronic 
ataxic neuropathy, ophthalmoplegia, M -protein, 
cold agglutinins, and 
antidisialoysl antibodies) syndrome, CANDA (chronic ataxic neuropathy with 
antidisialoysl IgM antibodies) syndrome, cryoglobulinemia and cryoglobulinaemic 
vasculitis, neurolymphomatosis, 
Bing Neel syndrome, and hyperviscosity 
syndromes (118, 121, 122, 124, 133). It should be noted that coexistence of M-protein 
and chronic axonal 
polyneuropathy in the absence 
of other systemic signs and 
symptoms, as well as in the right clinical context, is most likely coincidental, and 
the paraprotein 
is of unknown 
significance ( i.e., monoclonal gammopathy of 
undetermined significance – MGUS
) (118-121, 125, 133).  
In this thesis and all 
its included studies, the term “PDN” is used to represent IgM-
PDN with or without anti-MAG antibodies.  


 
8 
Autoimmune Nodopathy 
Autoimmune nodopathy (AN) is an emerging 
new entity associated with IgG 
antibodies targeting nodal and/or paranodal cell-adhesion molecules at the NoR, 
along with distinctive clinical features
 (3, 35-37, 39, 41, 57, 134). Anti-contactin-1 
(CNTN1) antibodies 
were the first 
to be identified and linked to specific clinical 
manifestations (36, 135). CNTN1 is 
located on the axolemma in the paranodal 
region and together with axonal contactin-associated protein-1 (Caspr1) and glial 
neurofascin-155 (NF155) form junctions that seal the terminal myelin loops to the 
axon on 
both sides of the nodal gap (136-139). Anti-CNTN1 antibodies are typically 
associated with an acute 
or subacute onset, featuring aggressive sensory-motor 
involvement in all four limbs. 
In addition, symptoms and signs such as sensory 
ataxia, postural tremor, facial weakness due to cranial nerve involvement, and 
concurrent nephrotic syndrome are not uncommon, and in some cases, 
neuromuscular respiratory failure has also been reported (36, 140- 145). 
Antibodies against Caspr1 were 
identified a few years later and have been 
associated with an acute -subacute onset of symmetrical proximal and distal 
sensory-motor impairment (with proximal involvement likely being more 
pronounced). This is often 
accompanied by sensory ataxia, tremor, severe 
neuropathic pain, and multiple cranial nerve involvement, including 
ophthalmoparesis, facial palsy, and dysphagia.  Compared to anti -CNTN1, 
respiratory failure is
 reported more 
frequently. Antibodies can target either Caspr1 
alone 
or the CNTN1/Caspr1 complex, but there are no notable differences in clinical 
characteristics between these two subgroups (144, 146-149). Antibodies against 
NF155 are the most 
common and are associated with typically symmetrical 
sensory-motor involvement in both upper and lower limbs, though predominantly 
distal. Ataxia and tremor 
are generally more debilitating compared to other types 
of AN, with the tremor often displaying cerebellar features. Unlike other forms of 
AN, the majority of these patients are younger at onset, and there have been 
reports of combined central and peripheral demyelination , i.e., simultaneous 
involvement of both the central nervous system (CNS) and peripheral nervous 
system (PNS) (30, 36, 144, 
148, 150-155). Although anti-neurofascin-186 (NF186) 
antibodies have been reported 
in patients with immune-mediated neuropathies 
almost at the same 
time as anti- CNTN1 antibodies, they did not receive much 
attention until a few 
years later (32, 33, 156 -159). NF186 is located at the nodal 
axolemma of the NoR 
and, along with other cell-adhesion molecules, promotes the 
attachment of Schwann cell 
microvilli to the axon and stabilizes the nodal 
structure. Additionally, it plays a key role in the normal clustering of nodal sodium 


 
 9 
(Na+) channels, helping to maintain and stabilize their localization at the node (136, 
138, 139, 160). A few case series reporting patients with isolated anti -NF186 
antibodies suggest an acute- subacute, 
GBS-like onset, characterized by rapid 
progression, severe proximal and 
distal predominantly motor impairment, and 
neuromuscular respiratory failure (32, 158, 159). Recent reports have confirmed 
severe motor impairment and the potential for respiratory failure, while also  
identifying new 
features such as concurrent involvement of both the CNS and PNS, 
asymmetric weakness and/or sensory loss, and isolated cranial nerve involvement 
(161-163). However, more attention in the literature has been given to anti -pan-
neurofascin (panNF) antibodies targeting both isoforms, NF155 
and NF186, as 
these antibodies are often associated with a fulminant disease course resembling 
aggressive GBS, resulting in 
severe tetraplegia and potentially life -threatening 
complications, such as respiratory 
failure, autonomic instability, and nephrotic 
syndrome (164-168).  
There are no specific incidence 
or prevalence studies on AN. Based on available 
reports, it is estimated 
that AN accounts for 5- 10% of patients who meet the 
diagnostic criteria for CIDP 
(36, 144, 148, 169). As with GBS, CIDP, MMN, and PDN, 
AN predominantly affects males (148). 
The 
field of immune-mediated
 neuropathies has been enriched by the discovery 
of AN, and additional antibodies, such as the newly reported anti-LGI4 and anti-
Caspr2, are expected to be identified in the future (170-173). 
 
 
Other immune-mediated neuropathies 
Other immune-mediated neuropathies 
are not the focus of this thesis and will 
only be briefly mentioned in this section. An immune-mediated demyelinating 
process at the level of 
the dorsal roots proximal to the dorsal root ganglion is 
characterised by the term chronic immune sensory polyradiculopathy (CISP), with 
the main feature being progressive gait ataxia (3, 57, 174- 176). CISP was later 
expended to 
a term chronic immune sensorimotor polyradiculopathy (CISMP) and 
CISP-Plus, including also motor involvement (177-179). Vasculitic neuropathy is  a 
complex and 
heterogeneous group of 
disorders with axonal electrophysiological 
features that can occur 
as part of primary systemic vasculitic diseases ( e.g., 
Churg-Strauss syndrome 
or Wegener granulomatosis), secondary to other 
systemic conditions ( e.g., 
connective tissue diseases such as primary Sjögren 
syndrome, rheumatoid arthritis  and systemic lupus erythematosus, or 


 
10 
multisystem disorders such as sarcoidosis and Behçet's disease ) or associated 
with probable cause such as infections 
(e.g., HIV, hepatitis B, or C virus) (2, 180-
194). Vasculitic neuropathy can 
also occur predominantly within the blood supply 
of the PNS, without systemic manifestations of vasculitis. This is referred to as 
non-systemic vasculitic neuropathy (NSVN), and according to more recent 
classification, it is considered a single -organ vasculitis  that can be further 
subclassified 
into various 
variants (180, 194, 195). The clinical presentation is 
characterized by a wide range of phenotypes, with the most common being 
asymmetric, multifocal, overlapping, and predominantly sensory neuropathy in 
cases of NSVN (194- 196). Another important and treatable immune-mediated 
neuropathy, characterized by axonal 
electrophysiological features and 
pure motor 
symptoms, is multifocal acquired 
motor axonopathy (MAMA) (197, 198). 
Furthermore, immune-mediated neuropathies have been linked to malignancies, 
commonly referred to as paraneoplastic neuropathies, as well as to immune-
enhancing drugs and immune-mediated adverse events, 
such as those caused by 
immune checkpoint inhibitors  or anti- tumor necrosis factor -alpha (TNF α) 
medications (199-205).  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


 
 11 
1.2 IMMUNOPATHOGENESIS AND IMMUNOPATHOLOGY OF IMMUNE-
MEDIAT
ED NEUROPATHIES 
Immune-mediated neuropathies are disorders in which innate and adaptive 
(humoral 
and/or cellular) immune mechanisms initiate or sustain damage and/or 
dysfunction of myelin , axon
 and/or the node of Ranvier (NoR)  (1, 206). 
Immunopathogenic mechanisms for some 
immune-mediated neuropathies are 
somewhat understood in 
general, but the precise immunopathology for individual 
subtypes of immune -mediated neuropathies has yet to be fully elucidated. A 
common pathogenic mechanism across several 
immune-mediated neuropathies, 
including GBS and CIDP, is the activation of autoreactive T and/or B lymphocytes, 
macrophages and complement due to 
known or unknown triggers. However, the 
immunopathogenesis of autoimmune nodopathy is distinct due 
to the lack of 
significant inflammation and macrophage-mediated 
demyelination at the target 
sites in peripheral nerves  (57, 
207-210). An important step in the pathway of 
peripheral nerve inflammation is a
 breakdown/dysfunction of the blood –nerve 
barrier (BNB), 
which in turn 
permits circulating antibodies and inflammatory cells 
in 
the blood to gain access to peripheral nerves and cause damage (1, 10). 
 
Guillain
-Barré syndrome 
Traditional classification of GBS into primarily demyelinating or axonal, based on 
electrophysiological and morphological features in patients with GBS, as well as 
its animal models, has 
been challenged , as reflected in the 2023 EAN/PNS 
Guidelines (25, 40, 41, 48, 211). 
 
Approximately two thirds of all 
cases of GBS are triggered by antecedent events 
such as upper respiratory tract infections or gastroenteritis within 3-4 weeks prior 
to developing neurological symptoms. 
However, one third of patients do not 
report any prior event (26, 40). Large prospective case-control studies have 
shown an association between 
GBS and certain pathogens, such as 
Campylobacter jejuni (C. jejuni), cytomegalovirus, Epstein-Barr virus, Mycoplasma 
pneumoniae, 
Haemophilus influenzae, hepatitis 
E virus, influenza A virus, and Zika 
virus (26, 212). The most common identifiable trigger of GBS globally is a C. jejuni 
infection, which leads to an 
immune response and the production of IgG1 and IgG3 
antibodies against bacterial membrane 
structures, which are molecularly similar 
to glycolipids or proteins found on Schwann cells, axons, and/or myelin — an 


 
12 
example of molecular mimicry (30, 213- 216). In susceptible individuals, t hese 
antibodies cross-react with 
gangliosides, such as GM1, GD1a, GQ1b and GT1a,  
located at the NoR and motor 
nerve terminals, leading to complement activation, 
formation of membrane attack complex (MAC), abnormal entry of calcium (Ca2+)  
into the axon, calpain activation, cytoskeletal disruption, and ultimately axonal 
damage (25, 30, 217-219). Macrophages infiltrate 
the periaxonal space, where they 
are activated by anti-ganglioside 
antibodies via Fcγ receptors, further scavenging 
damaged axons (220, 
221). Additionally, the binding of ganglioside antibodies 
disrupts normal Na+ channel clustering, displaces cell adhesion molecules within 
the nodal and paranodal regions, and leads to elongation of the NoR (25, 222). As 
a result, it initially causes reversible conduction failure with rapid recovery (1, 223). 
However, if the process persists, it can eventually lead to extensive axonal 
degeneration and a worse outcome (1, 41). This is reflected in electrophysiological 
findings showing a reduction in compound muscle action potential amplitudes 
with relatively preserved conduction velocities and a reversible conduction block 
in the first case, or non-excitable nerves in the case of severe axonal degeneration 
(41).
 Autopsies performed in the 1980s and 1990s on patients with the above-
mentioned 
electrophysiological features revealed predominantly 
axonal 
involvement, characterized by Wallerian degeneration, with little to no  
demyelination or T cell inflammation. This led 
to the terms acute motor axonal 
neuropathy (AMAN) when only motor nerves are involved, or acute motor and 
sensory axonal 
neuropathy (AMSAN) when both motor and sensory nerves are 
affected (224, 225).  
The immunopathogenesis of the 
demyelinating variant, 
known as acute 
inflammatory demyelinating polyradiculoneuropathy (AIDP) , remains 
inadequately understood . Compared to the axonal 
variant, anti- ganglioside 
antibodies are less frequently detected in AIDP (226
-229). Some gangliosides, 
such as GM1 and 
GQ1b, are not only found in axonal membranes but are also 
expressed on glial membranes at the NoR, where they may play a role in mediating 
paranodal demyelination (48, 230, 231). The search for antibodies targeting myelin 
antigens in GBS has not 
been as promising as the search for anti -ganglioside 
antibodies. Studies on anti -myelin autoantibodies
, including antibodies against 
peripheral myelin protein 22 
(PMP22), myelin protein zero (P0), and myelin protein 
2 (P2), have
 produced inconsistent or contradictory results, though there seems 
to be some evidence for the presence of antibodies against P0 and PMP22 in a 
minority of patients with 
GBS (232-240). However, more recent studies have 


 
 13 
identified IgG and IgM antibodies that strongly bind to myelin components other 
than P0 and PMP22 — particularly to Schwann cells themselves — suggesting that 
these antibodies recognize distinct 
myelin and Schwann cell antigens (241-243). 
Nevertheless, the identity of the 
target antigen remains unknown and requires 
further investigation (241, 243). Moreover, such reactivity has been found in 
approximately a quarter of the patients, implying that it may be significant in at 
least a subgroup of patients (241- 243). It remains unclear whether these 
autoantibodies result from 
molecular mimicry, as seen with anti- ganglioside 
antibodies, or from the activation of 
a broad range of B cells that does not involve 
a specific antigen (26, 30, 243, 244). Autopsy and biopsy studies from patients 
with AIDP 
have identified the presence of T cell infiltrates as well as macrophage-
mediated demyelination (245-250). Further support 
for T-cell autoimmunity in 
the demyelinating variant, but 
not the axonal, was demonstrated in a recent study 
where autoreactive memory CD4+ (with a pro- inflammatory cytotoxic Th1-
phenotype) 
and CD8+ T cells 
targeting P0, P2, and PMP22 myelin antigens were 
found in blood, cerebrospinal 
fluid (CSF) and nerve tissue of patients with AIDP, 
but not in those with AMAN
 (251). Cytotoxic myelin-autoreactive CD4+ T cells 
infiltrate myelin 
and recruit other immune cells, leading to inflammation and myelin 
damage (246, 251, 
252). However, it remains unclear whether the activation of 
autoreactive T cells is driven by the recognition of self -antigens presented by 
macrophages or rather by Schwann 
cells, which, in response to inflammation, may 
have an enhanced ability to process antigens and upregulate MHC class II 
molecules (253-255). It is also unclear whether primary Schwann cell and/or 
myelin damage is 
mainly elicited by 
antibody-mediated mechanisms or T cells, 
and 
to what extent the immune response to a putative autoantigen involves T cells 
and B cells (25, 26, 40). In summary, as Professor Hughes writes in his recent 
review on GBS, “it might be an oversimplification to disregard either”, referring to 
the long-standing debate over whether the demyelinating type is driven by T cells 
or B cells (26). 
It is plausible that the immune response in GBS could well involve 
both.
 
Deposits of complement C3d and 
C5b-9 in the peripheral nerves of patients with 
GBS have also been 
found (217, 248, 249, 256 -258). Compared to AMAN, where 
IgG and complement deposition 
were found at the nodal axolemma and 
macrophages were primarily located in the periaxonal space surrounded by intact 
myelin, complement deposition in AIDP was observed along the outer surface of 
Schwann cells in myelinated fibres, prior to macrophage invasion (217, 225, 247-


 
14 
249). As a result of complement deposition, vesicular dissolution of myelin may 
occur before the onset of myelin phagocytosis by macrophages. The degradation 
of myelin by macrophages could represent a later phase in the progression of the 
disease (248, 249). Activation of terminal components (C5b-9) of the 
complement system in the blood
, along with elevated levels of C3a and C5a in the 
CSF, cleavage products indicating activation of the complement cascade, have 
also been reported in patient with GBS (256, 259).  
Despite exposure to immune triggers, 
such as infections with C. jejuni, more than 
99% of individuals with a  C. jejuni gastroenteritis do not develop autoimmunity, 
suggesting that genetic 
and environmental factors are crucial for an individual to 
develop GBS (48, 260, 261). These factors are unknown, although certain genes, 
such as TLR4, TNFα, FcγR IIA, and HLA DRB (genes involving B and T cells, as well as  
innate immunity pathways) have been linked to GBS (261-264).  
 
Chronic Inflammatory Demyelinating Polyradiculopathy  
No trigger 
has been 
identified as an initiator of CIDP, in contrast to GBS (265, 266). 
An immune- mediated 
aetiology is primarily supported by  the 
immunopathological studies of nerve biopsies 
from patients with CIDP, which have 
identified the infiltrating inflammatory cells such as macrophages, activated T 
cells, immunoglobulin 
and complement deposition, elevated levels of soluble 
adhesion molecules, chemokines, and matrix metalloproteinases, and evidence of 
macrophage-associated demyelination (265, 267-269). It is presumed that both 
humoral and cell-mediated 
immunity act in parallel  against yet unidentified myelin 
and/or Schwann cell antigens
, as supported by evidence of specific inflammatory 
responses in the blood and CSF, and indirectly by the  positive response to 
immunomodulatory treatment (206, 265, 269
-271). However, much like GBS, CIDP 
also exhibits
 significant phenotypic variability 
and could rather be considered as 
a clinical spectrum, suggesting that different immunopathological mechanisms 
are responsible for its phenotypic heterogeneity (3, 265). 
Cell-mediated immunity involvement is indicated by findings of inflammatory cell 
infiltrates in sural nerve biopsies, along with alterations in T cell profiles and 
cytokine levels in the blood and CSF of patients with CIDP (8, 54, 265, 269, 272). 
Biopsy studies of the sural nerve in patients with CIDP suggest a role for cell -
mediated immunity, characterized by an 
increased proportion of macrophages – 
the dominant infiltrating cells – and 
T cells, particularly cytotoxic CD8+ T cells, 


 
 15 
which often form clusters around endoneurial vessels  (252, 273- 277). 
Macrophage-induced demyelination plays 
a central role in the pathogenesis of 
CIDP and has been 
observed in both typical CIDP and its variants, including the 
multifocal, distal, and sensory variants (210, 266, 270, 274, 278, 279). Macrophages 
have various functions, including antigen presentation , the release of pro -
inflammatory cytokines and toxic mediators. Similar 
to GBS, antigen-presenting 
major histocompatibility complex (MHC) is upregulated not only on macrophages 
but also on 
Schwann cells, which subsequently activate infiltrating T cells (280, 
281). They also play a crucial role in the final stages of demyelination by removing 
and phagocytosing myelin (253, 265). However, t he factors that initiate 
macrophage-mediated phagocytosis of myelin in CIDP remain unidentified. It is 
hypothesized that the deposition 
of unidentified autoantibodies on peripheral 
nerve myelin could activate macrophages through the immunoglobulin Fc portion 
or via the complement system, which in turn, is triggered by these autoantibodies 
(8, 241, 274, 282). Supporting this theory is a report of macrophage -induced 
demyelination in a patient with CIDP 
with anti-LM1 antibodies, a predominant 
glycolipid in human 
peripheral nerve myelin , along with the observation of 
complement deposition on myelin sheaths in sural nerve biopsy (283, 284).  
The presence of cytotoxic CD8+ 
T cells in the sural nerves of patients with CIDP, 
mirrored by the presence of similar CD8+ T cells in peripheral blood, suggests that 
the inflammation is likely antigen-driven,
 despite the fact that the nature of the 
target antigen is still 
unknown (273). The role of co-stimulatory molecules, which 
are crucial for effective antigen 
presentation and T-cell activation, has also been 
suggested. Upregulation of B7
-1 (CD80) 
on macrophages and myelinating 
Schwann cells has been 
observed in sural nerve biopsies from patients with CIDP, 
indicating that both cell types may function as professional antigen -presenting 
cells (APCs) (281, 285). Furthermore, a subset of patients with CIDP exhibit 
elevated levels of tumor necrosis factor- alpha ( TNFα) and interleukin-2 (IL2), 
indicating T-cell activation (286, 
287). Evidence of T cell reactivity against the 
myelin protein PMP22 in 
CIDP has been presented in one study (288), though this 
finding has not been confirmed. However, other studies have shown a similar 
dominance and broader activation of CD8+ T lymphocytes compared to CD4+ T 
cells in the blood, as well as a reduction in highly activated T cells (both CD8+ and 
CD4+) following treatment with intravenous immunoglobulins (289). A similar 
pattern of CD8+ T cell dominance was also observed in the CSF (290). 
Additionally, an increase in natural killer T cells (NKT), a unique subset of T 


 
16 
lymphocytes, was observed in patients with CIDP, whereas an increase in natural 
killer cells (NK), part of the innate immune system, was seen in patients with GBS 
(290-292). Furthermore, a significantly higher percentage of pro -inflammatory 
Th17 
T cells was found 
in both the CSF and blood during the active phase of the 
disease, with levels decreasing after treatment. This is noteworthy, as Th17 cells 
are potent inducers of autoimmune diseas es and have been linked to other 
autoimmune conditions, such as multiple sclerosis (293). In addition, 
immunoregulatory cell responses might be dysfunctional in CIDP. CD4+CD25+ T-
regulatory cells (Tregs), which are crucial 
for maintaining immune tolerance and 
preventing autoimmune 
diseases by suppressing self -reactive T cells, are 
reduced in both number 
and function in patients with CIDP compared to healthy 
controls (294-297). 
 
Humoral immunity involvement in CIDP 
is supported by findings from nerve 
biopsies, serological and immunohistochemistry 
studies, passive transfer 
experiments in which demyelination and conduction block s were induced in 
laboratory animals, and, finally
, the efficacy of plasma exchange in patients with 
CIDP (265, 298-300). In 
sural nerve biopsy studies, complement-fixing IgG and 
IgM
 antibodies and complement deposition has 
been found on myelin sheaths 
and the outer surface of Schwann cells (210, 241, 242, 258, 301). In patients with 
CIDP, B  cells exhibit reduced 
expression of FcγRIIB, an inhibitory receptor on B cell 
surfaces that 
prevents their entry into germinal centres  and differentiation into 
IgG-positive plasma cells
 (302, 303). Additionally, levels of B-cell activating factor 
(BAFF) in serum are 
elevated, suggesting prolonged survival of autoreactive B cells 
and ongoing autoantibody production (304, 
305). However, for most patients with 
CIDP, the specific target 
of the autoantibody response remains unidentified. There 
are conflicting reports on presence of antibodies in blood against myelin proteins 
P0, 
P2, and PMP22 (232, 235, 236, 238, 239, 267, 271, 282, 299, 306). Additionally, 
diverse ganglioside and ganglioside-complex antibodies, both IgM and IgG, have 
been reported in CIDP, 
though they are less frequently found compared to GBS 
(226, 282, 307, 308). Of particular interest are the previously mentioned 
antibodies against the glycolipid LM1 and the LM1-containing ganglioside complex 
(anti-GM1/LM1 and 
anti-GD1b/LM1 complex antibodies), which have been more 
frequently associated with ataxia; this could be explained by the predominant 
distribution of LM1 in the dorsal roots  (284, 309). Moreover, sural nerve biopsy 
from a patient with 
anti-LM1 antibodies showed complement deposition on 
myelinated 
fibres and 
macrophage-induced demyelination (283). The search for 


 
 17 
pathogenic antibodies in CIDP continues, as several studies have shown that up 
to 40% of patients exhibit IgG and IgM antibody reactivity against myelinated 
nerve fibres, although the antigen targets themselves have not yet been identified 
(8, 241, 242, 282). Finally, Schwann cells are essential for the regeneration of injured 
nerves in the peripheral nervous system. However, in CIDP, Schwann cells seem to 
become dysfunctional and fail to support regeneration. This dysfunction is likely 
driven by the cytokine granulocyte-macrophage colony-stimulating factor (GM-
CSF
) and by changes in transcription factors that normally promote the growth-
supportive phenotype of Schwann cells (310, 311).  
Activation of the complement system, 
which acts as a link between the innate and 
adaptive immune systems, is
 observed in patients with CIDP (312-314) . This is 
supported by sural nerve 
biopsy studies from the 1980s showing deposition of 
complement C3 on the myelin sheets and intraneural blood vessels (258, 301), as 
well as a more recent 
study demonstrating elevated levels of C5a and the soluble 
terminal complement complex (C5b
-9) in CSF and blood of treatment- naïve 
patients with CIDP (315). Additionally, 
disease activity correlates with the levels of 
complement in serum , which decreased in parallel with clinical improvement 
following treatment with intravenous immunoglobulins (IVIg) (315). Furthermore, 
several studies 
utilising animal models have demonstrated that depletion and 
inhibition of C3 reduce the severity of these models (316-318). 
Similar to GBS, genetic factors 
are thought to play a role in disease susceptibility. 
Previous candidate gene studies have identified an association between the 
SH2D2A gene and the HLA-DRB1*13 allele with an increased risk of CIDP (319, 320). 
A more recent genome -wide 
association study identified three candidate 
pathogenic genes (DIRAS1, GNG7, 
and SLC39A3) for CIDP, although functional 
analyses are needed to further confirm these associations (321).  
 
Multifocal  Motor Neuropathy 
Multifocal motor neuropathy (MMN) is 
generally accepted as a chronic 
progressive immune-mediated motor neuropathy, although there is an ongoing 
debate regarding whether MMN represents a demyelinating or axonal neuropathy 
and whether the underlying pathophysiology primarily affects the NoR (76, 98, 
322, 323).  


 
18 
IgM ganglioside antibodies against GM1 are reported in at least 40% of patients 
with MMN (99, 108, 117, 322). The ganglioside GM1 is found in the nodal and 
paranodal regions, expressed on the axolemma and the myelin sheath of 
peripheral motor and sensory nerves (324). It is thought to play an important role 
in stabilizing paranodal junctions, clustering 
ion channels  ( including the 
maintenance of potassium (K+) channels in paranodal/internodal regions and Na+ 
channels at nodes), cellular Ca2+ homeostasis, and the rapid propagation of action 
potentials (325, 326). However, GM1 is found in higher abundance in peripheral 
motor nerves than in sensory nerves, and its molecular composition differs 
between the two, leading to variations in the binding affinities of anti -GM1 
antibodies (230, 323, 327, 328) . This difference could provide a possible 
explanation for the selective 
impairment of motor fibres in MMN. The molecular 
mimicry theory involving
