GM1 antibodies in the AMAN model of GBS is well 
established, but in MMN, there is currently no clear evidence supporting this (40, 
322). There is no definitive evidence linking antecedent events or triggers to MMN. 
However, a small study initially suggested that antibodies against C. jejuni were 
more commonly found in patient 
with MMN than expected, though this finding was 
not confirmed by a subsequent study (329, 330). In contrast to GBS, IgM anti-
ganglioside GM1 
antibodies in most 
patients with MMN are produced by a single 
or restricted number of B cell clones, whereas autoimmune reaction resulting from 
infections is associated with polyclonal activation of B cells (331). Conversely, in 
chronic autoimmune disorders,
 like immune thrombocytopenic purpura, 
monoclonal or oligoclonal activation of B cells has been observed (332, 333).  
The pathogenicity 
of anti-GM1 IgM 
antibodies is not fully understood, but there is 
evidence supporting 
both complement- dependent and complement-
independent pathways in 
the immunopathogenesis of MMN (322, 327, 334). IgM 
anti-GM1 antibodies bind 
to GM1 and activate complement, leading to the 
formation of the membrane 
attack complex (MAC), disruption and loss of Na + 
channels at the NoR, and distortion of the paranodal axoglial junctions (222, 334, 
335). Interestingly, in vitro models have demonstrated that sera containing anti-
GM1 IgM from 
patients with 
MMN activated the complement system and this was 
inhibited by intravenous immunoglobulins (IVIg) (335, 336). In the complement-
independent pathway, acute disruption of 
Ca2+ homeostasis occurs, likely due to 
the crosslinking of GM1 gangliosides  and a direct action on voltage -gated Ca2+ 
channels, which leads to Ca2+ influx, elevated intracellular (axonal) Ca2+ levels and 
axonal damage, and an increased K+ current (334, 337).  


 
 19 
A significant proportion of patients with MMN are seronegative for IgM anti-GM1 
antibodies, raising the question of 
whether other unidentified antibodies and 
target antigens are involved, or if anti-GM1 antibodies are present but at very low 
concentrations. Other antibodies of interest include those targeting  disulfated 
heparin disaccharide (NS6S) or neurofascin -155 
(NF155), although the results 
remain conflicting (111, 338, 339)
. Additionally, other IgM ganglioside antibodies, 
such as anti-GM2 and anti-GD1b, have been reported in MMN, but they are likely 
cross-reactive with GM1 (110, 340)
.  
A motor conduction block is 
an electrophysiological hallmark of MMN; however, 
several reports have noted that MMN can present with typical clinical features but 
without detectable conduction block (97, 100, 341, 342). This could be due to the 
conduction block occurring at 
very proximal or distal sites, where standard 
electrophysiological tests may not be able to detect the block. Initially, the 
pathophysiological mechanism underlying conduction block was thought to be 
caused by focal demyelination. Nerve biopsy studies from patients with MMN are 
scarce and show conflicting 
results, with some indicating demyelination and a lack 
of inflammatory cells (343-345), while 
others show evidence of primary axonal 
degeneration with small perivascular lymphocytic infiltrates (76, 322, 346). One 
possible explanation is that 
different contributing pathological mechanisms lead 
to distinct pathological 
changes, or that demyelination is more pronounced at 
sites distal to the conduction block. Alternatively, patients with longer disease 
duration may have chronic axonal loss. However, growing evidence now points to 
functional disturbances at the NoR as the likely cause of the block  in MMN (76, 
322). Conduction block can 
arise from any pathological process that disrupts 
saltatory conduction, including demyelination and dysfunction of ion channels , 
such as 
an antibody-mediated functional channelopathy at the NoR (76, 347-
349). In addition, nerve excitability 
studies demonstrated hyperpolarisation and 
potential dysfunction of the 
axonal Na +/K+ pump, suggesting that axonal 
hyperpolarization may represent a stable state and that conduction block in MMN 
results from a functional disturbance (i.e., antibody-mediated attack), 
 rather than 
inflammatory demyelination as seen in CIDP 
(76, 322, 349, 350). 
Similar to GBS and CIDP, 
a genetic predisposition to autoimmunity has also been 
suggested, as an increased frequency of the HLA -DRB1*15 haplotype and other 
autoimmune diseases has been observed in patients with MMN (351, 352). 
 


 
20 
IgM Paraproteinemic Demyelinating Neuropathy 
Demyelinating polyneuropathies linked to 
monoclonal M protein (paraprotein) are 
commonly termed paraproteinemic, as the paraprotein  is considered to play a 
pathogenic role. Paraproteins are monoclonal 
immunoglobulins produced by an 
abnormal clone of plasma cells (353). Clonal proliferation may occur in the context 
of a hematologic malignancy/premalignancy, 
or a more benign condition known 
as a monoclonal gammopathy of undetermined significance (MGUS) (1, 124).  
Anti-myelin-associated glycoprotein (MAG) antibodies are produced by CD5+ B 
cells and are typically associated with monoclonal gammopathy, specifically IgM 
paraproteinemia-related neuropathy (PDN) (118, 354). These antibodies are found 
in 56% to 83% 
of affected patients (126, 127). As previously mentioned, the term 
anti-MAG 
neuropathy is occasionally used in a similar context . MAG is a 
glycoprotein 
expressed on the peri -axonal surface of Schwann cells  between 
myelinating cells and axons  (355-357)
. It promotes axon -Schwann cell 
interactions and has
 an important 
role in the maintenance of the integrity of both 
myelin and axons (358-360)
. MAG is also involved in the early stages of 
myelination, where it 
aids in the wrapping of axons by glial cells to form the myelin 
sheaths (361, 362).  
Anti-MAG antibodies target the human natural killer-1 (HNK1) epitope on MAG, and 
to a lesser extent, 
sulfo-glucuronosyl paraglobosid (SGPG) on peripheral nerves 
(358, 363). The co-localization of IgM deposits with MAG in the paranodal region 
of the NoR and in areas of split myelin lamellae further strengthens the pathogenic 
role of anti-MAG antibodies (120, 364- 366). Moreover, a study using an animal 
passive transfer model 
demonstrated features in the host animal closely 
resembling those observed in humans with anti-MAG neuropathy (367). However, 
anti-MAG antibodies have 
been reported in up to 5% of patients exhibiting clinical 
and electrophysiological features identical 
to PDN, but without detectable M -
protein (368- 370). In some cases, M- protein was detected after 3 -4 years, 
suggesting that the condition 
may begin with an antigen -driven autoimmune 
response, followed by clonal 
expansion and the development of monoclonal 
gammopathy (369, 370). 
Due to the large size 
of IgM paraproteins and anti-MAG antibodies, it is presumed 
that their passage through 
the BNB is restricted. However, several studies suggest 
that the BNB is impaired in patients with PDN, as demonstrated by increased 


 
 21 
transcellular permeability mediated by TNF α/nuclear factor -kappa B (NF -κB) 
paracrine signalling (371-373).  
Nerve biopsy typically
 reveals myelin 
widening, a characteristic pathological 
finding 
attributed to the direct binding of IgM antibodies to the myelin membrane, 
which subsequently leads to abnormal myelin spacing and the widening of myelin 
lamellae, particularly at the nodal regions (364, 374). Additionally, segmental 
demyelination and remyelination  with minimal inflammation , accompanied by 
deposits of IgM
 and complement on myelinated nerve fibres, are  observed, 
suggesting complement -mediated demyelination (355, 356, 375- 378) . This 
suggests that complement may 
function as a n important mediator, ultimately 
leading to 
the detachment 
of terminal loops from the axolemma  and leading to 
widely spaced myelin at the NoR (120).  
Interestingly, 60-70% of 
patients with PDN and anti -MAG antibodies have a 
somatic point mutation in 
the myeloid differentiation factor 88 (MYD88) gene, 
which promotes cellular proliferation by activating Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) 
(379, 380). This discovery has 
paved the way for precision medicine and the 
development of tailored therapies, as discussed further in Section 1.5 on the 
treatment of immune-mediated neuropathies (page 48).  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


 
22 
1.3 BLOOD-NERVE BARRIER 
Overview of Peripheral Nerve and Blood-Nerve Barrier Anatomy and 
Physiology
 
Connective tissue compartments of the peripheral nerve 
The three connective tissue 
compartments of the peripheral nerve are the 
endoneurium, perineurium, and epineurium ( Figure 3) (381). The endoneurium 
contains myelinated 
axons, Schwann cells, unmyelinated Remak bundles, resident 
macrophages, fibroblasts, and capillary -like microvessels. It also contains 
endoneurial 
fluid similar to the cerebrospinal fluid  (382). The perineurium 
surrounds
  the  endoneurium,  organizing  it  into  structures  called  fascicles,  i.e., 
collection of several axons, surrounded by the protective perineurium. The epi-
 
 
F 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 3. Cross-section of the peripheral nerve demonstrating its structural organization 
and component
s, with major structures highlighted. Reproduced from Sun Y et al. Adv Ther 
(Weinh). 2023 Apr;6(4):2200150. Copyright 2023 wit h permission from John Wiley & Sons, 
Inc. © 
2023 Wiley-VCH GmbH. 
 
neurium is the outermost connective tissue that encases the entire nerve; it is 
highly vascularized, but 
the epineurial blood vessels do not have barrier functions, 
hence the epineurium is not part of the blood-nerve barrier system (381, 383, 384). 
These connective tissues 
merge with the connective tissues of the CNS at the 
nerve root level. Here, the epineurium blends with dura mater, the outer layer of 
the perineurium merges with 
arachnoid mater, and the inner layer transitions into 
the root sheath, which subsequently merges with the pia mater, while at the root 


 
 23 
attachment zone, the glia limitans forms the connection between the CNS and the 
PNS (384-388).  
Blood supply of the peripheral nerve 
Peripheral nerves 
contain two 
distinct blood supply systems that are 
morphologically and physiologically different (Figure 4). The extrinsic, consisting 
of arteries, arterioles, and 
veins located within the epineurium. The intrinsic, 
consisting of microvessels within the fascicles and endoneurium (389). These  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 4 . Extrinsic and intrinsic vascular system  and c ontinuous  capillaries of the 
peripher
al nerve. Reproduced from Rei na MA et al. Nerves and Nerve Injuries. Elsevier 2015. 
Vol. 1, Part I, Chapter 7, p. 91-106. © Copyri ght 2015 wi th permission from Elsevier. 
 
two systems are connected by vessels originating in the epineurium, passing 
through the perineurium and extending into the endoneurium. The endothelial 
cells of the extrinsic system display numerous fenestrations and lack tight 
junction proteins, while the endoneurial microvessels have distinct characteristics 
that exhibit blood-tissue barrier properties (390).  
 
Structural and molecular composition of 
the blood-nerve barrier 
The 
blood-nerve barrier (BNB) is an anatomical and physiological structure 
specific to peripheral 
nerves, crucial for maintaining a stable microenvironment 
and homeostasis within the PNS
 (381, 383, 391). BNB exist s at two levels: 


 
24 
perineurial, and endoneurial (Figure 5). The epineurium, the outermost layer of the 
peripheral nerve, is highly vascularized, containing numerous fenestrated blood 
vessels but these do not possess a barrier function (392) (Figure 4).  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 5. Two vascular units of the BNB of the peripheral nerve (peri neurial and endoneurial). A) 
Schematic 
of the BNB in the cross-section. B) Confocal image of transverse sections; BV, blood 
vessel. Reproduced f ro
m Malong L et al. Dev Cell. 2023 Feb 6;58(3):174 -191.e8. doi: 
10
.1016/j.devcel.2023.01.002. © 202 3 The Authors. Open  access article under the Creative 
Commons CC-BY license (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/). 
 
The endoneurial level is considered the primary site of the BNB, as it involves direct 
communication between the microvascular endothelium of the endoneurium and 
circulating blood. It is composed of endothelial cells of the endoneurial 
microvasculature which are sealed with tight junctions (TJs) 
 and surrounded by 
basement membrane with embedded pericytes (391).  
The TJs are intercellular 
adhesion complexes of transmembrane and 
cytoplasmatic TJ -associated proteins, which are essential for controlling 
paracellular permeability and maintaining endoneurial homeostasis (382). They 
are found between endothelial cells and between pericytes (381, 393). There is 
limited knowledge on the molecular composition of BNB, but in situ studies have 
shown that TJs of 
the BNB at the endoneurial level consist mainly of claudin-5, 
occludin, zona occludens-1 (ZO1) and ZO2 (382, 394-396).   
In comparison with the blood-brain 
barrier (BBB), the BNB lacks cells equivalent to 
astrocytes and glia limitans (formed by astrocytic endfeet processes), which are 
crucial in enhancing 
the barrier function of the BBB in the CNS (397, 398). However, 


 
 25 
pericytes play an important role in maintaining vascular stability throughout the 
blood-nervous system barriers, including BNB, blood-spinal cord barrier and BBB 
(399, 400). They enhance the barrier 
function of endothelial cells through a 
paracrine mechanism by secreting basic fibroblast growth factor (bFGF) and glial 
cell-derived neurotrophic factor (GDNF), which promote the upregulation of 
claudin-5 and tightening 
of TJs. Pericytes have also neuroprotective properties, 
preventing axonal loss 
and promoting axonal regeneration through the secretion 
of additional neurotrophic factors such as nerve growth factor (NGF) and brain-
derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) (394, 
395). Additionally, pericytes display 
phagocytic activity and have the potential to act as pluripotent stem cells, 
capable of differentiating into neurons and glial cells, thereby contributing not only 
to BNB maintenance but also to tissue repair (401). Some authors consider 
pericytes in the peripheral nerves to have the same role as astrocytes in the BBB 
(10).  
The composition of the basement 
membrane (BM) in the BNB is not as well 
characterized as in the BBB, however, studies have shown that the BM consists of 
a combination of extracellular matrix proteins, including collagen type IV, laminin-
8 (laminin-411) and laminin
-10 (laminin-511), fibronectin, and tissue inhibitor of 
metalloproteinases-1 (TIMP1) (390, 402). The function of the BM is to provide 
structural support to endothelial cells and pericytes, while also regulating the 
leukocyte transendothelial trafficking and the movement of molecules according 
to their size and electrical charge (381, 390, 391). For instance, it has been shown 
in in vitro and animal models 
that laminin-10 has an important role in inhibition of 
transendothelial migration of autoreactive T cells (403, 404).  
The perineurium represents 
a continuum 
with the pia-arachnoid of the CNS and 
extends 
distally, composed of concentric layers of epithelioid myofibroblasts that 
encase the endoneurium. It plays a vital role in maintaining endoneurial 
homeostasis and acts as a critical barrier between the endoneurial and epineurial 
interstitial fluid compartments (391, 405)
. The external part (pars fibrosa) has 
mainly mechanical function, while the inner part (pars epitheloidea) functions as a 
diffusion barrier (382). TJs at the perineurial  level include ZO1, occludin, claudin-1, 
claudin-3, claudin-19, and tricellulin 
(406).  
In vitro and in 
situ studies 
have shown that the BNB composition and function is 
likely regulated 
by autocrine and/or paracrine secretion of glial -derived 
neurotrophic factor (GDNF), vascular 
endothelial growth factor (VEGF), basic  


 
26 
fibroblast growth factor (bFGF), transforming growth factor -beta (TGFβ), and 
glucocorticoids from endothelial cells, 
pericytes, Schwann cells, and fibroblasts. 
This is supported by the identification of mitogen receptors on the surface of 
endoneurial endothelial cell (394, 407-410). Furthermore, studies have shown that 
downregulation of claudin-5, leading to “leaky” BNB, can be induced by VEGF , 
TGFβ and pro-inflammatory cytokines, such as interleukin-8 (IL8) (394, 411, 412). In 
contrast, corticosteroids can upregulate 
claudin-5 and enhance BNB integrity 
(413, 414).  
The BNB also plays an 
important role in immunosurveillance  and leukocyte-
peripheral nerve cross -talk, regulated by 
specific chemokines and the up- or 
downregulation of various 
glycoproteins on 
the surfaces of endothelial and 
immune cells (391). In situ  and in vitro  studies have identified chemokines 
important for interaction between 
endoneurial endothelial cells and neutrophils 
(CXCL3), monocytes (CCL2, CCL14, CX3CL1), NKT cells (CXCL16), and T cells (CCL2, 
CX3CL1) during normal immunosurveillance (407, 
415). Moreover, endoneurial 
endothelial cells express cell adhesion molecules, including vascular cell adhesion 
molecule-1 (VCAM1), P -selectin, E -selectin, integrin β, intercellular adhesion 
molecule
-1 (ICAM1), fibronectin 
type III, which facilitate the adhesion of immune 
cells and their subsequent transmigration 
(407, 416-418).  
 
Blood-Nerve Barrier Dysfunction in Immune-Mediated Neuropathie s 
The breakdown and dysfunction of the BNB is a critical event in the pathogenesis 
of several immune-mediated 
neuropathies, allowing 
the entry of immunoglobulins, 
immune cells, and cytotoxic molecules into the endoneurium (1, 10).  
Guillain-Barré syndrome 
The detailed 
structural and molecular changes
 of the BNB in patients with GBS 
have yet to be 
described. However, sural nerve biopsies from three patients with 
GBS, along with observations using experimental autoimmune neuritis (EAN), an 
animal 
model of GBS, revealed intact endoneurial microvascular junctions, 
implying that  pathogenic leukocyte trafficking occurs across a structurally 
preserved BNB via a paracellular route, i.e., leukocyte transmigration is an active, 
regulated, and multistep process (419), as discussed later. This observation aligns 
with a previous EAN study demonstrating that most interendothelial junctions 
lacked visible gaps  (420). In contrast, in an EAN model induced by transfer of 


 
 27 
autoreactive T cells, disruption of interendothelial TJs, separation of adjacent 
endothelial cells, and abnormally shaped endothelium  were observed, 
accompanied by increased perineurial 
permeability with fibrin deposition and 
leukocyte infiltration between layers of the perineurial sheath (421). 
Nevertheless, it is important to 
note that GM1 is found not only on the axolemma 
and myelin but also 
on endoneurial endothelial cells (422-424). An in vitro model 
has demonstrated 
that anti -GM1 antibodies, 
present in GBS as well as other 
chronic immune-mediated neuropathies like
 MMN, can compromise the BNB, 
leading to 
its dysfunction 
in a complement-independent manner (425). Moreover, 
sera from 
patients with GBS 
have been shown to impair BNB integrity 
independently of antibody status — that is, even in the absence of anti -GM1 
antibodies, patient sera can still 
contribute to BNB dysfunction (426). In contrast, 
anti-GQ1b antibodies, frequently 
found in patient s with MFS, do  not appear to 
influence the permeability of BNB (427).  
 
Chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyradiculopathy  
Nerve biopsy studies 
in patients 
with CIDP have reported fenestration of 
endoneurial capillaries, loss of TJs, and intercellular gaps between adjacent 
endothelial cells (10, 428). Alterations in TJ proteins and a “leaky” BNB are likely 
due to downregulation of 
claudin-5 
and altered localisation of ZO1 (429). An in 
vitro BNB model 
confirmed a reduced expression of claudin-5, which plays a key 
role in regulating vascular 
permeability, in several CIDP phenotypes (typical CIDP, 
Lewis-Sumner syndrome, and 
distal variant), as well as in MMN  (430, 431). This 
reduction was more pronounced in typical CIDP 
compared to Lewis-Sumner 
syndrome and the distal variant (430). In 
contrast, in Lewis-Sumner syndrome 
CXCL10 (IP10), interleukin-7 (IL7), VEGF
, and granulocyte colony-stimulating factor 
(G-CSF) were upregulated 
compared to typical CIDP in an in vitro BNB model 
(432). Of 
these four cytokines, 
CXCL10 (IP10) was markedly elevated, and the 
authors concluded that this 
promotes the migration of CXCR3- positive T 
lymphocytes across the 
BNB. A more recent study (433) showed that CXCL10 
(IP10) contributes to BBB 
damage by stimulating the production of tumor necrosis 
factor-alpha 
(TNFα), a cytokine known to induce barrier dysfunction in various 
human diseases (434, 435). Further support for the dysfunction and breakdown 
of the BNB in CIDP comes from findings of elevated levels of TNFα and interleukin-


 
28 
1 beta (IL1β ) in the blood of patients with CIDP  — cytokines with the ability to 
disrupt endothelial TJs and promote vascular permeabilit
y (287, 434, 436-439).  
 
Multifocal motor neuropathy 
Serum from patients with MMN, 
both with and without IgM anti-GM1 antibodies, 
has been shown to 
induce BNB dysfunction, with motor nerves exhibiting greater 
susceptibility than sensory nerves (431). This occurs in two distinct steps. Firstly, 
the sera activate
 the microvascular endothelial cells of the BNB, prompting them 
to secrete VEGF in 
an autocrine manner. VEGF, a pro -inflammatory cytokine, 
increases endothelial 
cell permeability and disrupt s the BNB (440, 441). While the 
factor responsible for increased 
VEGF secretion remains unknown, this leads to a 
decrease in  claudin-5 levels and disruption of TJs. Secondly, the sera from 
patients with MMN stimulate the 
expression of ICAM1 on the endothelial cells of 
the BNB, which mediates 
leukocyte-endothelial cell adhesion through nuclear 
factor-kappa 
B (NF -κB) 
paracrine signalling. This, in turn, facilitate s leukocyte 
adhesion and their 
subsequent entry 
into the endoneurium (431). Similar to Lewis-
Sumner syndrome, CXCL10 (IP10) was 
also markedly elevated in an in vitro BNB 
model, suggesting its role in the dysfunction of the BNB (432).  
 
Paraproteinemic demyelinating neuropathy 
 
In patients with 
PDN, endoneurial 
capillaries were characterized by fenestrated 
endothelium, indicating a breakdown of the BNB, along with the selective presence 
of monoclonal IgM in the typically widened lamellae of myelinated fibres (442). 
This is supported by studies showing BNB impairment and increased transcellular 
permeability mediated by TNF α/NF-κB paracrine signa lling (371- 373).
  In 
demyelinating neuropathy linked to Waldenström's 
macroglobulinemia, nerve 
biopsy findings also revealed disruption of the BNB and the presence of gaps 
between the endoneurial endothelial cells (371).  
 
 
 
 
 


 
 29 
Leukocyte trafficking across the blood-nerve barrier in immune-mediated 
neuropathies 
An important step in 
the breakdown and dysfunction of the BNB and  
immunopathogenesis of immune -mediated neuropathies 
is an increase  in  
leukocyte trafficking across the BNB (7, 
10). This is a multistep process, involving 
recruitment, rolling, activation, adhesion, crawling, 
and paracellular or transcellular 
migration of leukocytes, all orchestrated by complex chemokines pathways and 
interactions with chemokines  receptors, integrins, matrix metalloproteases 
(MMPs), and cell 
adhesion molecules (CAMs) (443-445). The leukocyte trafficking 
cascade starts 
with selectin
-dependent leukocyte rolling followed by chemokine-
induced activation of endothelial cells 
and leukocytes. Slow rolling facilitates an 
integrin-dependent leukocyte adhesion to the surface of endothelial cells through 
integrin:CAM interaction, particularly with ICAM1 and VCAM1, and finally leukocyte 
extravasation (390).  
Upregulation of pro- inflammatory cytokines, suggesting activation 
of both the 
innate 
and adaptive immune systems, has been reported in an in vitro BNB model 
GBS (417). The activation of the innate immune system is indicated by the 
upregulation of chemokine 
(C-C motif) ligand-2 (CCL2) (also known as monocyte 
chemoattractant protein-1, MCP1), involved in 
monocyte/macrophage trafficking 
and acting 
as a chemoattractant 
for dendritic cells (DCs), and chemokine (C-X-C 
motif) ligand-2 and -3 (
CXCL2, CXCL3) ( also called macrophage inflammatory 
protein 2-alpha and 
2-beta, MIP2a and MIP2b), and chemokine (C- X-C motif) 
ligand-8 (CXCL8 ) 
(interleukin-8, IL8), which attracts and activates neutrophils 
(412, 417, 446). The activation 
of the adaptive immune system is indicated by the 
upregulation of CXCL9/CXCL10/CXCL11 (MIG, 
IP10, IP9), which attract Th1 T cells, 
CCL20 (MIP3), which attracts Th17 T cells, and CCL27, which recruits activated T 
cells (417, 447).  
Moreover, the interaction between ICAM1 
and its counterligand αM-integrin 
(CD11b), expressed on monocytes/macrophages, NK cells, DCs, neutrophils, CD8+ 
T cells, and a subset of B cells, highlights the crucial role of the α
M-integrin–ICAM1 
pathway in the pathogenesis and leukocyte trafficking in GBS (417). The 
pathogenetic relevance of αM-integrin (CD11b) is supported by findings of clusters 
of infiltrating CD11b+ leukocytes (predominantly 
monocytes/macrophages) in the 
endoneurium, along with disrupted Schwann cells membrane organization in sural 
nerve biops ies from patients with GBS (419). F low cytometry of circulating 
leukocytes from patients with 
GBS revealed increased numbers of CD11b+ CD14+ 


 
30 
monocytes, CD19+ B lymphocytes, and CD3+CD8+ T lymphocytes (391, 419). 
Additionally, elevated levels of soluble ICAM1, VCAM1, and selectin have been 
reported in GBS, highlighting 
the significance of CAMs in the pathogenesis of 
immune-mediated neuropathies (448, 449).  
Interestingly, almost all of the 
cytokines mentioned above (CCL2, CCL27, 
CXCL8/9/10) have been reported to be elevated in the CSF and/or blood of 
patients with GBS and 
CIDP (450-458). Furthermore, the upregulation of CCL2 
(MCP1) aligns with the finding of an increased number of DCs in the CSF of patients 
with GBS and CIDP (453). The upregulation of CXCL9 and CXCL10 is consistent 
with the presence of CD4+ T cells (with a pro -inflammatory, cytotoxic Th1 -
phenotype) in the 
blood, CSF, 
and nerve tissue of patients with GBS (251). The 
upregulation of CCL20 correlates with 
a significantly higher percentage of pro -
inflammatory Th17 T cells in 
the CSF and blood during the active phase of CIDP 
(293).  
Fibronectin connecting segment -1 (FNCS1) is 
a glycoprotein expressed on 
endoneurial endothelial cells at 
sites of inflammation within BNB in vitro models 
(416, 417). Its counterligand, α4-integrin (CD49d), has been linked to CD49+ 
leukocytes, which have been observed in nerve biopsies from patients with CIDP 
(459). A study utilizing  a n in vitro CIDP BNB model and nerve biopsies from 
patients with CIDP has suggest
ed that the α4-integrin – FNCS1 pathway plays an 
important role in leukocyte trafficking 
in CIDP (460). Of interest, RNA-sequencing 
of peripheral 
blood mononuclear leukocytes from patients with CIDP revealed a 
6.9-fold increase in CD11d (αD-integrin) compared to healthy controls. Additionally,  
flow cytometry of circulating leukocytes showed elevated
 CD11d protein 
expression on CD14+ monocytes, 
CD19+ B cells, and CD3+CD4+ T cells. Clusters 
of CD11d+ CD68+ macrophages, CD19+ 
B cells, and CD3+ T cells have also been 
observed in sural nerve biopsies 
from patients with CIDP (391). While further 
research is ongoing, these findings suggest that CD11d may facilitate leukocyte 
trafficking and contribute to a pro-inflammatory response in CIDP.  
 
 
 
 


 
 31 
1.4 DIAGNOSIS AND PROGNOSIS OF IMMUNE-MEDI ATED NEUROPATHIES 
When the clinical presentation is typical and supported by nerve conduction 
studies, the diagnostic process can be relatively straightforward. However, due to 
the heterogeneity and wide spectrum of immune-mediated neuropathies, 
including mimics, as well 
as emerging new entities like autoimmune nodopathy, 
diagnosis can often be 
challenging (15, 461, 462). This explains, for example, why 
misdiagnosis is common and has been reported in nearly half of CIDP cases (13, 
14).  
 
Clinical diagnostic and prognostic tools with focus on biomarkers 
The diagnosis of immune-mediated
 neuropathies is based on a combination of 
clinical presentation, electrophysiological testing, blood and CSF analysis, and in 
some cases, magnetic resonance imaging 
(MRI), nerve ultrasound studies and 
nerve biopsy, all in accordance with international guidelines and expert opinions 
(3, 36, 37, 41, 57, 97, 118, 164, 463).  
Since its original description by 
Guillain, Barré, and Strohl in 1916, high levels of 
albumin and low cell count in the CSF, referred to as albumino-cytological 
dissociation (ACD), have been a 
hallmark of GBS (26, 464). Elevated levels of 
albumin in the CSF 
and the CSF/blood albumin quotient (Q.Alb) are commonly 
used as indicators of BNB damage, a laboratory finding supporting the diagnoses 
of immune-mediated neuropathies. However, this finding is not specific to 
immune-mediated neuropathies, and normal values do not rule out these 
diseases. Additionally, there are no well- established cut-offs for the levels of 
albumin or Q.Alb (14, 56, 
372, 465, 466). ACD is neither specific nor sufficiently 
sensitive for immune-mediated neuropathies and can occur in other disorders of 
the peripheral and central nervous system (467, 468). While diagnostic specificity 
and sensitivity of nerve conduction studies in CIDP are reasonably high (57, 469, 
470), they vary significantly in GBS depending on the criteria used and there is no 
consensus on their application 
(471-475). Although decreased CMAP amplitudes 
in GBS (41, 476, 477) are 
most likely predictive of a less favourable outcome, the 
results 
of studies on CIDP are more conflicting  and not sufficiently reliable for 
assessing prognosis, disease activity or 
response to treatment (478-482). Nerve 
imaging with MRI and 
ultrasound is advancing rapidly, but they lack  diagnostic 
specificity, and their prognostic value is limited (483 -492). Additionally, their 
availability and cost pose significant limitations.  


 
32 
Nonetheless, there is an urgent need for reliable markers that can enhance the 
diagnostic process, serve as prognostic tools, and facilitate the monitoring of 
disease activity and response to treatment. 
 
Biological markers and their classification 
Biological markers, i.e., biomarkers, have 
been extensively investigated over the 
past decade and 
the original definition of biomarkers as “cellular, biochemical or 
molecular alterations that are measurable in biological media” has been expanded 
to include “biological characteristics that are objectively measured and evaluated 
as an 
indicator of normal biological processes, pathogenic processes, or 
pharmacologic responses to therapeutic interventions” (493, 494).  
Biomarkers can be classified in 
several ways. One approach is to group them into 
subtypes based on their potential uses and intended applications, with a single 
biomarker often meeting multiple criteria and serving various purposes (495). 
Depending on how they might be used in medical or scientific contexts, such as 
for diagnosing diseases, predicting outcomes, or monitoring treatment responses, 
they can be categorised in the following groups (496):  
• Diagnostic biomarkers – 
Used to 
identify or verify the presence of a disease or 
condition, these biomarkers 
can not only help identify individuals with a 
disease but also to redefine its classification. For example, the detection of 
nodal antibodies may lead to the reclassification of a patient with GBS or CIDP 
to autoummune nodopathy (169).  
• Prognostic biomarkers – They are 
used in patients with a disease or condition 
to assess the probability 
of a specific event, such as outcome, disease 
recurrence or progression, without taking medical intervention into account 
(e.g., drug treatment o
r surgical procedures). For example, age, the degree of 
muscle weakness
, or the levels of neurofilament light (NfL) chain in blood and 
CSF of patients 
with GBS may serve as prognostic biomarkers when assessing 
the probability of walking independently later on in the recovery phase of the 
disease (497-499).  
• Predictive biomarkers – The presence or 
change in these biomarkers predicts 
and identifies patients who 
are more likely to have a favorable or unfavorable 
effect from treatment and/or a medical intervention, compared to those 
without the same biomarker. 
Compared to prognostic biomarkers, which are 


 
 33 
associated with different disease outcomes, predictive biomarkers are 
associated with the effect of medical intervention/exposure and discriminate 
between those who will or will not respond to therapy. For example, it would be 
interesting to investigate the predict ive value of mutation in the myeloid 
differentiation factor 88 (MYD88) 
gene and the response to treatment with 
Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) inhibitors in patiens with IgM PDN (500).  
• Monitoring biomarkers – They are used 
to longitudinally and repeatedly assess 
the status of 
a disease 
or the effect of a medical intervention. As such, they 
need to be easily collectable and easily quantifiable. For example, serial 
measurements of NfL in blood can facilitate monitoring of disease progression 
and the conversion of asymptomatic 
mutation carriers into early symptomatic 
patients in hereditary transthyretin-mediated (hATTR) amyloidosis (501, 502).  
• Response biomarkers – They reflect the 
change in the level of a certain 
biomarker in response to 
a medical intervention and are associated with a 
biological effect, potentially beneficial or harmful. However, the interpretation 
of response biomarkers is 
not straightforward, as they are not necessarily 
linked to the pathogenesis 
of the disease or the mechanism of drug action. For 
example, studies suggest that levels of NfL in blood decrease in patients with 
CIDP who respond to immunotherapy , but they do not provide definitive 
information on disease activity or whether the treatment can be terminated  
(503, 504). 
• Risk biomarkers – They are biomarkers 
that indicate the likelihood of 
developing a disease or 
condition in an individual who shows no clinical signs 
at present. In contrast to prognostic biomarkers, they indicate the potential 
and susceptibility of an 
individual for developing a disease, rather than 
prognosing an outcome after the disease has already developed. For example, 
cert
ain genetic polymorphisms (e.g., H LA DRB1*0401 and HLA DRB1*1301) have 
been assocaited with an increased risk 
of developing GBS (264). Another 
example is the presence of mutation in the PKNOX gene (rs2839629 and/or 
rs915854) which may 
serve as a risk marker for developing painful 
polyneuropathy in patiens multiple myeloma treated with bortezomib (505).  
• Safety biomarkers – They are used 
to assess the occurrence and severity of 
toxicity 
and potentially harmful adverse effects of a medical intervention (e.g., 
drug treatment). Like monitoring biomarkers, they need to be easily collectable 
and quantifiable, and should 
be collected both before and after exposure to a 
certain medical intervention. For example, complete blood counts and 
urinalysis can serve as safety biomarkers in patients with CIDP undergoing 


 
34 
treatment with cyclophosphamide, as this treatment can cause 
myelosuppression and urotoxicity (506-508). 
• Multicomponent biomarkers – These consist of 
a defined combination of two 
or more individual biomarkers.
 When analysed together in a specific manner, 
their measurements serve as a complex marker 
of either normal biological or 
pathological processes, as well as a marker of biological responses to a medical 
intervention. For example, a 
combined assessment of age, prior diarrhea, and 
muscle weakness in the Erasmus GBS Outcome Score (EGOS) model can be 
used to assess long-term outcome in patients with GBS (499, 509). 
• Digital biomarkers - an 
integral part of future biomarker research. There is no 
consensus on a definition 
of digital biomarkers. According to the European 
Medicines Agency, a digital biomarker is “an objective, quantifiable measure of 
physiology and/or behaviour used as an indicator of biological, pathological 
process or response to an exposure or an intervention that is derived from a 
digital measure” (510, 511). A digital biomarker represents digitized data 
collected from patients through digital devices, which can be used to track 
real-time changes in an individual’s condition and potentially enhance 
personalized medicine by enabling 
early detection, more effective monitoring, 
and tailored interventions (495, 512). Digital biomarkers are measured with 
wearable devices such as wristwatches and smartphones, portable devices 
such as sensors and smart home devices, and even implantable or digestible 
digital devices, including continuous glucose monitors and smart 
gastrointestinal capsules (511- 515). Importantly, data collected through 
patient-reported measurements 
or traditional devices, such as digital 
questionnaires answered on portable tablets, is not classified as a digital 
biomarker. In the field of neurology, for example, digital remote monitoring of 
motor symptoms in patients with Parkinsons disease and multiple sclerosis is 
currently under investigation (516, 517). Since the assessment of 
immunotherapy effects in patients with chronic immune -mediated 
neuropathies relies on clinical evaluations 
and self-reported questionnaires, a 
similar development would be highly welcomed.  
 
A new and intriguing classification 
of biomarkers is based on the novelty of 
measurements, and categorizes them into three categories: approved, original, 
and novel (513, 518). An approved biomarker is well-established and improves or 
enhances widely 
recognized medical practices; an original biomarker is a hybrid 


 
 35 
of approved and novel biomarkers, meaning it utilizes approved measurements 
for a novel clinical outcome; a novel biomarker involves a new measurement for a 
new clinical outcome (513). Regardless of their category, both original and novel 
biomarkers must undergo thorough evaluation and testing, as well as robust 
validation processes, before they can be adopted in clinical practice. On the other 
hand, in the 
field of neurology, biomarkers are typically classified according to 
their source of origin, such as myelin biomarkers, axonal damage biomarkers, and 
immunological biomarkers (Figure 6). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Figure 6 . Biomarkers in neurology and to their source of origin . Reproduced from 
Brettschneider J at al. J Neurol. 2009 Jan;256(1):3-12. © Copyright 2009 with permission from 
Springer
 Nature Customer Service Centre. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


 
36 
Biomarkers in Guillain-Barré syndrome  
The absence of ACD is not 
unusual during the first week of disease and does not 
exclude GBS, since 30
–50% of patients in the first week have a normal Q.Alb (18, 
44). A recent study 
of 1231 patients with GBS found that high levels of albumin in 
CSF were associated with the sensorimotor variant with global or proximal-
predominant weakness, later performance of lumbar 
puncture (LP) and CSF 
sampling, demyelinating findings on electro
physiological tests, and a poorer 
short-term outcome 
(within 4 weeks from admission) (18). However, high levels of 
albumin in CSF did not 
predict the outcome beyond week 4 or the risk for needing 
mechanical ventilation (18, 519). The 
specificity of ACD is unknown, as it can been 
found in other diseases 
that mimic GBS and it can be affected by previous 
treatment with IVIg or plasma exchange (PE) (15, 41, 520, 521). Moreover, most 
published studies rely on
 the levels 
of albumin in CSF, although Q.Alb should be 
preferred, since the Q.Alb 
is not influenced by intrathecal protein synthesis, it is 
not affected by changes 
in levels of other proteins in CSF, and most importantly, 
it is corrected for the concentration of albumin in blood (522-525).  
In 
most patients, the 
cell count in 
the CSF is normal, although mild pleocytosis may 
be present in 
some patients with an otherwise typical phenotype (18, 44, 526, 527). 
Pleocytosis of 5 -49 cells/μL has been reported in 15% of patients, while levels 
exceeding 50 cells/μL 
are observed in only 1% (18, 44, 526). Such findings should 
prompt the search for alternative diagnosis.  
IgG and IgM antibodies targeting gangliosides 
can be found in blood of patients 
with GBS, particularly in motor-predominant GBS and 
MFS. Although certain GBS 
features have been associated with specific
 anti-ganglioside antibodies (e.g., anti-
GM1 and anti -GD1a 
antibodies with AMAN, 
and anti-GT1a with the pharyngeal-
cervical-brachial variant), 
their presence only supports the diagnosis, while their 
absence does not exclude GBS (25, 
30, 41, 528-530). One study showed that the 
positivity rate in a 
combinatorial glycoarray increased from 11.8% to 62% when 
ganglioside complexes or gangliosides combined with other glycolipid complexes 
were tested, rather than testing a 
single ganglioside (228). Despite this, the role of 
antibodies against ganglioside complexes remains unclear (26, 531). However, high 
titres of IgG and IgM 
anti-GM1 antibodies at onset, along with persistently high IgG 
anti-GM1 antibody levels at 
3- and 6-month follow up, have been linked to poor 
prognosis in patients with 
GBS (532). If MFS is suspected, testing for anti-GQ1b 
antibodies 
is recommended, as these are 
highly specific for MFS (up to 100%) and 
demonstrate high sensitivity (88-100%) (427, 
533, 534) . In patients with severe 


 
 37 
impairment, respiratory and autonomic involvement, GBS-TRF, and poor response 
to first-line treatment, testing 
for IgG anti-panNF antibodies, as well as anti -CNTN1, 
anti-Caspr1, and anti-NF155 antibodies, should be performed , as this is crucial for 
treatment decision and prognosis (36, 41, 164).  
Myelin biomarkers, including autoantibodies against myelin proteins (e.g., P0, P2, 
PMP22) 
and other lipids and proteins on the myelin sheath , have been 
investigated, but 
none have been found to be useful as diagnostic or prognostic 
biomarkers (232, 233, 235, 239, 240). More recently, an Italian research group 
reported elevated
 levels of sphingomyelin
, a myelin lipid,  in the CSF but not in the 
blood of
 patients with GBS  and demyelinating features on electrophysiological 
tests, proposing sphingomyelin as 
a potential new biomarker for demyelination  
(535, 536). Two other biomarkers of interest, 
which are closely related to myelin 
pathology, 
are neural cell adhesion molecule -1 (NCAM1) and p75 neurotrophin 
receptor (p75). Both are expressed on Schwann cells and their levels in the blood 
have been reported 
to be elevated in patients with GBS compared to those with 
axonal 
neuropathies and healthy controls (537, 538). 
Axonal damage biomarkers 
have also been investigated, with  neurofilament light 
(NfL) isoform being the most 
studied marker of axonal damage. Growing  evidence 
suggests that high levels of NfL in both CSF and blood during the acute phase of 
GBS can  serve as a prognostic biomarker (497, 498, 539- 541). However, the 
diagnostic value is 
limited, particularly in the CSF,  as high levels have been 
reported in a variety
 of neurological disorders (542- 545). Another novel and 
promising biomarker of axonal 
injury is peripherin, which is almost entirely specific 
to the peripheral nerves, 
in contrast to NfL, which is found throughout both the 
PNS and CNS (546). The 
discovery of peripherin has also contributed to a better 
understanding of GBS and 
CIDP pathology, as in the latter, the levels of peripherin 
were not significantly different from those 
of controls. Total tau (T-tau), another 
major axonal cytoskeletal 
protein, is well studied in neurodegenerative CNS 
disorders. However, studies on tau  in 
CSF of patients with GBS have yielded 
inconsistent results (547-550), and tau in blood has not been investigated.  
Studies on immunological biomarkers have 
reported elevated levels of several 
pro-inflammatory chemokines and interleukins 
in CSF, including MCP1 (CCL2), IP10 
(CXCL10), chemokine (C-X
-C motif) ligand-12 (CXCL12, also known as stromal cell-
derived factor-1 (SDF1
)), interleukin-6 (IL6), interleukin-8 (IL8), interleukin-17 (IL17), 
and interleukin-23p19 (IL23p19, 
also known as IL23A), as well as in blood, including 


 
38 
CXCL12b (SDF1b), soluble interleukin-2 receptors (sIL2R), interleukin-9 (IL9), and 
interleukin-17A (IL17A) (450, 454, 458, 551-556).  
Other biomarkers in blood,
 such as low levels of albumin, a small rise in IgG after 
treatment with IVIg, and higher levels of fasting glucose, have been associated with 
a more severe clinical course and a poorer outcome  in patients with GBS (557-
559). These, along 
with other 
laboratory tests – including complete blood count 
with differential white blood 
cell (WBC) counts , electrolytes, creatinine, liver 
enzymes, cobalamin, and thyroid 
hormones, should be conducted
 to rule out other 
causes of flaccid paralysis, such 
as metabolic and electrolyte imbalances (15). In 
addition, lymphocyte-based ratios, such as the neutrophil-to-lymphocyte and 
platelet
-to-lymphocyte ratio,
 have been reported as predictors of short-term 
outcome and 
the risk for requiring mechanical ventilation (560-562). 
 
Several clinical prognostic 
models for 
GBS have been developed. High age, 
antecedent diarrhoea and higher GBS disability score or lower Medical Research 
Council (MRC) sum score 
during the acute phase of GBS were found to be 
associated with poorer outcome
s. The original prognostic tool, the Erasmus GBS 
Outcome Score (EGOS), predicts 
the inability to walk independently at 6 months 
based on age, antecedent 
diarrhoea, and the GBS disability score two weeks after 
hospital admission (499). The second version, the modified Erasmus GBS 
Outcome 
Score (mEGOS), predicts the inability to walk unaided at 4 weeks, 3 
months, and 6 months
, using age, antecedent diarrhoea and MRC sum score at 
hospital admission and on
 day 7 of admission (509). Similarly, a clinical predication 
model for assessing the risk 
of respiratory insufficiency (mechanical ventilation) 
within the first week of hospital admission was developed, named Erasmus GBS 
Respiratory Insufficiency Score (EGRIS). In the EGRIS model , a shorter duration 
(days between onset of 
weakness and admission), a lower MRC sum score, and 
the presence of facial 
weakness/bulbar weakness were associated with a higher 
risk of respiratory insufficiency (563). A simplified version, i.e., the modified EGRIS 
(mEGRIS), which assesses only 3 muscle groups instead of 12, has been developed 
to predict the risk 
of respiratory failure both in the first week and at other time 
points (564).  
The mEGOS and EGRIS models ha
ve been validated in the International GBS 
Outcome Study (IGOS), although 
regional differences have been observed (44, 
565, 566). In addition, a recalibration model for both mEGOS and EGRIS, named 


 
 39 
mEGOS-Eu/NA, and EGRIS-Eu/NA, respectively, improved the predictive accuracy 
for Europe and North America (565, 566).  
 
Biomarkers in chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyradiculopathy 
Similar to GBS, the absence 
of ACD does not rule out the diagnosis, but when 
present, it can support the 
diagnosis of CIDP (57). Normal levels of albumin in CSF, 
however, are 
not uncommon in CIDP and may unnecessarily cast doubt on the 
diagnosis. On the other 
hand, slightly elevated values could lead to a misdiagnosis 
in the absence of supportive 
electrophysiological findings (14). The sensitivity of 
elevated levels of albumin 
in the CSF is hence low to moderate (40-76.9%), and 
the specificity remains unknown 
(66, 469). Findings from an in vitro BNB model 
suggested that patients with 
typical CIDP had more severe BNB damage 
compared to those with CIDP variants (Lewis -Sumner syndrome and distal 
variant) (430). However, no significant 
differences were observed between 
patients with typical CIDP and CIDP variants regarding the levels of albumin in CSF, 
Q.Alb, or the frequency of these laboratory findings 
(372).  
Testing for anti-ganglioside antibodies in blood in 
typical CIDP does not aid in the 
diagnostic process, nor does 
it influence treatment decision or prognosis (57, 
567). However, certain 
phenotypes should prompt clinicians to test for specific 
antibodies, as this may 
have implications for  treatment and prognosis (567). 
Disialosyl antibodies (GD1b, GD3, GT1b, and GQ1b), which can be associated with 
IgM M-protein and 
CANOMAD or CANDA syndromes, should  be tested in cases of 
sensory
-ataxic phenotype (568
-571). Additionally, IgM anti-GM1 antibodies can 
be detected in a 
subset of patients with multifocal CIDP  (Lewis-Sumner 
syndrome) (66, 68, 572). Furthermore, patients with  a predominantly distal 
phenotype, with or 
without IgM M -protein, should be tested for anti -MAG 
antibodies, as this 
can impact 
treatment decisions (119, 126, 369, 573). In case of 
otherwise typical 
CIDP that does not respond to first -line treatment, testing for 
IgG nodal-paranodal antibodies , should 
also be considered, and if positive, 
treatment 
should be adjusted 
accordingly (36, 57, 164, 567). If the initial test is 
negative but there is strong clinical suspicion of autoimmune nodopathy, 
repeated testing should be conducted.  
Myelin biomarkers, such as autoantibodies against myelin proteins (P0, P2, 
PMP22), have been investigated but have not demonstrated significant clinical 


 
40 
utility, similar to the scenario in GBS  (235, 238, 239, 282). The previously 
mentioned study on sphingomyelin
 reported significantly 
higher levels of 
sphingomyelin in active 
CIDP compared to stable CIDP, which, if validated, could 
have implications for
 disease monitoring (536). Also noteworthy are the 
biomarkers of myelinating 
Schwann cells, NCAM1 and p75, previously discussed in 
the context of GBS, as 
well as  the novel transmembrane protease serine- 5 
(TMPRSS5). Elevated levels of 
NCAM1 and 
p75 have been observed in the blood of 
patients with CIDP, compared to individuals with axonal neuropathies and healthy 
controls. Notably, while the levels of both NCAM1 and p75 are elevated in CIDP, 
only NCAM1 is elevated 
in patients with the hereditary demyelinating neuropathy 
Charcot-Marie-Tooth 1A (CMT1A). This distinction warrants further investigation, 
as it may offe
r a valuable tool for differentiating CIDP from late-onset CMT (537, 
538). TMPRSS5 has been reported 
as elevated in CMT1A, but its role as a biomarker 
for immune-mediated neuropathies has not been investigated 
(574).  
Among axonal biomarkers in CIDP
, NfL is the most extensively studied, with a 
rapidly increasing body of 
research published in recent years. While its diagnostic 
utility is low, there is 
evidence suggesting that levels of NfL in blood could serve 
as a marker 
for the 
short-term disease activity and treatment response, as levels 
have 
been shown to decrease in patients who respond to therapy (503, 504, 539, 
575, 576). However, longitudinal studies 
involving repeated paired sampling over 
an extended period are lacking.  
Research on immunological biomarkers has yielded inconsistent results and 
detecting cytokines and chemokines 
as diagnostic and/or prognostic markers 
presents challenges due to the wide variety of mediators and pathways, their low 
concentrations, the highly dynamic  nature of their secretion , and the complex, 
multifaceted 
nature of CIDP as a disease. However, a recent systematic review has 
suggested that some cytokines and 
chemokines may play a role in the 
pathogenesis of CIDP, potentially 
serving as indirect diagnostic biomarkers (577). 
The most consistent findings were elevated levels of IL6, IL17, CXCL10 (IP10), and 
TNFα	in CSF and 
blood of patients with CIDP compared to controls (287, 293, 436, 
450, 456, 551, 578 -583). Of interest, levels of B -cell activating factor (BAFF), a 
potent activator crucial for 
B cells proliferation and differentiation, are elevated in 
blood of patients with 
CIDP regardless of disease activity stage, i.e., no difference 
was observed between treatment-naïve patients, stable patients 
on maintenance 
therapy,
 and those in remission after treatment  discontinuation (305). 


 
 41 
Prognostic tools that combine multiple factors, such as EGOS and EGRIS for GBS, 
are lacking for CIDP. However, when evaluating the effect of treatment, well -
established disability scales — such as the Inflammatory Rasch -built Overall 
Disability Scale (I-RODS) and 
the Inflammatory Neuropathy Cause and Treatment 
(INCAT) scale, as well 
as impairment measures – like the Medical Research Council 
(MRC) scale and grip 
strength measurement using dynamometry – can support 
the objective evaluation of treatment response (57, 584-587).
 
 
Biomarkers in multifocal motor neuropathy 
Approximately 30% of patients show 
a mildly elevated levels of albumin level in 
the CSF (115). ACD is not mandatory for diagnosis but can serve as a supportive 
finding (97, 98). The absence 
of elevated levels of albumin in CSF is likely due to 
the predominantly distal distribution 
of peripheral nerve pathology in MMN, which 
is anatomically distant from 
the CSF compartment.  While elevated levels of 
albumin in CSF, an 
indicator of BNB dysfunction, are not commonly seen, strong 
evidence suggests that 
sera from patients with 
MMN disrupt the barrier , 
regardless of IgM anti-GM1 antibody status (431).  
High 
titres of IgM anti
-GM1 antibodies are associated with MMN and can support 
the diagnosis; however, more than 
half of patients are seronegative (98, 110, 112). 
Galactocerebroside (GalC) enhances the binding 
of anti -GM1 antibodies, and 
studies have shown increased 
detection rates of IgM anti-GM1 antibodies when 
both anti-GM1 and 
anti-GM1:GalC antibodies are tested (111, 588) . Patients with 
MMN regardless of the presence 
of anti-GM1 antibodies exhibit similar clinical 
features. Moreover, 
low titres 
do not rule out  the mimics, such as motor -
predominant multifocal CIDP or motor 
neuron disease (69, 589, 590). Other anti-
ganglioside antibodies have been reported in 
patients with MMN, but they lack 
diagnostic utility (591-593)
.  
Myelin and Schwann 
cell antibodies and markers have not been studied in patients 
with MMN. 
In 2018, levels 
of NfL 
in blood, a biomarker of axonal damage, were reported to be 
lower in three patients with MMN compared to patients with GBS and CIDP (539). 
A recently published study suggested that 
NfL levels in blood could serve as a 
diagnostic marker to differentiate 
between MMN and early amyotrophic lateral 
sclerosis with predominant lower motor neuro involvement (594).  


 
42 
Small-sample studies have provided limited evidence of immunological biomarker 
alterations 
in patients with MMN (577). Levels 
of interleukin-1 receptor antagonist 
(IL1Ra), an anti
-inflammatory cytokine with potential regulatory function, is 
reported to be elevated in both blood and CSF (595, 596). TNF α is reported to be 
elevated in blood but 
not CSF (595-597). Levels of TNFα in blood are elevated in 
patients with electrophysiological 
features of 
focal demyelination, although this 
does not correlate with clinical impairment as measured by the MRC sum score 
(595). On other hand, levels of 
TNFα in blood increase after each IVIg treatment, 
alongside clinical improvement, and 
return to baseline levels after 3 -4 weeks in 
parallel with clinical deterioration 
(597). The relevance of these finding remains 
unclear. Results on levels of 
several other cytokines, such as interleukin-2 (IL2) and 
CXCL10 (IP10) are conflicting (595, 
598). Interestingly, an in vitro BNB model 
demonstrated increased autocrine secretion of 
CXCL10 (IP10) induced by sera 
from patients with MMN (432). In addition, in the same study, significantly higher 
levels of CXCL10 were observed in blood of patients with conduction block when 
compared to those without (432).  
Currently, there are no available 
biomarkers to determine disease activity, 
treatment response, or prognosis in patients with MMN. Similar to CIDP, disability 
and impairment scales are used to objectively assess treatment effects  (599, 
600). Furthermore, a disease-specific 
RODS scale for MMN  (RODS-MMN©) has 
been developed to provide more tailored assessment (601).  
 
Biomarkers in IgM paraproteinemic demyelinating neuropathy 
Elevated
 levels of albumin in CSF and ACD are reported in the majority of patients 
with PDN 
(66.7-86%), with levels typically similar to those seen in CIDP (118). Similar 
to 
GBS and CIDP, 
the specificity is unknown and elevated levels are not required 
for the diagnosis, but they can support the diagnosis of PDN (118).  
  
Paraprotein (M-protein) detection and quantification should be performed using 
both serum and urine 
electrophoresis, including immunofixation to increase 
sensitivity.(122, 124, 133, 602). As mentioned in Section 1.2 on the 
immunopathogenesis of PDN, up to 5% of patients exhibit clinical and 
electrophysiological features of PDN, but IgM paraprotein may not be detectable 
until 3- 4 years after disease onset (368-370). Therefore, the absence of the 
paraprotein 
does not necessarily rule out the diagnosis, especially when the 


 
 43 
clinical presentation is characteristic and antibodies against myelin-associated 
glycoprotein (MAG) are present (364, 370)
. Additionally, levels of free light chain s 
and their ratio should 
be measured (122, 124, 603). Moreover, an early decision to 
refer a patient with paraproteinemia to a haematologist should be considered, as 
early specialist evaluation can 
help identify underlying haematological conditions 
and guide appropriate management (118, 124, 
604-606). Regular monitoring of 
paraproteinemia levels is mandatory, 
as patients with IgM M -protein are at 
increased risk of malignant 
transformation (607). IgM paraproteinemia can be 
associated with other neuropathies, syndromes 
and conditions, making a careful 
investigation of the clinical phenotype and electrophysiological tests crucial (118, 
124, 608-610).  
Antibodies against myelin-associated 
glycoprotein (MAG) are detected in 56-
83% of patients with PDN 
(126, 127).  IgM anti- MAG antibodies  are typically 
analysed using an enzyme-linked 
immunosorbent assay (ELISA) from Bühlmann 
Laboratory AG, Schönenbuch, Switzerland, 
with test 
results expressed in 
Bühlmann titre units (BTU) (611)
. Although 1000 BTU is reported as the threshold 
for a positive test, values between 
1000-7000 BTU should be regarded as weakly 
positive and interpretated with 
caution due to the risk for false positivity (124, 605, 
612). This is attributed to the lower specificity of ELISA at low titres, which may be 
caused by cross-reactivity 
with GM1 and disialosyl gangliosides (84, 613). A more 
optimal balance of sensitivity 
and specificity is achieved with a cut -off of 1500 
BTU, while the highest 
specificity is observed with a cut-off above 7000 BTU (118, 
120, 124, 612). A 
recent retrospective analysis of forty studies, including 303 
patients with PDN, found that a relative reduction of more than 50% in IgM anti-
MAG titre units and/or M -protein 
levels compared to pretreatment values was 
associated with clinical improvements (614). 
Additionally, non- responders 
showed no or minimal change, 
while worsening was associated with an increase in 
antibody titres (614). If negative, testing for anti -glycolipid sulfoglucuronyl 
paragloboside (SGPG), anti-ganglioside antibodies, 
and anti-sulfatide antibodies 
can 
be considered because 
a positive result could imply the association between 
M-protein and the neuropathy (118, 127, 605, 615-617).  
While axonal damage markers, such 
as NfL, have been extensively studied in 
various neurological disease s, findings 
on the levels of NfL  in blood  remain 
conflicting, and no 
data are currently 
available on the levels of NfL in CSF of 
patients with PDN (539, 618). 


 
44 
Immunological biomarkers have been investigated to some extent in patients with 
PDN. The most consisting finding is elevated levels of interleukin -10 (IL10) and 
interleukin-6 (IL6) cytokines , which stimulate B cells and antibody production  
(578, 619). Despite evidence of
 B-cell activation in patients with PDN, levels of B-
cell activating factor (BAFF) are 
similar to those in healthy individuals (619, 620). 
Therefore, BAFF does not appear to be a useful marker of disease activity in PDN. 
The relevance of 
mutation in 
the myeloid differentiation factor 88 (MYD88) gene 
and its predictive value in assessing response to treatment with Bruton's tyrosine 
kinase (BTK) inhibitors is still to be elucidated (500); se e further discussion in 
Section 1.5 on the treatment of immune-mediated neuropathies (page 48). 
 
Biomarkers in autoimmune nodopathy  
Similar to other immune-mediated neuropathies, 
the absence of the ACD does 
not rule out diagnosis and 
the levels of albumin in CSF can be normal early in the 
disease. However, levels of albumin usually increase during the disease and, in 
some cases, can be very high (36, 164, 169, 567).  
IgG antibodies against nodal and paranodal proteins (contactin -1, contactin-
associated protein -1, neurofascin- 155, 
and neurofascin -186) in blood are 
diagnostic of autoimmune nodopathy in 
the appropriate clinical context and when 
analysed using reliable laboratory techniques .
 While these antibodies  are 
predominantly of the IgG4 subclass 
during the chronic phase, IgG1-3 may also be 
detected during the early phase 
and in some patients with anti-pan-neurofascin 
autoimmune nodopathy (29, 35, 36, 166, 
168, 169, 567, 621). According to the 2021 
EAN/PNS Guidelines, 
screening should be performed using  a cell-based assay 
(CBA), 
along with a confirmatory 
test, either enzyme-linked immunosorbent 
assays (ELISA) or teased- nerve fibre assays  (57). For antibody titration and 
subclass identification, both ELISA 
and CBA have been used  (567, 622, 623)   
However, a recently 
published study 
showed that adding a second test did not 
always guarantee additional benefits over using 
the most accurate test alone  
(622). Although there is 
no gold standard yet, other techniques should be avoided 
or compared to 
one of these (169, 567, 622). An important question is when these 
antibodies should be tested, 
and there is no straightforward answer. While it may 
be tempting to test for nodal -paranodal antibodies in all patients with a 
phenotype resembling GBS and CIDP, this approach is not practical. However, 


 
 45 
these antibodies should be tested in cases of GBS with an aggressive clinical 
course, including severe tetraplegia, neuromuscular respiratory insufficiency, and 
autonomic instability. Patients who meet the 
2021 criteria for demyelination and 
present with clinical features not 
commonly seen in typical CIDP or its variants, 
such as cranial nerve 
involvement, severe ataxia, high-amplitude tremor, younger 
age (late childhood  and early 
adulthood), nephrotic syndrome, subacute or 
aggressive 
disease progression, neuromuscular respiratory insufficiency, and 
especially those who do not respond to first-line treatment, should also be testes 
(41, 57, 169)
. Furthermore, repeated testing should be performed if the initial test 
is negative but there is strong clinical suspicion of autoimmune nodopathy (621). 
Likewise, repeated testing is 
recommended during the course of the disease, as 
several studies have shown that antibody titre decrease and, in some cases,  
antibodies disappear, in parallel 
with clinical improvement (36, 166, 169, 567, 621, 
623, 624). Levels of IgG nodal-paranodal antibodies may also be used to monitor 
disease recurrence and optimize the treatment (169).  
In the context of the
 pathology of autoimmune nodopathy with mild or absent 
myelin disruption (624, 625), myelin biomarkers are likely of limited clinical 
significance, although their exploration remains important. In contrast, Schwann 
cell markers, such as the previously mentioned NCAM1, p75, and TMPRSS5 may be 
more relevant for investigation
, as o ne study demonstrated the detachment of 
Schwann cell paranodal loops from the axon and the loss of Schwann cell nodal 
microvilli (626).   
Axonal loss and destruction, 
though occurring to varying degrees, explain the 
elevated levels of 
NfL in CSF and blood during the acute phase of the disease (169, 
624). Similar to the 
levels of antibodies against nodal -paranodal proteins, the 
levels of NfL 
tends to decrease and even normalise in parallel with clinical 
improvement, making both NfL in blood and antibodies against nodal-paranodal 
proteins valuable markers for monitoring disease activity (36, 164, 166, 169)
.  
 
 
 
 
 


 
46 
1.5 TREATMENT OF IMMUNE-MEDI ATED NEUROPATHIES  
Immunotherapy is the main treatment for immune- mediated neuropathies (1). 
However, current treatments are often 
unspecific due to the limited 
understanding of the exact immunopathogenesis underlying these conditions. As 
a result, current immunotherapy options may not fully address the complex 
mechanisms driving neuroinflammation in the PNS , leading to suboptimal 
outcomes for some patients.  
There are only two treatment 
options for GBS, namely intravenous 
immunoglobulins (IVIg) or plasma exchange (PE), which are equally effective but 
with several considerable drawbacks, including high cost, limited availability, and 
a lack of specificity, as both treatments target immune mechanisms with out 
addressing the underlying pathophysiological processes of the disease (15, 41, 
627). GBS is a potentially fatal disease with 20 to 30% 
 of patients requir ing 
intensive care due to autonomic 
and/or respiratory failure, despite the treatment 
(15, 40). Moreover, more than 
half of these patients are unable to walk unassisted 
one year later, and many patients have residual signs and symptoms, physically 
disabling sequelae, secondary medical complications, and reduced quality of life 
(628-631). A trial evaluating a second dose of IVIg showed no improvement in 
outcomes, while also revealing 
an increased risk of thromboembolic events (632). 
While there was some indication of a positive effect of eculizumab, a humanised 
monoclonal antibody targeting the complement 
protein C5, the phase 3 trial did 
not demonstrate  any significant impact
 on outcome (633- 635). Preliminary 
results from a randomized, 
double-blind, placebo- controlled phase 3 study 
(ClinicalTrials.gov 
NCT04701164) of ANX005, a C1q blocking antibody that inhibits 
the activation of the classical complement pathway, are promising.  
The first- line treatment for 
typical CIDP and its variants consists of IVIg or 
corticosteroids (oral 
prednisolone or 
intravenous methylprednisolone, IVMp) (57, 
636-639). Some CIDP variants, such as multifocal or motor-dominant CIDP may 
respond better to 
IVIg than corticosteroids or may even worsen with 
corticosteroids (640, 641). If patient respond well to IVIg, subcutaneous 
immunoglobulin (SCIg) could be used for maintenance treatment instead of IVIg 
(642
-646). However, there are several notable drawbacks to these treatments, 
including the numerous side 
effects of long-term steroids use, as well as  the high 
cost and limited availability 
of immunoglobulins. Furthermore, not all patients with 
CIDP (approximately 70 -75%) respond 
to first -line treatment options (647). 


 
 47 
Among those who initially respond to first- line treatments, 20- 25% eventually 
become treatment-refractory (648). Early 
and effective immuno therapy is crucial 
in preventing axonal degeneration and long-term disability (648, 649). PE can be 
highly effective, especially 
in patients with CIDP experiencing severe relapses, but 
it requires good vascular access and specialized equipment (300, 636, 650, 651). 
A combination of 
IVIg and 
IVMp is a choice if monotherapy with either IVI g or 
corticosteroids is insufficient (652). Azathioprine, 
mycophenolate mofetil, or 
cyclosporine may be considered as steroid- or immunoglobulin-sparing agents in 
patients with CIDP undergoing 
maintenance treatment with either 
immunoglobulins or corticosteroids (57, 653-657). Cyclophosphamide may be 
considered for 
patients who do not respond adequately to first-line treatments 
and is typically reserved 
for those with severe or treatment -resistant disease 
(658-660). Hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) is considered a 
treatment of last recourse for patients with severe CIDP who have not responded 
to conventional therapies (661- 664). Emerging therapies for CIDP,  which are 
currently being evaluated in 
clinical trials, include neonatal Fc receptor (FcRn) 
antagonists and complement inhibitors  (ClinicalTrials.gov 
NCT04281472, 
NCT04280718. NCT06637072, NCT03861481, NCT06290128, 
NCT06290141). 
Although a phase 2 trial of the FcRn antagonist efgartigimod demonstrated a 
reduction in relapse rate in CIDP compared to placebo, a recently published case 
series of efgartigimod in a real-world setting has raised some questions about its 
efficacy
 (665-668). Additionally, 
a trial of rozanolixizumab, another FcRn 
antagonist, failed to show 
efficacy in patients with CIDP (669). While preliminary 
reports from a phase 2 trial on the effects of riliprubart, a humanized anti-C1s IgG4 
monoclonal antibody inhibiting the 
classical complement pathway, are promising 
(PNS Abstracts 2024 doi.org/10.1111/jns.12648), two 
phase 3 trials are still ongoing 
(ClinicalTrials.gov NCT06290128, NCT06290141). Of note, recent studies on 
the 
use of chimeric 
antigen receptor  T cell (CAR-T) therapy in systemic lupus 
erythematosus and myasthenia gravis 
(670, 671), along with a case report of a 
patient with therapy -refractory CIDP who responded well to BCMA -CD19 
bispecific CAR-T 
treatment, are contributing to the development of more tailored 
therapy options for CIDP (672). 
A large 
number of immunotherapy 
treatments have been investigated for patients 
with MMN, but only IVIg 
has been shown to be effective (100, 115, 673, 674). Both 
IVIg and SCIg are effective in maintaining clinical stability in patients with MMN 
(599, 675-677). Corticosteroids and PE offer no benefit and, in some cases, may 


 
48 
even worsen the condition (678-680). In patients who are refractory to IVIg and 
experience progressive worsening, 
treatment with cyclophosphamide or 
rituximab may be considered, but is usually not  effective (681-685). There is 
indication of a potential 
positive effect of eculizumab in MMN, but this needs to 
be validated in a 
randomized controlled trial (686). Preliminary data from a phase 
2 trial (ClinicalTrials.gov NCT05225675) of empasiprubart, a monoclonal antibody 
that inhibits complement factor 2, are encouraging (PNS Abstracts 2024 
doi.org/10.1111/jns.12648).  
Patients with IgM PDN , with or 
without anti -MAG antibodies, who do not 
experience gait difficulties or 
have mild, stable disease without significant 
disability receive supportive care, such as exercise and balance training, along 
with regular haematological evaluations for early detection of malignant 
transformation of MGUS (84, 118, 119). In cases where disability affects daily 
activities, treatment is considered, although, there is no 
 consensus on the choice 
of first- line immunotherapy, and many  different immunotherapies have been 
suggested (573, 
687). Nonetheless, some experts recommend rituximab as the 
treatment of choice, as 
cumulative data from several uncontrolled studies 
demonstrated benefits for 30- 67% of patients with PDN (119, 122, 124, 127, 573, 
687-693). It has 
been suggested that initiating rituximab treatment early, before 
axonal degeneration occurs, could 
improve long-term prognosis and functional 
outcomes by potentially 
altering the disease course and minimizing irreversible 
nerve damage (127, 694). However, it has also been reported that rituximab may, 
paradoxically, exacerbate the neuropathy 
in some patients due to the IgM flare 
phenomenon. This occurs as a result of B cell lysis, which can lead to the release 
of intracellular paraprotein or an increase in cytokine production, potentially 
worsening the clinical symptoms. This worsening is rare and, in most cases, can be 
reversed with IVIg or PE (119, 695). While the benefits of IVIg and PE remain 
unproven or minimal, they 
may offer short-term effects and, in some patients, a 
good response (119, 
364, 695-698). Corticosteroids alone are rarely effective, but 
they may provide some benefits when used in combination with 
cyclophosphamide (119, 124, 129, 131, 697, 699, 700). As mentioned, 60 -70% of 
patients with PDN and 
anti-MAG antibodies have a somatic point mutation in the 
myeloid differentiation factor 88 
(MYD88) gene, which promotes cellular 
proliferation by activating Bruton's tyrosine kinase (BTK) (379, 380). Ibrutinib, a 
BTK inhibitor, has demonstrated 
efficacy in PDN, although this was primarily 
limited to a subgroup 
of patients with the MYD88 mutation and those with  


 
 49 
neuropathy associated with Waldenström's macroglobulinaemia (380, 500, 701, 
702)
. Phase 2 clinical trials of second -generation BTK inhibitors , acalabrutinib 
(ClinicalTrials.gov NCT05065554) 
and zanubrutinib (ClinicalTrials.gov 
NCT05939037), are currently ongoing.  
The majority of 
patients with autoimmune nodopathy
 have antibodies of the 
IgG4 subclass, which do 
not exert pathogenicity through inflammatory-mediated 
processes, such as activation of the 
complement system and cellular immune 
response, in contrast to their IgG1 counterparts (36, 703). Instead, IgG4 antibodies 
interfere with protein-protein interactions 
at the node of Ranvier, affecting cell 
adhesion or signal transduction 
pathways. This distinction has a significant impact 
on the choice of immunotherapy (169, 623). Patients with autoimmune nodopathy 
typically 
show a poor or 
limited response to first-line therapies (IVIg, steroids, and 
PE), but 
most patients 
respond well to B -cell depletion therapies like rituximab 
(169, 703). However, there is no 
consensus nor clear recommendation on therapy 
protocol or dosage, and different treatment regimens have been reported (151, 164, 
621, 704- 706). Other 
therapies, such as cyclophosphamide, azathioprine, 
daratumumab, bortezomib, and autologous hematopoietic 
stem cell 
transplantation have been used in a limited number of patients and with variable 
results (148, 149, 165, 707).  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


 
50 
1.6 ANIMAL MODEL OF GUILLAIN-BARRÉ SYNDROM E 
There is an increasing demand for new therapies for immune -mediated 
neuropathies, with animal models frequently 
used to evaluate the potential 
efficacy and mechanisms of drug action (708). Experimental autoimmune neuritis 
(EAN) is a 
widely used animal model for studying immune-mediated neuropathies. 
In EAN, susceptible animals
, such as rats and mice , are immunized with various 
antigens, triggering an autoimmune response 
that targets the PNS. This results in 
inflammation, demyelination, and/or axonal damage, providing valuable insights 
into the mechanisms underlying diseases like GBS (446).  
Demyelinating EAN is induced by 
immunization with proteins related to PNS 
myelin, while ataxic or axonal EAN models are induced by injecting gangliosides 
(446, 709). However, all models 
are monophasic and share common stages, 
including the pre-symptomatic phase, progression, peak, and recovery, with some 
animals left with sequela (446). An animal model of chronic autoimmune 
polyneuropathy, resembling CIDP, can 
be induced by administering 
immunosuppressants like cyclosporine A to animals with EAN or through genetic 
modification of the nonobese diabetic (NOD) mouse strain, resulting in a 
deficiency of B7-2  (CD86) co-stimulation molecules, which triggers spontaneous 
chronic autoimmune neuropathy 
(710-712).  
Demyelinating EAN can 
be induced 
using various combinations of antigens and 
animal species (713-717). The most commonly used antigens are P0 and P2, which 
have a high 
induction rate of EAN, with Lewis rats being the most commonly used 
animals due to their susceptibility and higher reliability compared to mice (715, 
716, 718, 719) . EAN can 
also be induced by immunisation with peptides derived 
from the P2 protein, such as P253-78, P257-81, and P2 61-72 (717, 720, 721). ). In acute 
demyelinating EAN, the first symptom is flaccid palsy of the tail that usually 
develops 10- 14 days post- immunization (p.i.). This weakness ascends, 
 and 
progresses rapidly to the hind limbs and, in some severe cases, even to the 
forelimbs. After several days, usually 20-22 days p.i., paralysis starts to ameliorate 
spontaneously, and animals finally recover with various motor residues.  
Following immunization, phagocytes at the injection site capture and process the 
antigen. The processed 
antigens are 
then presented by antigen-presenting cells 
(APCs) in the local draining lymph 
nodes, triggering the clonal expansion and 
activation of CD4+ T 
cells (446). These activated T cells migrate throughout the 
body in search of their target antigen, which, in this case, is located in the 


 
 51 
peripheral nervous system (PNS). Upon encountering the antigen, the T cells 
release cytokines and chemokines that recruit additional inflammatory cells from 
the bloodstream, leading to their infiltration of the nerve roots and peripheral 
nerves (722, 723). Strong evidence suggests that interferon-gamma plays a crucial 
role in the development 
of EAN, alongside other pro-inflammatory Th1 cytokines 
such as TNFa, IL1, IL6, 
and IL17 (724-726). Simultaneously, the blood-nerve barrier 
(BNB) breaks 
down as 
the endothelial cells of endoneurial microvessels lose their 
tight junctions, become activated, and express cell adhesion molecules that 
facilitate the migration of immune cells into the PNS  (420, 421, 727, 728). The 
majority of infiltrating lymphocytes 
consists of CD4+ Th1 cells, crucial for triggering 
antigen-specific autoimmunity, followed 
by cytotoxic CD8+ T cells and NK cells 
(729-732). Macrophages 
are recruited by CD4+ T cells and act as effector cells 
causing demyelination and tissue 
damage (733, 734). Paranodal disruption at the 
NoR, where myelin sheet is tightly bound to the axon, is one of the earliest 
pathological events observed (735). Interestingly, early complement deposition 
on Schwann cells and myelin sheaths prior to overt demyelination has also been 
observed, suggesting that complement activation contributes to the onset of 
immune-mediated myelin damage (736). During 
 the recovery phase, a clonal shift  
from Th1 to Th2 occurs 
with IL10 acting as an anti- inflammatory cytokine that 
suppresses scavenging macrophages, 
cytotoxic T lymphocytes, and NK cells,
