while regulatory T cells increase in the PNS and M2-differentiated macrophages, 
which have a suppressive 
role, clear myelin debris, promoting Schwann cell-
mediated remyelination and 
regeneration (446, 737-739). The 
neurophysiological 
findings are consistent 
with demyelination, including conduction blocks, slowing 
of nerve conduction velocity, and prolonged distal motor latencies and F- wave 
latencies (740-742) 
Axonal 
EAN can be induced 
by inoculating a ganglioside mixture, including GM1, 
GD1a, 
GD1b, and GT1b, or by using purified gangliosides, such as GD1b , in 
susceptible animal s, such as the Japanese white rabbit , doubly genetically 
modified GD3s-/-/CFP mouse, or a BALB/c mouse  (218, 222, 743-745). Similar to 
demyelinating EAN, this model 
is also characterized by an acute onset of 
ascending flaccid limb paralysis and follows a monophasic course. Pathological 
features resemble those of 
human AMAN, with predominantly axonal involvement, 
characterized by Wallerian degeneration, macrophages infiltration in the 
periaxonal space of myelinated internodes, minimal or no demyelination, and an 
absence of lymphocyte infiltration (744, 746, 747). Moreover, high titres of IgG 


 
52 
anti-gangliosides antibodies were observed  in the plasma, along with IgG and 
complement deposition at the NoR 
and disruption of nodal Na+ channel clusters, 
paranodal axoglial junctions, and Schwann cell microvilli (218, 222, 709, 744, 747). 
In the acute phase, neurophysiological studies demonstrated an absence of F -
waves and normal motor nerve 
conduction velocity (222, 747). However, in the 
recovery phase, F-waves reappeared, while compound muscle action potential 
amplitudes decreased and electromyography revealed
 denervation potentials, 
suggesting motor nerve conduction 
failure at the nerve root level during the acute 
phase, followed by
 Wallerian axonal degeneration extending distally in the later 
stages of the disease (222, 
747). Furthermore, this model was used to establish 
molecular mimicry between microbes [C.jejuni lipo
-oligosaccharides (LOS)] and 
autoantigens in GBS by actively 
immunizing rabbits with GM1 or C.jejuni LOS (744, 
748). 
 
 
 
 
 
 


 
 53 
2 Research aims 
The overall aim of this thesis was to explore novel diagnostic and prognostic 
biomarkers and to gain insights into the underlying pathophysiological 
mechanisms of various immune- mediated neuropathies, as well as to 
investigate 
the effects of imatinib
 on experimental autoimmune neuritis (EAN), 
an animal model of Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS)
. 
The specific aims were: 
Study I: to investigate the diagnostic and prognostic value of 
axonal damage biomarkers , neurofilament 
light (NfL) chain and 
total 
tau (T- tau), in GBS, chronic inflammatory demyelinating 
polyradiculoneuropathy (CIDP), multifocal motor 
neuropathy 
(MMN), and paraproteinemic demyelinating neuropathy (PDN). 
Study II: to
 identify novel diagnostic biomarkers using large-scale 
multiplex proteomics (Olink) and to further 
investigate and 
validate the finding of elevated interleukin-8 (IL8) levels in patients 
with GBS, CIDP, MMN, and PDN. 
Study 
III: to investigate the clinical 
value of beta -trace protein 
(BTP), the second 
most abundant protein in CSF after albumin, in 
both blood and CSF of patients 
with GBS and CIDP, and to evaluate 
its potential role as a surrogate 
marker of blood-CSF barrier 
damage. 
Study 
IV: to explore 
the potential therapeutic benefits of imatinib, 
a small tyrosine kinase 
inhibitor, on blood -nerve barrier (BNB) 
integrity and immune cell 
recruitment in an animal model of GBS, 
and to 
lay the groundwork for a clinical trial of imatinib in patients 
with GBS. 
 




 
 55 
3 Materials and methods 
3.1 MATERIALS 
Study I-III 
Study I- III are retrospective. 
All patients and controls, except for the healthy 
controls (HC), were recruited 
at the Department of Neurology, Karolinska 
University Hospital, Stockholm, Sweden, between 2003 and 2017.  
The patient group consisted of 
adult patients with acute and chronic immune-
mediated polyneuropathies, including GBS, CIDP, PDN, and MMN. The diagnosis of 
GBS was based on the Asbury criteria, Brighton criteria (level 1 or level 2 of 
diagnostic certainty), as well as the 2023 guidelines (41, 749, 750). The diagnosis 
of CIDP was based on 2010 European Federation of Neurological 
Societies/Peripheral Nerve Society (EFNS/PNS) and/or 2021 European Academy 
of Neurology/Peripheral Nerve 
Society (EAN/PNS)  G uidelines (57, 751). The 
diagnosis of MMN 
and PDN were based on the EFNS/PNS criteria from 2010 (97, 
118). The CSF and blood samples included those collected during the diagnostic 
work-up and/or prior to treatment (pre- treatment), as well as those collected 
during the convalescence phase and after treatment (post-treatment).  
The control groups consisted 
of disease-free controls and disease controls. The 
latter group included patients 
with non- inflammatory polyneuropathies (NIP ), 
amyotrophic lateral sclerosis 
(ALS), and multiple sclerosis (MS). The disease-free 
controls consisted of a
 combination of 
healthy controls (HC) and patients with 
benign headaches (HA) or only HC. The HC group consisted of healthy volunteers 
recruited at the Department 
of Neurology, University Hospital of Umeå, Sweden. 
The HA group consisted of 
patients with sudden -onset headaches who had 
undergone diagnostic lumbar puncture 
(LP) to rule out subarachnoid 
haemorrhage and showed no pathology in blood, CSF, brain imaging, or at follow-
up. The NIP cohort comprised chronic 
idiopathic axonal polyneuropathies, 
hereditary demyelinating and axonal neuropathies, and diabetic axonal 
neuropathies. The diagnosis of ALS and MS was based on well- recognized 
international criteria (752, 753). The patients 
with ALS exhibited signs of both 
upper and lower motor neuron involvement. All CSF and blood samples from 
disease controls, i.e. NIP, ALS and MS, were collected at the time of 
 diagnosis, and 
for patients with MS, prior to the initiation of immunomodulatory treatment. 


 
56 
Materials used across studies are summarised in Table 1 and 2. Note that there 
was some overlap of patients across the subgroups in the Study I – III.

neuropathy. HC, healthy controls. HA, headaches. NIP, non-inflammatory polyneuropathies. MS, multiple 
scle
rosis. ALS, amyotrophic lateral sclerosis. NA, not applicable.  
 
At the time of sampling, CSF and blood were centrifuged at 2000 g for 10 minutes. 
Four hundred microliters 
(400 μL) of each sample were initially aliquoted into 
glass tubes and 
stored at −80°C in the laboratory of the Department of Neurology, 
Karolinska University Hospital. All samples were thawed once and subsequently 
aliquoted into polypropylene tubes before analysis. 


 
 57 
Clinical data and demographics were retrieved from the medical chart e-health 
system TakeCare and the national 
Swedish Neuro Registries 
(https://www.neuroreg.se). Patients with GBS were assessed using the GBS 
disability 
scale (754, 755) at 
the time of the first physician examination, at nadir, 
and 3 months (±1 
week), 6 
months (±2 weeks), and 12 months (±4 weeks) after 
disease onset. Patients with chronic immune-mediated neuropathies, i.e., CIDP, 
MMN and PDN,  were 
assessed with the Inflammatory Neuropathy Cause and 
Treatment (INCAT) scale (756) at 
the abovementioned time points at admission 
to 12 months, and 
additionally at 18 months (±4 weeks), 24 months (±4 weeks), 5 
years (±2 months), and 8– 10 years post- onset (the latter group combined). In 
Study I, patients with 
MMN were assessed in a subanalysis using only INCAT-arm 
score (0-5 points).The 
scoring was done retrospectively by IK using data from 
medical charts and Swedish 
Neuro Registries when scoring was unavailable in the 
medical charts.  
 
Study IV 
Study IV utilized EAN, an animal 
model of GBS. We induced the EAN by inoculating 
200 µg of peripheral myelin 
protein P253-78, which has been shown to induce acute 
inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy in this animal model (709, 715, 720). 
Ten weeks-old male 
and female Lewis rats , a strain previously shown to be 
susceptible to EAN (446), were immunized via subcutaneous injection at the tail 
base with the
 P253-78 peptide in complete Freund’s adjuvant The incidence rate 
was 100%. Both sexes exhibited a monophasic disease course with distinct 
progression, peak, and recovery 
phases. The first symptom was flaccid tail palsy, 
which 
developed 7
-8 days post-immunization (p.i.). Weakness ascended rapidly, 
progressing to the hind limbs  and, 
in severe cases, even to the forelimbs. After 
several days, typically around 18
–19 days p.i., paralysis began to improve, and the 
animals eventually recovered, although some exhibited residual motor deficits.  
The rats 
were weighed and 
assessed daily for disease severity. The disease score 
was determined using a 
well-established 10 -point clinical symptom scoring 
system as follows: 0 
= normal; 1 = less lively; 2 = impaired righting/limb/tail; 3 = 
absent righting; 4 = 
ataxic gait, abnormal position; 5 = mild paraparesis; 6 = 
moderate paraparesis; 7 = severe paraplegia; 8 = tetraparesis; 9 = moribund; 10 = 
death (727, 757).  
 


 
58 
3.2 METHODS 
Study I 
In CSF, NfL levels were 
quantified using an in-house enzyme- linked 
immunosorbent assay (ELISA) method, 
whereas T-tau was measured using the 
Lumipulse technology (Fujirebio) (758). In plasma, NfL 
and T-tau were measured 
using the NF
-Light and 
Tau 2.0 kits on a Simoa HD - X Analyzer (Quanterix) 
according 
to the manufacturer's 
instructions. All analyses were performed at the 
Neurochemistry Laboratory in Mölndal, Sweden, in 2018.  
Study II 
Initially, we performed 
a broad 
pilot screening using several multiplex proteomic 
panels (Olink Bioscience, Uppsala) 
with proximity extension assays (PEA) 
technology. To further validate our preliminary multiplex proteomics results on IL8 
and related proteins, we performed 
a replication analysis with multiplex 
proteomics, along with a routine available chemiluminescence immunoassay for 
IL8,
 in a larger group of patients with immune- mediated neuropathies and 
controls. Multiplex 
proteomics were 
performed at the Olink laboratory, Uppsala, 
Sweden, and the chemiluminescence assay at the laboratory of the Department 
of Clinical Chemistry, Karolinska University Hospital, Stockholm, Sweden.  
The PEA technique utilizes
 multi-epitope binding for detection, providing a high 
specificity, 
while amplification ensures high sensitivity, allowing detection of low 
protein concentrations (759). CSF and plasma were analysed using all available 
Olink 
panels (thirteen in total) in 
2018. Each assay panel consisted of ninety-two 
protein targets (92 + 4 
controls), resulting in over 1100 protein measurements per 
sample and patient. Protein quantification was 
performed using PEA technology, 
which utilizes paired oligonucleotide antibody probes and quantitative 
polymerase chain reaction (qPCR) for detection and quantification (759). A 
mixture of antibody probes was incubated with each sample, i.e., two matched 
antibodies, labelled with DNA oligonucleotides bound to a target protein in 
solution. This brought 
the two antibodies into proximity, which allowed DNA 
oligonucleotides to hybridize and 
extend by a DNA polymerase. After 
hybridization and extension this newly created piece of DNA, which is unique for 
the specific antigen, was amplified by P CR. Following binding, hybridization, 
extension, and amplification , protein levels were qu antified using qPCR and 
expressed 
on a relative logarithmic scale with two as a base (log2) and with 
arbitrary units presented as normalized protein expression (NPX). Proteins with 


 
 59 
call rate less than 60%, i.e., proteins detected in fewer than 60% of the samples 
were excluded from further analyses.  
In the validation cohort ,
 l evels of IL8 in CSF and plasma were analysed at the 
Department of 
Clinical Chemistry, 
Karolinska University Hospital, using a 
chemiluminescence assay on an Immulite 
1000 instrument  from Siemens 
(Siemens Healthcare Diagnostics Products GmbH,  Marburg, 
Germany). The 
measurement range was 2-7500 ng/L. Imprecision
, expressed as coefficient of 
variation (CV), was
 7.2% at 109 ng/L, and 6.4% at 484 ng/L. 
Study III 
BTP levels in CSF and 
plasma were analysed at the Department of Clinical 
Chemistry, Karolinska University Hospital, Stockholm, Sweden. Quantification of 
BTP was done with N Latx BTP assay (Siemens Healthineers) using a Atellica NEPH 
630 immune nephelometer system (Siemens Healthineers, Erlangen, Germany). 
The method was calibrated 
using Siemens N Protein Standard UY. The 
measurement range was 0,25 -64 mg/L. Intra- and inter -day coefficient s of 
variation (CV) were less than 6%, according to internal validation.
 
Study IV 
Preparation and administration of imatinib  
The daily dose of imatinib was 
prepared by pulverising the imatinib tablets 
(Imatinib Acord Healthcare), which were then dissolved in sterile phosphate -
buffered saline 
(PBS), thoroughly vortexed 
and incubated in a water bath at 37°C  
for 6– 7 minutes. 
Insoluble components were separated by centrifuging the 
mixture at 4000 rpm for 8 minutes, after which the supernatant was removed and 
used immediately for oral gavage. Oral gavage was performed using a soft gavage 
tube attached to a 2 mL 
syringe. A total of 1.5 mL of either imatinib solution or PBS 
(control) was administered twice daily, delivering 
a dose of 150 mg/kg/animal/day 
for males and 100 mg/kg/animal/day for females. The tube was inserted into the 
rat's mouth and guided into the stomach, where the dose was administered. 
Treatment began either on day 2 p.i. for the prophylactic protocol, or on day 8 p.i. 
after the clinical onset 
of symptoms for the therapeutic protocol and continued 
until the end of the experiment.  
Assessment of BNB integrity 
To assess the 
extent of 
BNB disruption, we evaluated its permeability in vivo using 
the fluorescently labelled tracer dextran 
tetramethylrhodamine (dextran-TMR) 


 
60 
(383). The tracer was injected into the bloodstream via the tail vein 2 hours prior 
to euthanasia on day 21 p.i. under general anaesthesia with isoflurane (1000 mg/g, 
Piramal Critical Care). After 2 hours of tracer circulation, the animals were 
sacrificed, and the following tissues were collected post-mortem: cauda equina, 
spinal- and peripheral nerves
 including ischiatic nerve; inguinal lymph nodes, 
spleen, liver
 and heart muscle ; blood and cerebrospinal fluid (CSF). Dextran 
images, indicating tracer extravasatio n, 
were captured using a confocal 
microscope (Leica). Vascular permeability was quantified through fluorescent 
pixel area analysis, performed 
using ImageJ software (NIH). To further investigate 
BNB dysfunction, vessel isolates from the BNB were subjected to qPCR analyses. 
These vascular fragments (vessel/BNB isolates), which contain key components 
of the BNB— such as endothelial cells, pericytes, and fibroblasts— serve as an ideal 
functional unit to study 
the crosstalk between the immune system and the barrier. 
Vascular fragments were isolated from spinal- and peripheral nerve homogenates 
and extracted using 
an antibody against the endothelial cell marker CD31 (421). 
Vascular fragments from imatinib - and control (PBS) -treated rats undergoing 
EAN, respectively, were used 
for mRNA extraction, and expression analysis. 
Notably, all analysis mentioned above were performed at the peak of the disease 
(day 15 p.i.), and during the recovery phase (days 21-23 p.i.).  
Electrophysiological assessment 
To functionally assess the effects of 
imatinib in the EAN model, 
electrophysiological tests were performed one day before immunization, day 15 
p.i. (peak of the 
disease), and on day 21 p.i. (recovery phase).  Paired disposable 
monopolar needle electrodes were 
inserted into the right sciatic notch (hip, 
proximal) and into the popliteal fossa (knee, distal), with a ground electrode 
positioned between the stimulating electrode and recording electrodes, as 
described earlier (760, 761). Nerve conduction of the right sciatic nerve was 
assessed by measuring the amplitudes, durations and latencies of the evoked 
compound muscle action potentials (CMAPs). CMAP amplitudes were measured 
from the maximum negative to the maximum positive peak of the most 
representative biphasic wave . CMAP durations were determined by the time 
interval from the onset of the initial negative deflection to initial return to baseline. 
Distal latencies (DLs) were measured as the time interval from the stimulus to the 
first deflection from the baseline. The right sciatic nerve was stimulated with 
supramaximal pulses, and the resulting CMAP amplitudes (mV) and DLs (msec) 
were recorded. All rats were examined under isoflurane anaesthesia (1000mg/g, 


 
 61 
Piramal Critical Care). To minimize temperature variations, electro physiological 
tests were performed immediately after 
the anaesthesia took effect. The tests 
were conducted by a neurophysiology specialist in a blinded manner.  
 
3.3. STATISTICAL ANALYSIS 
Statistical analyses were performed using GraphPad Prism 8.0 (GraphPad 
Software, Boston, Massachusetts, USA), 
with the exception of Study II , where R-
4.1.3
 was also used. In all analyses, 
the threshold for statistical significance was set 
at 5%. 
Study I-IV 
The normality of data distribution 
was assessed with the Anderson-Darling test, 
D`Agostino & Pearson test, 
Shapiro-Wilk test, and Kolmogorov- Smirnov test. 
When the distribution was 
Gaussian (normal), parametric tests were used to 
compare groups ( t-test 
for two groups comparisons or one -way ANOVA 
corrected with Bonferroni´s test 
for multiple comparisons). When the distribution 
was non-Gaussian or 
the sample size was small, nonparametric tests were used 
(Mann-Whitney test for two groups or Kruskal-Wallis corrected with Dunn’s test 
for multiple comparisons). Likewise, 
depending on data distribution, correlation 
analysis was done by 
calculating Pearson or Spearman correlation coefficients. In 
addition, a logarithmic (log) transformation was used in Study I-II due to a non -
Gaussian distribution of data. 
Study I-III  
The 
diagnostic performance of NfL and 
T-tau in Study I, IL8 in Study II, and BTP in 
Study III was evaluated using receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve 
analysis, with the area under the curve (AUC), sensitivity, and specificity 
calculated. The prognostic performance of IL8 in Study II and BTP in Study III was 
assessed using Fisher's exact 
test and regression models. Additionally, multiple 
logistic regression models were used in Study III for multibiomarker analysis, 
further evaluating the diagnostic value of BTP in conjunction with other 
biomarkers. 
 
 


 
62 
Study I  
A simple logistic regression model was 
used to investigate the association 
between NfL levels and clinical outcome at different time points, and multiple 
regression models (logistic and linear) were used to investigate the association 
between NfL, age, and clinical outcome. In multiple regression models, CSF and 
plasma levels of NfL were evaluated both as continuous and binary variables. NfL 
as a binary variable was stratified as low or high based on median pre-treatment 
GBS, CIDP, PDN, or MMN 
group, respectively. NfL levels above the group median 
were considered as a cut- off for high NfL. A further subanalysis was performed 
where NfL was stratified 
as low or high based on the 90th percentile disease-free 
controls.  
Study II 
The multiplex 
proteomics data and 
associations between protein levels were 
analysed using a multivariable linear regression model, adjusted for sex and age at 
sampling, as previously described (762). Protein 
measurements were analysed 
with the default log base-two 
transformed protein levels and illustrated through 
volcano plots, with significance shown 
as the negative 10-log transformed NPX-
based beta coefficients. Associations were 
further corrected for multiple testing 
using a false discovery rate (FDR) approach, with a significance threshold of pFDR 
< 0.05. Considering sample 
handling issues, the model was also corrected with 
markers used to assess sample handling (CCL19 for CSF and CD40L for plasma) 
(762, 763).  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


 
 63 
3.4 ETHICAL CONSIDERATIONS 
Studies I-III are retrospective and the data were derived from the analysis of 
samples collected and stored  (CSF 
and EDTA plasma) in the biobank at the 
Department of Neurology, 
Karolinska University 
Hospital, Stockholm, Sweden, 
between 2003 and 2017. The material was 
collected during routine clinical 
investigations at the Department of Neurology, Karolinska University Hospital with 
the exception of healthy 
volunteers who were recruited by the Department of 
Neurology, University Hospital of Umeå, Sweden. Ethical approvals for studies I-III 
were obtained (2011–39-31 
M, 2014/1815-31/4, 2017/952-31/1 and 2018/832-32). All 
samples were anonymized and stored 
under ID code. Since these studies were 
retrospective and utilized biobank 
samples, patients were not exposed to any 
additional risks or interventions during the course of the studies. For study IV, 
which involved animals, we followed the 3R 
principle (replace, reduce, refine), a set 
of ethical guidelines designed to minimize animal use in research while ensuring 
scientific rigor. Replace refers to using alternative methods when possible, such 
as in vitro models, to 
avoid animal testing. We considered whether replacement, 
i.e
., using an animal- free method to achieve the same level of relevant results, 
including functional assessment of 
the treatment, was possible, but unfortunately 
it was not. Reduce aims to minimize the number of animals used in experiments 
by optimizing study design 
and statistical analysis. We used a study protocol to 
adhere to this principle 
and hence could reduce the number of experimental 
animals to the absolute minimum without compromising the quality of results. 
Refine focuses on enhancing experimental protocols to reduce animal suffering. 
We refined our methods 
to minimize suffering for each individual animal 
throughout the experiment, including the use of anaesthesia, cage enrichment, 
and euthanasia procedures designed to avoid distress or anxiety. All experiments 
detailed in study IV were approved 
by and performed to the standard and within 
the guidelines of the Swedish National Board for Laboratory Animals and the 
European Union Directive (2010/63/EU), approved by the North Stockholm Animal 
Ethics Committee (9328-2019, 12106-2021, 6137-2022). All rats 
 were assessed by 
a qualified veterinarian during the study. 
 




 
 65

chronic immune-mediated neuropathies (Study I) 
Pre-treatment levels of NfL in CSF were elevated in patients with GBS, CIDP, and 
PDN, but not 
MMN compared to healthy individuals ( Figure 1). Additionally, the 
levels of NfL 
were elevated in patients with GBS and CIDP compared to non -
inflammatory polyneuropathies, and 
CIDP compared to MMN . ROC analysis 
yielded AUC values, presented in the figure below (Supplement 2).

NfL in CSF and plasma for differentiating between GBS and CIDP in the acute 
phase of disease (Study I) 
Pre-treatment 
levels of NfL in both CSF and plasma were elevated in patients 
with GBS compared to CIDP (
Figure 1 and 2). ROC analysis yielded AUC value of 
0.70 (95%CI 0.5 – 0.8, p = 0.04) for NfL in CSF, while the result for NfL in plasma 
was not statistically significant. 
 
NfL in CSF and plasma as diagnostic biomarker for differentiating MMN from 
ALS (Study I) 
Pre-treatment levels of NfL in both CSF and plasma were significantly lower in 
patients with MMN compared to those with ALS (Figure 1 and 2). ROC analysis 
yielded AUC values of 
0.94 (95% CI 0.88 – 1, p < 0.001) for NfL in CSF, and 0.90 
(95% CI 0.81 –
 0.9, p = 0.005) for plasma ( Supplement 2). In CSF, a cut-off of 
2100 pg/mL 
distinguished the patients with MMN from those with ALS with 100% 
specificity and 86% 
sensitivity. In plasma, a cut-off of 46 pg/mL differentiated 
the patients with MMN from ALS with 88% specificity and 75% sensitivity.

BTP in CSF as a potentially more informative diagnostic bio marker than Q.Alb  
(Study III) 
Patients with GBS and CIDP with a normal to slightly elevated albumin quotient 
(Q.Alb), defined as 1 
unit above the age -dependent upper reference limit, had 
significantly elevated levels of BTP in CSF compared to healthy individuals. ROC  
analysis yielded an AUC 
values of 0.79 for BTP in CSF at the cut-off of 14 mg/L, 
based on the

increased, reaching its 
highest value when combining BTP in CSF with NFL in both 
CSF and plasma, and T-tau in plasma, yielding an AUC of 0.98, a positive predictive 
value 93%, and a negative predictive value 100%  (Figure 4
).

In CSF, the most consistent result in proteomic analysis was the upregulation of

, MMP3 and THBD, were upregulated, while four others 
showed a tendency to be 
upregulated, including CD28, MMP7, KIT, and CD274. 
Additionally, ITGAM was downregulated (Figure 1 D).  
Protein expression associated with 
CIDP compared to HC was assessed with 
multiple linear regression (Table 6).

4.3 PREDICTIVE BIOMARKERS  
BTP in CSF as a predictive biomarker for therapy response in CIDP (Study III) 
Treatment
-naïve patients 
with CIDP who did not respond to therapy had elevated 
levels of BTP in 
CSF. Non-responders were defined as those who did not improve 
or deteriorate by at least 1 point on INCAT scale, and treatment was changed or 
escalated. Elevated levels of BTP, with a cut-off of 17 mg/mL (based on the median 
in the CIDP 
group), were associated with a higher probability of non-response to 
first-line treatment ( Fisher's exact test p = 0.009) , with a specificity of 89%, 
sensitivity 73%, positive predictive 
value 89%, negative predictive value 72%, and 
likelihood ratio of 6.6. This association was further supported by multiple logistic 
regression (ORCSF BTP = 1.4, 95% CI 1.1 – 1.9, p = 0.03; ORage = 0.98, 95% CI 0.89 – 1.06, 
p = 0.55) and 
an AUC value of 0.90 (95% CI 0.75 –  1, p = 0.003) (Figure 6).

5 Discussion 
Neurologists often find it challenging to diagnose immune-mediated neuropathies 
due to the highly 
variable clinical presentations and the lack of reliable diagnostic 
tools. Hence, both underdiagnosis 
and overdiagnosis are common. Similarly, early 
prediction of therapy response 
is nearly impossible , and prognosis estimation 
remains unsatisfactory, except in GBS, 
where the EGRIS and EGOS models offer 
some guidance, although 
they have limitations. Moreover, objectively determining 
whether a patient with a chronic immune- mediated neuropathy, such as CIDP, 
remains 
treatment-dependent after months 
or years of therapy is currently not 
possible, often leading to overtreatment. Although treatable, there has been little 
progress in developing more efficacious 
treatment options since the 1990s, and 
several clinical trials have failed.  
Driven by clinical questions, this 
thesis seeks to provide some answers regarding 
potential diagnostic, prognostic, 
and predictive biomarkers, while also offering 
insights into pathophysiological mechanisms 
and novel therapies in both acute 
and chronic immune- mediated neuropathies. In this chapter, I will present our 
findings in the context of recent research in the field. 
 
Neuroaxonal damage 
markers, neurofilament 
light chain and total tau, as 
diagnostic and prognostic biomarkers 
In 2017, when Study I  was initiated, 
little was known about the role of 
neurofilaments 
(NFs) in immune-mediated 
neuropathies (764, 765). Since then, 
interest in NFs
 has grown rapidly, and our study aligns with others, confirming the 
presence
 of elevated levels 
of neurofilament light (NfL) isoform in both CSF and 
blood during the acute 
phase of GBS and CIDP  (497, 503, 539) , as well as its 
potential as 
a prognostic biomarker in GBS (497, 498). Among all five NF isoforms, 
NfL is the most abundant, stable, and soluble unit, making it the most reliable 
biomarker of axonal damage 
(542, 543). Several studies, similar to ours, have also 
demonstrated a strong association between the levels of NfL and prognosis in 
patients with GBS (497, 498, 541, 766). However, establishing a clear cut-off is 
challenging due to the 
clinical heterogeneity of immune-mediated neuropathies, 
differences in study 
populations, and the influence of confounding factors such 
as age, body weight, renal function, and non-neuronal sources of NfL (767-770). 
Moreover, the 
impact of 
race, ethnicity, lifestyle factors, and other comorbidities 


 
84 
on levels of NfL is still unknown (542). Nevertheless, it is notable that the Barcelona 
research group reported a similar cut -off for NfL in blood  of patients with GBS, 
predicting 
the ability to 
run after one year, as us in Study I. In their cohort the cut-
off was 34 pg/mL (498)
, while in ours it was  37 pg/mL. Equally interesting is a 
recent study from the 
Netherlands, which confirmed the prognostic value of NfL 
in GBS and demonstrated the dynamics of NfL during the first four weeks of the 
disease (541). The Duch research group found that the levels of 
 NfL continued to 
increase during the initial weeks, suggesting that early axonal involvement occurs 
in most, and possibly 
all, patients with GBS despite treatment.  
Regarding CIDP, 
in Study I, 
we found that NfL is not a reliable biomarker for 
assessing short- and long-term outcomes, which contrasts with the study by 
Godelaine et al.  (771). This discrepancy 
may be explained by differences in 
outcome definitions, the use of different clinical scales, and possibly the choice of 
immunotherapy. In our study, an unfavourable outcome was defined as a 3 or 
more point increase on the INCAT scale at follow-up, whereas in Godelaine et al.’s 
study, disease progression was 
defined as a decrease of at least four points on an 
80-point MRC sum-score scale, which is more sensitive in detecting changes in 
muscle strength than the INCAT. 
Our study was not des igned to investigate NfL as 
a marker for evaluating treatment response or monitoring disease course 
(response and/or monitoring biomarker) due to its retrospective design. However, 
more recent studies have 
demonstrated that levels of NfL in blood decrease and 
normalize in patients with CIDP who respond to first-line treatment (503, 504, 575, 
576). Although the 
number of patients in each study was relatively low, these 
studies are important because they suggest that NfL in blood could be used as a 
biomarker of disease activity and treatment response in patients with CIDP. 
The levels of NfL in 
patients with PDN and MMN have not been previously 
investigated. In Study I, we also assessed NfL in these patients  and found elevated 
levels in plasma in both PDN and 
MMN compared to healthy controls. In  the CSF, 
elevated levels were observed only 
in patients with PDN. These findings may be 
explained by NfL being released into the CSF from damaged nerve roots, which 
are not affected in patients with MMN as they are in GBS, CIDP, and PDN. This 
suggests proximal inflammation at the spinal nerve root level in PDN, an immune-
mediated neuropathy otherwise 
primarily characterized by distal demyelination. 
Additionally, we demonstrated that 
levels of NfL in CSF and blood can serve to 
differentiate MMN from ALS , a finding that was independently confirmed by 
another research group last year (594). This could be clinically useful in cases 


 
 85 
where the differential diagnosis between MMN and motor neuron disease with 
predominantly lower motor neuron involvement is difficult to establish.  
Although tau, a microtubule-associated 
protein, is a validated diagnostic marker 
in neurodegenerative disorders like Alzheimer's 
disease (772, 773), it has been  
largely overlooked and underexplored  in other neurological diseases
. The 
cytoskeleton of 
neurons and axons primarily consists of microtubules and NFs 
(545, 774-776). Tau, along 
with other microtubule-binding proteins, plays a key 
role in stabilizing microtubules, 
which, together with NFs, help maintain axonal 
stability (774, 777-779). However, in contrast to NfL, which is identical in the CNS 
and PNS (542, 545), tau in the CNS, with a molecular weight from 48 to 67 kDa, 
differs from tau in the PNS (780-782). The high-molecular-weight isoform, known 
as
 big tau
 (110 kDa), that is primarily expressed in the adult PNS, and to much lesser 
extent in some neurons of the CNS, has received little attention despite being 
discovered in the mid-1980s (780, 781, 783). The results of Study I support the 
use of tau in blood to differentiate immune-mediated neuropathies from non -
inflammatory neuropathies and 
from healthy individuals. Although 
our study could 
not distinguish between tau derived 
from the brain and peripheral nerves, the lack 
of correlation 
between the levels of tau in the blood and CSF, as well as with Q.Alb, 
alongside a positive correlation between the levels of tau and  NfL in blood, 
suggests that the 
elevated levels of tau in blood in our cohort of patients with GBS, 
CIDP, PDN, and 
MMN likely originate from a secondary axonal damage to peripheral 
nerves and is comprised most pr obably of big tau. Of interest, two separate 
research groups have reported elevated levels of big 
tau in the blood of patients 
with 
Charcot Marie Tooth disease, GBS, and 
CIDP at the 2023 Peripheral Nerve 
Society Annual Meeting in Copenhagen, Danmark (784).  
In conclusion, Study I demonstrates
 that elevated 
levels of tau in blood may 
support the diagnosis 
of both 
acute (GBS) and chronic immune -mediated 
neuropathies (CIDP, PDN, MMN). Furthermore, NfL in both blood and CSF can serve 
as a 
prognostic biomarker for GBS, but not for CIDP, and as a diagnostic biomarker 
for differentiating MMN from ALS. However, 
NfL cannot reliably differentiate GBS 
from CIDP, highlighting the need for a biomarker capable of distinguishing GBS 
from acute-onset CIDP in the early phase of the disease. 
 


 
86 
High-throughput proteomics analyses using PEA technology for the discovery 
of new biomarkers and the role of interleukin-8 (IL8) in immune-mediated 
neuropathies 
The use of proximity  extension assays 
(PEA) technology (Olink Bioscience, 
Uppsala) to detect new biomarkers has expanded across various fields of 
medicine, as it enables the measurements of  over 1100 proteins per sample with 
high specificity 
and sensitivity (759). However, there are some drawbacks, such 
as the potential for interference from background signals (i.e., non-specific signals 
that can 
affect the detection 
of the target antigen), the time-consuming nature of 
the 
process, high cost, 
the need for sophisticated bioinformatics analysis for 
accurate data interpretation, and the limitation of detection to only known 
antigens (22). The benefits of this ultrasensitive assay technology may be 
compromised by the method's 
vulnerability to inconsistent results if sample 
handling and collection are not performed with strict standardization, leading to 
variability caused by protein degradation or leakage from blood cells (762, 763). 
Preanalytical factors influencing the quality of samples are pre - and post-
centrifugation duration and temperature, type 
of collection tubes and additives, 
storage conditions (time and 
temperature), number of freeze -thaw cycles etc  
(763, 785).  
In Study II
, we used PEA 
technology to screen for potential diagnostic biomarkers 
for GBS 
and CIDP in 
two separate cohorts (Multiplex discovery and Multiplex 
replication). In the CSF of patients 
with GBS, we  identified fourteen candidate 
biomarkers that were upregulated compared 
to healthy individuals. Of these, nine  
proteins (IL2Ra, IL6, 
IL8, SELE, CR2, CD1C, THBD, ITGAM, and NRP1) passed rigorous 
statistical corrections, including adjustments
 for sample handling variability and 
correction for multiple 
testing using a false discovery rate. In plasma, CD28 was 
upregulated, and five additional 
proteins (MMP3, MMP7, NCAM1, THBD, and NRP1) 
were downregulated  in patients with GBS compared to healthy individuals ; 
however, only CD28 was replicated in the Multiplex replication cohort.  
In the CSF of 
patients with 
CIDP, we identified six candidate biomarkers that were 
upregulated compared 
to healthy individuals. Of these, only IL8 passed the same 
statistical correction analyses. In 
plasma, MMP3 and THBD were upregulated, while 
ITGAM was downregulated.  
Comparing GBS to 
CIDP, we 
found ten candidate biomarkers that were 
upregulated in 
the CSF. Of these, five (IL2Ra, IL6, IL8, ITGAM, and NRP1) passed the 


 
 87 
same statistical correction analyses. In plasma, MMP3 and KIT were 
downregulated, even after correcting for sample handling variability, but not for 
false discovery rate.  
The finding of IL2Ra upregulation in GBS
 in Study II , compared to both healthy 
individuals and patients 
with CIDP, is consistent with previous reports of elevated 
levels of soluble IL2Ra in 
the blood of patients with GBS, but not those with CIDP 
(556, 784, 786, 787). Soluble IL2Ra is released by activated T cells, hence, elevated 
levels indicate T
-cell activation (788). However, the exact source of soluble IL2Ra 
in GBS remains unclear, particularly 
whether it originates from effector or 
regulatory T cells  (Treg). Importantly, T reg cells exhibit the highest constitutive 
expression of IL2Ra (789)
, suggesting that activated T lymphocytes may function 
as suppressor cells during 
recovery in GBS, an acute monophasic disease, in 
contrast to CIDP.  
In contrast, there are no  previous
 reports on the levels of  ITGAM (CD11b) and 
neuropilin-1 
(NRP1) in patients with immune -mediated neuropathies. ITGAM 
(CD11b or αM-integrin) is a key component of the CD11b/CD18 receptor, the 
principal transmembrane integrin expressed on the surface of neutrophiles (790-
792). It can bind 
to over 40 ligands, reflecting its pleiotropic effects on various 
cellular processes, including cell adhesion, transmigration, and phagocytosis (791, 
792). In
 both Multiplex 
cohorts in Study II, we observed an upregulation of ITGAM 
in the CSF 
of patients with GBS compared to both healthy individuals and those 
with CIDP. In fact, the 
interaction between ITGAM (CD11b) and its counter-ligand, 
intercellular adhesion 
molecule-1 (ICAM1)  on endothelial 
cells has been 
demonstrated in an in vitro BNB 
model of GBS (417). This underscores the crucial 
role of the CD11b/CD18— ICAM1 
pathway in the pathogenesis of GBS and leukocyte 
trafficking. The pathogenetic relevance 
of CD11b is also supported by findings of 
clusters of infiltrating CD11b  positive leukocytes (predominantly monocytes/  
macrophages) in the 
endoneurium, along 
with disrupted Schwann cells membrane 
organization in sural nerve biopsies from patients with GBS (419). If the 
upregulation of ITGAM is 
confirmed in larger cohorts and with other laboratory 
techniques, it would further support 
the involvement of neutrophiles, IL8 and 
related pathways in the pathogenesis of GBS.  
NRP1 is a transmembrane glycoprotein expressed in various cell 
types, such as 
neuronal, 
endothelial, and immune cell
s. It plays important roles in neuronal and 
cardiovascular development, as well as in immune system function  (793, 794). 


 
88 
NRP1 functions as a co -receptor for a range of ligands, including semaphorins, 
growth factors, and extracellular 
microRNA–AGO2 complexes  (795). In the 
immune system, NRP1 
is expressed on plasmacytoid dendritic cells and other 
antigen-presenting cells, particularly tissue-resident macrophages, as well as on 
T cell subsets, especially Treg cells (794, 796). Although NRP1 has diverse functions 
within the immune system, 
it is most frequently associated with immune 
suppression and serves as a marker for thymically derived  T reg (794, 795). 
Moreover, in the animal model of multiple sclerosis ( experimental autoimmune 
encephalomyelitis, EAE), deletion of NRP1 
in CD4+ T cells led to an increase  in 
inflammatory Th17 cells, a reduction 
in Treg cells, and a subsequent worsening of 
disease severity (797). Hence, the upregulation of NRP1 in patients with GBS — but 
not in those with CIDP
 — may suggest that it originates from Treg cells, which act 
as suppressor cells during recovery in the monophasic course of GBS, in contrast 
to the chronic nature of CIDP.  
Nevertheless, the most consistent finding  in the Multiplex cohorts was the 
upregulation of IL
8 in the CSF of patients with GBS and CIDP. Therefore, we 
extended Study II by analysing IL8 in patients with PDN and  MMN, and validated 
the finding of IL8 upregulation in GBS 
and CIDP from the Multiplex cohorts in a 
third cohort , using a secondary
 method, the routinely  available 
chemiluminescence 
assay. IL8 is 
a pro-inflammatory cytokine primarily produced 
by blood monocytes and 
tissue macrophages. Its main function is to recruit mainly 
neutrophils, but also T cells and dendritic cells, to the site of inflammation (798-
800). Significantly higher levels 
of IL8 in the CSF of patients with GBS compared 
to those with CIDP 
suggest that  intrathecal IL8 and/or IL8-secreeting cells, such 
as monocytes, along 
with the activation of the innate immune system may play a 
more crucial role in the pathogenesis of GBS than CIDP. Moreover, elevated levels 
of IL8 in the CSF in GBS and CIDP may point toward another important biological 
function of IL8, namely its role in disrupting endothelial tight junctions and 
promoting vascular permeability (412). However, further investigation into the IL8 
network is warranted.  
In line  with a recently published study suggesting IL8 in CSF as a diagnostic 
biomarker to differentiate GBS from CIDP (458), we also confirmed an IL8 
concentration of 73 ng/L in 
CSF as a cut -off to differentiate patients with GBS 
from those with CIDP
, with a specificity of 94% and sensitivity 90%. Furthermore, 
we proposed a cut-off of 51 ng/L to differentiate healthy individuals from patients 
with GBS, CIDP, and 
PDN (specificity 91%), respectively, as well as to differentiate 


 
 89 
CIDP and PDN from patients with non -inflammatory neuropathies (specificity 
94%).  Additionally, we 
provided new insights into the prognostic significance of 
IL8, particularly during the acute phase of GBS. 
Of note, only one other published 
study has utilized PEA technology in patients 
with immune -mediated neuropathies. A
 Dutch research group investigated 
biomarkers in blood related 
to clinical disease activity in CIDP and identified five 
potential biomarkers (IRAK4, SUGT1, DCTN1, 
NT5C3A, and GLRX ) (21). These results 
require further validated.  
In 
conclusion, Study II validated 
IL8 in 
CSF as a diagnostic biomarker, specifically 
for patients with GBS 
and CIDP, with potential to aid in distinguishing between the 
two conditions. This study also 
provides new insights into IL8’s prognostic value 
in GBS, and highlight s the 
role of IL8 in the pathogenesis of acute and chronic 
immune-mediated neuropathies. Additionally, Study II identified novel candidate 
biomarkers that warrant further investigation.  
 
Beta-trace protein 
as a more informative biomarker of blood -nerve barrier 
damage than albumin 
Albumino-cytological 
dissociation (ACD), while a hallmark of GBS and CIDP, does 
not provide optimal sensitivity and specificity for these disorders  and can also 
occur in other peripheral and 
central nervous system conditions (41, 57, 467). The 
second most abundant 
protein in CSF after albumin is beta- trace protein (BTP) 
(801, 802). It is 
mainly produced intrathecally and secreted by fibroblasts in the 
pachymeninx (the dura mater surrounding the brain and spinal cord), 
leptomeninges (the arachnoid and pia mater), epithelial cells of the choroid plexus, 
and oligodendrocytes within the brain and spinal cord (802, 803). BTP has also 
been identified in lower concentrations in other human fluids, including blood, and 
it is hypothesized that the BTP in blood originates from the CSF (804, 805). BTP 
has a smaller molecular 
size (26 –29 kilodaltons) compared to albumin (66 
kilodaltons) (806), which may 
allow it to pass through the blood- cerebrospinal 
fluid (BCSF) barrier even in the case 
of minor damage. Due to the concentration 
gradient of BTP from CSF to blood, damage to the BCSF is expected to result in 
elevated levels of BTP in the blood. 
Based on this
 hypothesis, we 
conducted Study III to investigate whether BTP in 
plasma could serve as a 
surrogate marker for BCSF barrier damage in GBS and 


 
90 
CIDP. Unexpectedly, we found that levels of BTP in blood were elevated only in 
patients with CIDP and not 
those with GBS. Moreover, no correlation was observed 
between levels of BTP 
in CSF and plasma, despite a BCSF damage as indicated by 
the elevated Q.
Alb. These findings suggest that elevated levels of BTP in CIDP may 
not be 
due to damage to the BCSF barrier, but rather could reflect other, yet 
unidentified, pathophysiological mechanisms. These mechanisms might involve a 
PNS origin of BTP in 
plasma in patients with demyelinating neuropathies, or a non-
neural source. Elevated BTP levels in blood in immune-mediated polyneuropathies 
could potentially 
arise from myelin 
damage in the PNS, as indicated by the 
detection of BTP in the PNS through single radial immunodiffusion analysis of 
femoral nerves obtained at autopsy from patients without prior neurological 
disease (807). This could explain the 
 higher plasma BTP levels observed in CIDP 
compared to GBS, as demyelination in CIDP is typically more prolonged and 
affects a larger portion of the peripheral nerves by the time of diagnosis, in 
contrast to GBS. 
Despite our initial hypothesis regarding the 
utility of BTP in blood as a surrogate 
marker for 
BCSF barrier damage, Study III demonstrated that the elevated levels 
of BTP in 
CSF – rather than in blood – could serve as a diagnostic biomarker for 
CIDP and GBS, independent 
of ACD and Q.Alb. However, the levels of BTP in the 
CSF did not distinguish between CIDP and GBS. We speculate that the elevated 
levels of BTP in 
the CSF originate from increased production and/or release of BTP 
by the spinal leptomeninges in
 response to inflammation at the nerve root level.  
Our findings suggest that BTP 
in CSF could serve as a potentially more informative 
diagnostic biomarker than albumin in patients with GBS and CIDP, particularly 
those with normal or mildly elevated Q.Alb. Additionally, we identified a cut-off 
 of 
17 mg/L in CSF as 
predictive of therapy response in treatment-naïve patients with 
CIDP. These findings are 
preliminary and exploratory, requiring further validation 
and confirmation. As such, 
they should be interpreted with caution and not 
regarded as definitive evidence at this point.  
In conclusion, Study III reports a 
novel finding of elevated levels of BTP in CSF of 
patients with 
both CIDP and 
GBS, and also in plasma in CIDP, which could 
potentially aid in the diagnosis of these diseases. 
 
 


 
 91 
First step towards a clinical trial of imatinib in Guillain-Barré syndrome  
Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS) is an acute, life -threatening, but treatable 
immune-mediated disease 
of the peripheral nerves and nerve roots. There are 
only two available treatment options for GBS, namely intravenous 
immunoglobulins 
(IVIg) or plasma exchange (PE),  both of which are equally 
effective but with several 
considerable drawbacks, including high cost, limited 
availability, and a lack of 
specificity, as both treatments target immune 
mechanisms without addressing the underlying pathophysiological processes of 
the disease (25, 41). 
Numerous attempts to find new therapies with a higher 
efficacy than the 
currently available 
treatments for i mmune-mediated 
neuropathies have failed, such as the 
recent trial of oral fingolimod in CIDP and 
eculizumab in GBS (635, 808)
. New treatment options for GBS are necessary as 
the prognosis in a 
large group of patients with GBS is still far from satisfactory  
despite treatment with IVIg or 
PE (8, 24, 211). As discussed before, the dysfunction 
of the blood- nerve 
barrier (BNB) and increased permeability is one of the key 
events in GBS pathogenesis, 
allowing pro-inflammatory molecules and immune 
cells to 
migrate from the blood stream across the endoneurium into the nerve 
compartment, where they can lead to myelin destruction and/or axonal damage 
(1, 10, 390, 809). Hence, it 
could be argued that an effective strategy to reduce 
neuroinflammation in the peripheral nerve compartment is to restore the integrity 
of the leaky blood–nerve and blood–CSF barriers early in the course of disease.  
Previous studies have identified a platelet
-derived growth factor receptor-alpha 
(PDGFRα),	a tyrosine kinase 
receptor signalling pathway responsible for regulat ing 
the 
function of 
the blood
-brain barrier (BBB) (810-812). PDGFRα is expressed on 
astrocyte
 endfeet, and 
fibroblast-like cells at the BBB, as well as on fibroblasts at 
the BNB (810, 813, 
814). When activated by its ligand, the platelet-derived growth 
factor CC 
(PDGF-CC), PDGFR
α led to BBB impairment in animal  models of 
ischemic stroke (815). On the other hand, blocking PDGFRα using imatinib, a smal l 
tyrosine kinase 
inhibitor, reduced disruption 
of BBB in animal models of ischemic 
stroke, haemorrhagic brain injury, multiple sclerosis, traumatic brain injury, and 
spinal cord injury (810, 
812, 816-819). In humans, a phase II study has demonstrated 
that imatinib is
 safe, well-tolerated and might help reduce neurological disability 
in patients  who received intravenous 
thrombolysis following ischemic stroke  
(820).  


 
92 
Since a common injury response mechanism involving activation of PDGFRα in the 
neurovascular unit has been  implicated 
in endothelial barrier dysfunction, we 
investigated 
in Study IV if imatinib ameliorates experimental autoimmune neuritis 
(EAN), an 
animal model of GBS, by preserving function and integrity of the BNB.  
We found that the 
disease course in EAN was significantly ameliorated by imatinib 
treatment compared to the control group . This was supported by a histological 
assessment of the BNB integrity, expression 
analysis with  qPCR of v ascular 
fragments (vessel/BNB isolates) containing 
main components of the BNB  
(endothelial cells, pericytes and fibroblasts) , 
and electrophysiological tests. 
Histopathological analysis of peripheral  nerves demonstrated a significant 
decrease in extravasation of 
fluorescently labelled dextran-TMR tracer into the 
perivascular area (due to a 
“leaky” BNB) in rats treated with imatinib. This indicates 
a better-preserved BNB in rats treated with imatinib compared to a dysregulated 
BNB in control rats. Furthermore, i
matinib-mediated changes detected on 
biochemically isolated 
BNB showed downregulation of the transcripts important 
for 
endothelial cell activation (Il1a, Tnf, and Ccl2), leukocyte recruitment (Sele, Selp, 
Icam1, Itgals, Cd14, and Cxcl10 ), and angiogenesis (Vegfa, Thbs1, N os2, and Ch25h), 
which 
all are important biological events 
implicated in compromising the integrity 
of the BNB. Finally, functional assessment by means of electrophysiology showed 
preserved compound muscle action potentials 
(CMAPs) during the course of EAN 
in rats treated with imatinib compared to controls, indicating that treatment with 
imatinib protects a peripheral nerve function. 
In conclusion, Study IV demonstrated that 
treatment with imatinib ameliorates 
EAN 
and that targeting PDGFR α signalling in peripheral immune- mediated 
neuropathies could represent a novel, 
more specific 
treatment for GBS. This study 
also lays the groundwork for a clinical trial to evaluate the efficacy and safety of 
imatinib in patients with GBS. 
 
Limitations 
Shared limitations of Studies 
I-III include their retrospective design , lack of 
longitudinal follow-up 
samples, heterogeneity of patients with non-inflammatory 
polyneuropathies, absence of clinical 
assessments with other disability scales 
(such as I-
 RODS) and impairment scales (such as the MRC sum score and grip 
strength measurements, particularly in patients with MMN), a low number of 
patients with 
PDN and MMN, and a lack of data on antibodies against 


 
 93 
nodal/paranodal epitopes. Specific limitations of Study I  include the limited 
number of paired samples 
before and after treatment, and the inability to 
distinguish brain-derived tau from tau originating in peripheral nerves; for Study 
II
, a low 
number of 
patients and controls in the multiplex cohorts, as well as a low 
number of patients with SIDP  and acute- onset CIDP; for Study III, a lack of 
validation cohort and the overrepresentation 
of non-responders in CIDP cohort. 
EAN 
used in Study IV is a 
valuable model for studying immune-mediated nerve 
injury and testing immunotherapies. 
However, limitations include its translational 
relevance by biological, pathological, 
and immunological differences between 
rodents and humans. For example, T cell subsets, cytokine profiles, and responses 
to antigens differ between 
humans and rodents, which can affect the 
generalizability of findings. It is characterised by less severe paralysis/lower 
degree of motor and autonomic dysfunction compared to GBS.  Moreover, EAN 
models are commonly conducted 
in inbred strains, which limits the genetic 
variability representative of the human population . Furthermore, EAN 
predominantly mimics AIDP, in contrast to axonal forms , i.e., anti-GM1 are not 
addressed in this model. 
Finally, EAN is typically a short, monophasic disease with 
spontaneous recovery, which does not fully replicate the variability of GBS. 




 
 95 
6 Conclusions 
The main conclusions of this thesis can be categorized into diagnostic, prognostic, 
and 
predictive biomarkers for 
immune-mediated neuropathies, with a novel 
therapeutic approach for GBS also being proposed
.  
 
DIAGNOSTIC BIOMARKERS 
• T-tau in blood is a novel diagnostic biomarker for GBS, CIDP, MMN, and PDN. 
• NfL in CSF and blood is a biomarker for differentiating MMN from ALS. 
• IL8 in CSF is a diagnostic biomarker mainly for GBS, but also CIDP, and PDN. 
• BTP 
in CSF and plasma is a novel diagnostic biomarker mainly for CIDP. 
• BTP in CSF may 
serve as 
a more informative diagnostic biomarker than albumin 
in patients with GBS and CIDP with normal or mildly elevated Q.Alb. 
• Novel candidate biomarkers that should 
be investigated further in CSF include 
IL2Ra, IL6, SELE, CR2, CD1C, 
THBD, ITGAM, and NRP1, and in plasma, CD28,  
NCAM1, THBD, NRP1, MMP3, MMP7, and KIT.  
 
PROGNOSTIC BIOMARKERS 
• NfL in CSF and blood is a prognostic biomarker for GBS but not CIDP.  
• IL8 
in CSF may be  a 
prognostic biomarker for both short- and long term 
outcomes in GBS.
 
 
PREDICTIVE BIOMARKERS 
• BTP in CSF may serve as a 
predictive biomarker for treatment response in CIDP.  
 
NOVEL TREATMENT FOR GUILLAIN-BARRÉ SYNDROME  
• Treatment with i
matinib, a 
small tyrosine kinase inhibitor , presents a novel 
theraputic approach for Guillain -Barré syndrome (GBS), targeting the entry 
site of immune cells into the PNS, 
i.e., the blood-nerve barrier. 
• We demonstrated the efficacy of imatinibin 
treating EAN and thereby provided 
strong evidence for its potential clinical application in patients with GBS.




 
 97 
7 Points of perspective 
The field of immune-mediated neuropathies is evolving at a faster pace than ever 
before! New diagnostic tools, such as nerve imaging with ultrasound and magnetic 
resonance imaging (MRI), have gained increased understanding and application. 
Biomarkers in CSF and blood 
are steadily making their way into clinical practice, 
offering valuable insights in disease mechanisms and enabling earlier diagnosis, 
disease monitoring, 
treatment response, and prognosis assessment. Globally, NfL 
is most likely the 
first biomarker to be integrated into everyday clinical practice. 
Novel entities, such as autoimmune 
nodopathy, are being discovered, broadening 
our understanding of the disease spectrum. New treatments are actively being 
tested and several clinical trials are underway, offering hope for more effective 
and personalized therapeutic approaches. Significant advancements are being 
made, and 
even when the relevance of a particular finding or result is not 
immediately clear or of practical relevance, it still represents a step toward greater 
knowledge and a deeper understanding of immune-mediated neuropathies.  
Our studies on biomarkers 
have hopefully made a meaningful contribution to the 
field of 
immune-mediated neuropathies, although there is still a long road of 
rigorous validation and standardisation procedures, before 
any of biomarkers 
discovered in our studies 
can become a part of clinical practice. Specifically, larger 
studies, ideally involving diverse 
and heterogeneous populations — encompassing 
various races, ethnicities, ages, sexes, 
body weights, comorbidities, and life factors 
— should be conducted to determine 
the optimal cut-off values for NfL and IL8 as 
prognostic markers
 in patients with GBS, and BTP  as a predictive biomarker for 
therapy response in CIDP. The same should be done if we want to use NfL as a 
response and monitoring marker in patients with CIDP.  Our novel findings of 
diagnostic use of tau 
and BTP should be investigated in prospective studies and 
larger cohorts. Tau in blood, in particular, should be examined using anti- tau 
antibodies or equivalent methods that 
selectively bind to the peripherally 
expressed 'big tau' isoform, distinguishing it from brain-derived tau. The diagnostic 
value of tau in 
blood and NfL in both blood and CSF in differentiating MMN from 
ALS is also important and should be validated in other cohorts before being 
implemented into clinical routine. Since in vitro studies suggest that BNB damage 
is more pronounced in 
typical CIDP than in CIDP variants, it would be interesting 
to investigate whether this is reflected on the levels of BTP. It is also  crucial to 
explore in other cohorts 
whether our preliminary finding of BTP as a more sensitive 
biomarker than Q.Alb for 
BNB damage holds true, particularly in GBS and CIDP 


 
98 
cases with normal levels of albumin in CSF, and finally whether BTP can predict 
therapy response in patients with CIDP.  
Our multiplex proteomics study (Olink) 
has highlighted several interesting markers 
in both GBS and CIDP, but these need further investigation and validation. IL8, in 
particular, seems to be a promising candidate biomarker for differentiating 
between GBS and CIDP in the acute phase of disease, as elevated levels of IL8 in 
GBS has been reported 
even by others. However, the key question — whether the 
levels of IL8 in 
CSF could be used to differentiate between GBS and acute-onset 
CIDP —
 needs to be explored in larger cohorts, as this has significant implications 
for treatment decisions. Further exploration 
of the IL8 pathway, especially in GBS 
patients, could yield valuable insights into the pathogenesis of these disorders. 
Furthermore, IL8 may also serve as 
a potential therapeutic target in immune-
mediated neuropathies, as IL8-inhibitors have 
already been developed and are 
being tested in patients with cancer. If our results are confirmed by other research 
groups, exploring IL8-inhibitors in patients with GBS and CIDP could offer a novel 
therapeutic approach. 
The increasing availability of biomedical 
data from biobanks, electronic health 
records, patient reported outcome measures (PROMs),  and wearable biosensors, 
combined with decreasing costs and the growing accessibility of various analytic 
technologies, calls for the integration of biological, clinical, mathematical, and 
technological expertise  (821, 822). Standard analytical tools are no longer 
adequate for analysing multimodal 
data (datasets that include various modalities 
such as multiple biomarkers, 
images, clinical information, genetic factors etc) 
(823-825). However, multimodal 
biomedical artificial intelligence (AI)-based 
models can integrate complex
 datasets from multiple sources, enabling more 
precise analysis and delivering significant value in discovery of biological 
pathways, disease management
, treatment and prevention  (824, 826, 827). 
Moreover, given the complexity 
of immune -mediated neuropathies and the 
immune system, it 
is unlikely that a single biomarker, or even proteomics, will be 
sufficient to answer all 
of our questions. Multiomic technologies - integrating 
various omics 
data such as proteomics, genomics, epigenomics, transcriptomics, 
metabolomics, image-omics, 
and pathomics -  combined with AI, are likely to 
shape the future
 of biomedical research.  
Lastly, our 
findings on imatinib’s effect in 
the EAN model are encouraging and have 
prompted us to consider 
initiating a clinical trial in patients with GBS. Given that 


 
 99 
GBS is a relatively rare disease and patient recruitment is often slow at individual 
centres, collaborative efforts at 
both national and international levels will be 
essential.
